Human-Centered AI for Composition: A Literature Review

Compose → Annotate → Compile → Edit — surveying 464 works for the design of an open-source AI music studio

Henry Tan · Maestro · 7 September 2026

The dominant public paradigm in music AI is text-to-full-song generation: a prompt in, an opaque master out. This review surveys the space from the opposite premise — that the composer authors the material, annotates it in notation, ink, voice or example, and asks a machine to compile a fuller realization under those annotations, then edits and recompiles. Drawing on 464 annotated entries (399 unique records, 1957–2026) across historical, symbolic-generation, audio-generation, HCI, multimodal-annotation, infrastructure, evaluation and legal literatures, it organizes the field by the stages of that loop rather than by technique. Five findings structure the argument: infilling under hard constraints, not generation from scratch, is the primitive that implements the loop; interface steerability moves control and ownership at least as much as model capability does; every input modality that works routes through an editable symbolic representation; the annotation layer — treating a composer’s marks as a compilable instruction set — is the field’s genuine white space; and a symbolic-first, human-authored system sits on materially better legal and ethical ground than the prompt-to-audio alternative. Twelve gaps are consolidated into a sequenced research agenda.

Contents

  • 2. Historical lineages (F1–F3)
  • 3. Theories of human–AI co-creation (F4–F5)

1. Introduction

1.1 The paradigm problem

By September 2026 the public face of music AI is a text box. A user types a sentence, waits, and receives a finished stereo master with vocals. Suno’s models advanced from v3 to v5.5 in roughly two years, Udio reached commercial scale before settling with two major labels, and Google’s Lyria family moved from research demo to a consumer product line and a real-time API (Suno Inc. 2026; Universal Music Group and Udio 2025; Google DeepMind 2025b). Open weights followed the same shape: ACE-Step, YuE, DiffRhythm, SongGen and HeartMuLa all take a text prompt and lyrics and return a song (Gong et al. 2026; Yuan et al. 2025; Ning et al. 2025; Z. Liu et al. 2025; HeartMuLa Team 2026).

For a composer this is the wrong shape. A prompt is a very low-bandwidth channel for musical intent, the output arrives as an opaque mixdown with no handle for correction, and the human contribution collapses to prompt authorship and curation. The evidence that professionals experience it this way is now direct. Seventeen producers given MusicGen and Demucs reported “creative misalignment” and pain aligning generated material to their own tempo, key and beat grids, and asked for control and editability above all (Ronchini et al. 2025). Six professional composers interviewed for ISMIR 2023 asked for MusicXML and MIDI interoperability, open and explainable models, and preservation of control (Newman et al. 2023). An eighteen-month study embedded with six professional acts at Sony CSL found that practitioners want to pull material rather than be pushed it, to prime models on their own audio, and to stay inside the tools they already use (Deruty et al. 2022). A four-week ecological study of a variation tool found that transformations of the user’s own authored material preserved creative ownership while automatic completion was rejected outright (Krol et al. 2025).

The market itself has been drifting toward the composer’s shape without adopting its premise. Suno added Covers, Personas and Replace Section, then a DAW-like Studio in September 2025, then MIDI import and editing in August 2026; Udio added inpainting; Google’s Music AI Sandbox added region editing and a multitrack view; Stable Audio 2.5 and ElevenLabs Music advertise inpainting; ACE-Step ships repainting, lyric editing and vocal-to-accompaniment (Suno Inc. 2026; Universal Music Group and Udio 2025; Google DeepMind 2025b; Novack et al. 2025; ElevenLabs 2025; Gong et al. 2026). Every one of these is an admission that one-shot generation is insufficient, retrofitted onto an architecture that was not designed for iteration.

This review surveys the space from the opposite starting point. It asks what the literature and the existing systems offer to a studio in which the composer authors the material, marks it up, and asks a machine to compile a fuller realization under those marks — then edits, re-annotates, and recompiles. The loop is

compose → annotate → compile → edit → annotate → compile → …

with editing understood broadly: opening the notation and changing it by hand, scribbling on it, humming a line, attaching an example recording, a picture, a video or a sentence of prose. The word compile is deliberate. It implies a source that a human owns, intermediate representations that can be inspected, a deterministic and repeatable build, and a build log that explains what the machine did with each instruction.

1.2 Scope, method and corpus

The corpus is 464 annotated entries gathered in September 2026 across eight parallel research clusters, yielding 399 unique bibliographic records. Coverage runs from Hiller and Isaacson’s Illiac Suite in 1957 to preprints of mid-2026, with emphasis on 2023–2026. The clusters were seeded by a practising composer-engineer’s own reading list — Music Transformer and Anna Huang’s lineage, the CMU cluster around Chris Donahue’s Generative Creativity Lab, Amuse, ExpressEdit, music21 and MIT’s computational music theory teaching, Suno and ACE-Step, and a January 2026 CMU result on human versus AI-assisted creativity — and then expanded systematically: symbolic generation and editing; audio generation and controllable audio; HCI and creativity-support research on co-creation; multimodal input, annotation and the music-as-code lineage; infrastructure, datasets, computational music theory and education; and the historical, commercial, evaluative and legal context.

Each entry was recorded with a verification level. Roughly two-thirds were verified against a primary source fetched during the survey — an arXiv abstract page, an ACM Digital Library record, a project page, a repository README, a court docket or an official release note. Entries marked partial were confirmed only from secondary pages or search snippets, and a small number marked unverified rest on recall alone. Claims drawn from partial and unverified entries are hedged in the prose that follows, and precise figures are omitted where the source could not be re-read. Three limitations follow from the method. Search budgets were exhausted in several clusters, so older canonical works (Koenig’s Project 1, Vercoe’s Synthetic Performer, GenJam) are recorded at lower confidence than 2025–2026 preprints. Non-English literature, the commercial Japanese and Chinese ecosystems beyond the systems named here, and the substantial NIME and ICMC hardware-instrument literature are under-represented. And the fast-moving product and legal material is a snapshot: it is accurate as of early September 2026 and will decay quickly.

1.3 The organizing taxonomy

Existing surveys of music AI are organized by technique, domain or task (Ji et al. 2023; Le et al. 2025; Ma et al. 2024; Kader and Karmaker 2025). Those axes are the right ones for a modeller and the wrong ones for a builder, because they do not say where a given contribution sits in a composer’s working day. The companion taxonomy therefore uses the loop itself as its trunk — Compose (S1), Annotate (S2), Compile (S3), Edit (S4), with Play (S5) as the real-time branch — surrounded by Foundations (F) that precede it, a Substrate (B) of representations, engines, toolkits and data beneath it, Context (C) of products, law and economics around it, and Evidence and Evaluation (E) as the meta-layer that decides whether any of it works. A second, orthogonal view codes each work on ten dimensions: output domain, task framing, control granularity, input modalities, initiative and timing, communication direction, editability of the intermediate representation, integration surface, evidence level, and openness and provenance.

Three pieces of vocabulary are borrowed rather than invented. Rowe’s 1993 distinctions — score-driven versus performance-driven input, transformative versus generative versus sequenced response, and the instrument versus player paradigm — describe the studio precisely as a score-driven, transformative system in the instrument paradigm (Rowe 1993). Kantosalo and Toivonen’s alternating versus task-divided co-creativity names the turn structure (Kantosalo and Toivonen 2016). Rezwana and Maher’s COFI supplies the two communication directions and, in coding 92 co-creative systems, the finding that the AI-to-human direction is almost entirely undesigned (Rezwana and Maher 2023).

Sections 2 through 12 follow the taxonomy’s order. Section 13 consolidates the gaps into a research agenda.

1.4 Five theses this review establishes

First, infilling under hard constraints — not generation from scratch — is the primitive that implements the loop. Coconet framed this in 2017 in almost the founder’s words, observing that composers write “in a nonlinear fashion, scribbling motifs here and there, often revisiting choices previously made,” and modelling it as blocked-Gibbs completion of a partially masked score (Huang et al. 2017). Nine years of work has made it mature: bar-by-track masking in MMM and MIDI-GPT, arbitrary track-measure selection with roughly ten fine-grained controls in Composer’s Assistant, anticipation tokens that let any subset of notes be pinned as hard constraints in the Anticipatory Music Transformer, and arbitrary piano-roll masks in Polyffusion and GETMusic (Ens and Pasquier 2020; Pasquier et al. 2025; Malandro 2024; Thickstun et al. 2024; Min et al. 2023; Lv et al. 2023). The strongest deployment evidence in the entire corpus is Hookpad Aria’s: about 74,000 of 318,000 span-level suggestions accepted by roughly 3,000 users inside a lead-sheet editor — about a 23% acceptance rate, reported in full under E3 (Donahue et al. 2024).

Second, interface steerability moves the outcomes that matter to composers at least as much as model capability does. The only two studies that varied model and interface independently found that steering tools produced the larger gains in control, ownership and self-efficacy, while the more expressive model produced better listener-judged musicality — the two are complementary, not substitutes (Louie et al. 2020, 2022). The corollary from Cococo is a design rule: generative power should be “partitioned into smaller, semantically meaningful tools” rather than exposed as one button. MMM-C’s single-slider design confirms the negative case, scoring acceptable usability while its users’ desire for more control averaged about 9.5 out of 10 (Tchemeube et al. 2023).

Third, every input modality that works routes through an editable symbolic representation. Humming becomes MIDI or a lead sheet, drawing becomes notes, an image becomes chords, and in each case the symbolic layer is what gives the human a correction handle (Bittner et al. 2022; Donahue et al. 2022; Liang and Wang 2024; Yewon Kim et al. 2025). Systems that map a loose gesture straight to audio are impressive and uncorrectable (Donahue et al. 2023; Tian et al. 2025). Amuse is the cleanest demonstration of the alternative: a multimodal model proposes keywords the user can edit, and those keywords drive chord candidates filtered by rejection sampling against a chord prior trained on real music, with significant gains in inspiration support and controllability over baselines (Yewon Kim et al. 2025).

Fourth, the annotation layer is the studio’s genuine white space. The pieces exist separately. Score annotation already has standardised, machine-readable, musical-time-anchored formats in MEI, Dezrann’s labels, Humdrum and RomanText (Pugin et al. 2014; Giraud et al. 2018; Huron 1995; Gotham et al. 2019). Analysis models can propose harmonic and structural labels well enough to be corrected rather than trusted, with full-label Roman-numeral accuracy of about 51% for AugmentedNet and 62% for RNBert, and key accuracy of 82–83% (Nápoles López et al. 2021; Sailor 2024). Generation systems have converged on a control vocabulary — instrument, note density, polyphony, pitch range, chord per beat, section label, style — that reads like a list of things a composer would write on a score (Rütte et al. 2023; Guo et al. 2022; Chen et al. 2024). And the interaction pattern for turning free-form marks into machine instructions has been demonstrated in video, where ExpressEdit parses natural language plus frame sketches into temporal, spatial, operation and parameter references, resolves each with a per-modality resolver, and returns inspectable, editable suggestions (Tilekbay et al. 2024). No system found in this survey combines them for music. Treating a composer’s margin notes as a compilable instruction set is the most defensible research contribution available here.

Fifth, a symbolic-first, human-authored studio sits on materially better legal and ethical ground than the prompt-to-audio alternative. The Munich Regional Court held in November 2025 that memorisation of lyrics in model weights constitutes reproduction, and extended the reasoning to Suno in a judgment of 31 July 2026 in which the text-and-data-mining exception was found inapplicable (Landgericht München I 2025, 2026). In the United States, the Copyright Office’s second report and the Thaler line hold that human authorship is required for protection, with certiorari denied in March 2026 — which means human-authored, human-edited notation is protectable where prompt-only output largely is not (U.S. Copyright Office 2025; U.S. Court of Appeals for the D.C. Circuit 2025). Meanwhile licence-clean training material for symbolic models genuinely exists: PDMX offers more than 250,000 public-domain MusicXML scores under CC-BY, and OpenScore’s critical editions are CC0 (Long et al. 2025; Gotham and Jonas 2022). Creator surveys point the same way, with a majority accepting AI that assists the creative process while overwhelming majorities demand consent and disclosure (APRA AMCOS and Goldmedia GmbH 2024).

2. Historical lineages (F1–F3)

The studio’s central metaphor — a composer authors material and annotations, and an AI compiles a fuller realization that the composer then edits — has three ancestries, each contributing something the others lack. Algorithmic composition (F1) supplied generation from rules and probabilistic style models, and later the idea that a neural model can be a primitive inside a tool rather than an autonomous author. Interactive music systems (F2) supplied real-time listening, score following and Rowe’s three axes, which this review’s taxonomy reuses directly. The commercial arranger and notation lineage (F3) supplied the only mass-market instantiation of “chords plus style yields an arrangement,” a 35-year history whose strengths and limits define what a learned, annotation-steerable descendant must improve upon.

2.1 F1 Algorithmic composition lineage

Both paradigms a compiler needs were present at the origin. Hiller and Isaacson’s Illiac Suite (composed 1956–57 on ILLIAC I) is generally credited as the first computer-composed score; its four experiments ran from cantus firmus generation, through four-voice writing under strict counterpoint rules by generate-and-test, to rhythm and dynamics, and finally to Markov-chain generation, with the symbolic output engraved by hand and performed by a string quartet (Hiller and Isaacson 1959). Experiment 2 (hard constraints) and experiment 4 (soft stylistic priors) are exactly the two modes a compile step must combine. Koenig’s Project 1 (1964) and Project 2 (1966), as commonly dated, moved toward the composer programming the generator: selection principles and constraints went in, and a structured table of pitch, duration, register, dynamics and entry delays came out for transcription into notation (Koenig 1970). Xenakis’s UPIC (1977) made drawing an executable score — arcs on a tablet with time on one axis and pitch on the other, rendered directly to sound and transformable by stretching, transposition and inversion — a lineage continued by IanniX and UPISketch (Xenakis 1992). The paradigms are surveyed by Nierhaus (2009).

The recombinant strand culminated in Cope’s Experiments in Musical Intelligence (from 1981): roughly 20,000 lines of Lisp segmented a single composer’s corpus, matched “signatures,” classified segments with the SPEAC scheme and recombined them under grammatical constraints into new works in the style of Bach, Mozart or Chopin (Cope 2001). Hofstadter’s touring “Chopin versus EMI” tests found audiences guessing at roughly chance, Virtual Music (2001) collected the ensuing debate on whether pastiche is creativity, and Cope deleted the database in 2005 (Cope 2001). The 1994 CD Bach by Design was the first commercial recording of EMI output; the same year McIntyre’s “Bach in a Box” harmonised a melody in four-part Baroque style with a genetic algorithm and rule-based fitness — recorded in the notes from recollection rather than a fetched source (McIntyre 1994). EMI’s legacy is the canonical warning about style pastiche and authorship, and the origin of the “creativity versus perception of creativity” argument later formalised by Colton (section 3.1).

The neural turn began with Eck and Schmidhuber’s 2002 LSTM, which learned 12-bar blues progressions and melodies and is generally credited with keeping global form where earlier recurrent networks drifted (Eck and Schmidhuber 2002). Boulanger-Lewandowski et al.’s RNN-RBM (2012) modelled polyphonic piano-rolls and reportedly popularised the JSB Chorales, MuseData, Nottingham and Piano-midi.de benchmarks that anchored evaluation for a decade (Boulanger-Lewandowski et al. 2012). folk-rnn (2016) trained an LSTM on roughly 23,000 ABC-notation folk tunes, let musicians steer by key, metre and seed, and was evaluated at population and individual levels and by composers using the output, which entered sessions, concerts and an album (Sturm et al. 2016); the follow-up case study argued that generated tunes are raw material for editing and that likelihood is the wrong yardstick (B. L. Sturm et al. 2019). Two Magenta models mark the pivot from generation-as-artifact to generation-as-primitive. Coconet (2017) trained an orderless model over four-voice chorales so that any subset of notes could be masked and re-sampled by blocked Gibbs sampling — “an analogue to rewriting,” motivated by composers who “write music in a nonlinear fashion, scribbling motifs here and there” (Huang et al. 2017). Music Transformer (2018–19) made relative self-attention linear in memory, generated minute-long performance MIDI with repeated motifs and ABA-like structure, and demonstrated unconditioned generation, motif continuation and melody-to-accompaniment, with listening studies preferring it to PerformanceRNN (Huang, Vaswani, et al. 2019). What made these the pivot is what was built on them: Coconet became the Bach Doodle, which answered more than 55 million harmonisation queries in three days and released 21.6 million rated melody–harmonisation pairs (Huang, Hawthorne, et al. 2019), and the Cococo steering interface (Louie et al. 2020); MusicVAE’s latent space became an interpolation primitive (Roberts, Engel, et al. 2018) packaged as clip-level DAW devices in Magenta Studio (Roberts et al. 2019). From here the model is a component of a composer’s tool; descendants are treated under S3.1–S3.4.

2.2 F2 Interactive music systems and accompaniment lineage

Real-time accompaniment has a birth year. At ICMC 1984 Dannenberg matched a live monophonic performance against a stored score by dynamic programming and drove synchronised accompaniment from a tempo estimate (Dannenberg 1984); at the same conference Vercoe’s Synthetic Performer reportedly tracked a flautist through pitch and fingering sensors and learned tempo tendencies across rehearsals (Vercoe 1984). The Dannenberg line continued through polyphonic following, vocal tracking and the CACM 2006 review, then broadened into Human-Computer Music Performance (2011–14), which cast the computer as a band member with beat- and section-level synchronisation, human cueing and “active scores” — machine-readable, structure-aware scores with repeats and cues that can be re-sequenced live and followed by humans and machines alike (Dannenberg et al. 2014). Both founders also left a music-as-code inheritance: Vercoe’s Csound (1985–86) compiles an orchestra file and a score file into sound (Vercoe 1984), and Dannenberg’s Nyquist treats scores as programs of behaviours and temporal combinators, later becoming Audacity’s scripting language (Dannenberg 1997).

Rowe’s Interactive Music Systems (1993) turned this practice into a taxonomy whose axes the present review reuses. Rowe defined an interactive system as a sensing → processing → response chain and classified it on three dimensions (Rowe 1993). Input: score-driven systems match incoming events against a stored score and can therefore use beat and metre, whereas performance-driven systems have no stored score and rely on perceptual measures such as density and regularity. Response: transformative systems vary the material they receive, generative systems produce complete output from elementary seeds under rules, and sequenced systems play back prerecorded fragments. Relationship: in the instrument paradigm the system extends a performer’s gesture (solo-like), while in the player paradigm it is an artificial musician with its own personality (duet-like). The vocabulary became standard at ICMC and NIME (Rowe 1993). In these terms the studio is a score-driven, transformative system in the instrument paradigm, with optional player agents in Play (S5); the taxonomy’s initiative dimension D5 inherits the axes directly.

The player pole is populated by a distinguished line. Lewis’s Voyager (begun 1987) listens to an improviser through pitch-to-MIDI and generates multiple independent voices that may respond, ignore or initiate — explicitly not an instrument the human controls, rooted in Afrological improvisation aesthetics (Lewis 2000). Biles’s GenJam (1994) evolved jazz licks over a chord progression with a human “mentor” supplying real-time good/bad judgements as fitness, exposing the fitness bottleneck and formalising annotation as fitness, the primitive form of the studio’s annotate-and-regenerate loop (Biles 1994). Pachet’s Continuator (2003) learned a variable-order Markov model of a player’s phrases in real time and continued them in the same style, biasable by external constraints (Pachet 2003). Flow Machines (2012–17) generalised this into Markov constraints — style plus hard constraints on metre, harmony and position — and FlowComposer, where users chose a corpus from more than 12,000 lead sheets, entered partial melody or chords, regenerated any selected region under sliders, and in professional use rarely generated more than eight bars at a time (Papadopoulos et al. 2016). IRCAM’s OMax and Somax2 learned a Factor Oracle over the live stream and improvised by recombining the performer’s own material, without any external corpus (Assayag et al. 2006). A parallel MIT strand is institutional: Machover’s hyperinstruments (from 1986) augmented instruments with sensors so gesture shaped sound beyond the acoustic instrument, then reached non-professionals and children under the goal of “making every human being into a musician” (Machover 1992); the sketch-based Hyperscore from that group is treated under S1.3 (Farbood et al. 2004). The thread from Vercoe’s 1984 accompanist to the RL-tuned ReaLchords of 2024 is forty years long at one institution (Vercoe 1984; Yusong Wu et al. 2024).

2.3 F3 Commercial auto-accompaniment and notation lineage

Band-in-a-Box (PG Music, 1990) is the longest-lived commercial “compile from a lead sheet”: chord symbols typed into a bar grid plus key, tempo and style yield a full band arrangement, originally as MIDI styles and since 2007 as RealTracks — recorded human phrases time-stretched, pitch-shifted and spliced to fit the chords (PG Music Inc. 1990). Soloist (1997), RealDrums (2006) and the Audio Chord Wizard (2007) followed; the 2026 release advertises more than 4,400 hours of recordings with 202 new sets, a notation view, DAW export and an “AI-Notes” polyphonic transcriber (PG Music Inc. 2026). The Korg i3 (1993) established the hardware “pro arranger”: 48 styles each with four variations, two fills and two intros/endings; left-hand chord recognition driving drums, bass and three to four accompaniment parts in real time; and a 16-track sequencer plus a “Backing Sequencer” that recorded the style performance into an editable song, at about $2,500 (Korg Inc. 1993). It is credited with inspiring the Roland G-800 and Yamaha PSR variants, and the lineage runs to the Korg Pa5X (2022) and Yamaha Genos2 (2023), which add hundreds of styles, style-creator editors, chord sequencers and style parts on fixed MIDI channels (Korg Inc. 1993).

Notation editors and sequencers form two families that never merged: engraving-first Finale (1988), Encore (1990), Sibelius (1993; Avid since 2006), MuseScore (2002) and Dorico (2016), versus timeline-first Cakewalk (1987), Cubase (1989) and Logic (Notator 1988 → Apple 2002), bridged by MusicXML (2004); the sequencer dates are partially verified (MakeMusic, Inc. 2024). MakeMusic discontinued Finale on 26 August 2024, stating that its “massive codebase — millions of lines accumulated over decades — has exponentially increased the challenge of delivering customer value,” and offered Finale owners a $149 crossgrade to Dorico Pro (list $579) (MakeMusic, Inc. 2024). Dorico 6 (2025) added a rule-based Proofreading panel and no learned features (Steinberg Media Technologies 2025), Sibelius’s February 2026 release shipped no AI (Avid Technology 2026), and MuseScore Studio 4.6–4.7 added a beta audio-to-score converter with a 5.0 roadmap toward DAW-like features (Muse Group 2025, 2026). No notation incumbent ships a generative composition assistant in 2026.

The arranger was instead reborn inside the DAW. Logic Pro 11 (May 2024) extended the 2013 Drummer into Session Players: a Bass Player with eight players, complexity and intensity controls and articulations (slides, mutes, dead notes, pickup hits) and a Keyboard Player with four styles, both following the Chord Track, “trained in collaboration with” session musicians and emitting editable MIDI regions (Apple Inc. 2024). Scaler 3’s Arrange page and the Orb and Captain Chords families drive part generators from one shared progression using curated content and a “re-roll a block” convention (Scaler Music and Plugin Boutique 2025; Hexachords 2022), while Ableton Live 12 is the deliberate foil, with rule-based MIDI Generators and machine learning reserved for sample retrieval (Ableton AG 2024). The first learned member on a commercial notation surface is Hookpad Aria (2024), DeepBach’s 2017 MuseScore plugin having preceded it in research (see S3.2), which infills, continues and harmonises inside a lead-sheet editor and reported a roughly 23% acceptance rate across its deployment (see E3) (Donahue et al. 2024).

Together these products give “compile from chords + style” a 35-year history and a stable set of virtues: instant, deterministic, reproducible arrangements; few and legible parameters; editable MIDI output; and, in the best cases, curated, licensed performance data rather than generic synthesis (PG Music Inc. 2026; Apple Inc. 2024). Their limits are equally stable. The control vocabulary is coarse and closed — a style name and a few switches, with no way to say “like this recording” or “quieter here.” Styles are fixed pattern libraries external to the composer’s material, the source of the lineage’s genericness; nothing learns from what the composer wrote. The only input is a chord sequence; audio examples, humming, sketches and text play no role. Output is MIDI or audio rather than notation, the round-trip to a score is weak, and editing one part does not reconcile the others. Structure stops at intro, variation, fill and ending, and no system explains what it did.

System (year) D2 task framing D3 control handle D4 input D7 output D8 surface
Band-in-a-Box (1990–) (PG Music Inc. 1990, 2026) accompaniment from chords style, tempo, key, RealTracks chord symbols MIDI/audio; notation view standalone; DAW export
Korg i3 and arrangers (1993–) (Korg Inc. 1993) real-time accompaniment style × variation/fill/intro/ending left-hand chords sequencer MIDI hardware
Logic Session Players (2024) (Apple Inc. 2024) accompaniment from Chord Track player, complexity, intensity chord track editable MIDI regions DAW-native
Hookpad Aria (2024) (Donahue et al. 2024) infilling, continuation, harmony↔︎melody selected region lead sheet editable notation notation editor

2.4 Implications for the studio

The lineages agree on the shape of the compile step without sharing a vocabulary. From F1 the studio inherits the two-mode structure of hard constraints and soft stylistic priors, and from Koenig, Xenakis, Csound and Nyquist the idea that a composition is a program whose constraints the composer authors and whose realisation is compiled (Hiller and Isaacson 1959; Koenig 1970; Xenakis 1992; Vercoe 1984; Dannenberg 1997). From the Magenta pivot it inherits the finding that the useful unit is a model that fills holes in a human’s material in any order, deployable at web scale on a notation surface (Huang et al. 2017; Huang, Hawthorne, et al. 2019). From F2 it inherits Rowe’s axes as design coordinates, Dannenberg’s active scores as an early machine-followable structured score, Biles’s annotation-as-fitness, Pachet’s Markov constraints as the pre-deep-learning form of “in my style, subject to my annotations,” and OMax’s proof that an agent can work from the composer’s own material alone (Rowe 1993; Dannenberg et al. 2014; Biles 1994; Pachet 2003; Assayag et al. 2006). From F3 it inherits a validated interaction and a market in which the notation incumbents have left generative assistance untouched while the DAWs have rebuilt the arranger with learned players (Apple Inc. 2024; Steinberg Media Technologies 2025; Avid Technology 2026).

The gaps are as instructive. No commercial system accepts the loose modalities the studio proposes or learns from the composer’s own material; the research precedents that did — FlowComposer’s corpus selection, OMax’s reinjection, the Continuator’s real-time style learning — never reached the arranger market (Papadopoulos et al. 2016; Assayag et al. 2006; Pachet 2003). The engraving and sequencing families remain split, so a notation-first compiler that renders to audio has no incumbent architecture to copy, and the Finale exit shows that legacy monoliths are liabilities and an open engraving core the safer foundation (MakeMusic, Inc. 2024; Muse Group 2025). And the lineage leaves the AI silent: from the Illiac Suite to Session Players, no system explains which constraint or style choice produced which note — a gap section 3 shows to be general. The history therefore argues that the studio should present its compile step as the learned, annotation-steered successor of the arranger — keeping instant, deterministic, editable output and legible parameters — while replacing the closed style vocabulary with the composer’s material and annotations, treating notation as the source of truth, and adding reconciliation across parts, structure beyond the fill, and an account of what was done. Aria’s telemetry suggests that even the lead-sheet version of this proposition finds users (Donahue et al. 2024); the arranger keyboard shows what the full-arrangement version could feel like (Korg Inc. 1993).

3. Theories of human–AI co-creation (F4–F5)

Where section 2 traced what has been built, this section reviews how human–computer creative partnership has been theorised and evaluated, and which research programmes share the studio’s stance. The frameworks (F4) supply the vocabulary in which the studio’s position is stated — initiative, task division, communication direction, the AI’s role — and the instruments by which its claims can be tested. The programmes and design spaces (F5) show that “steerable, decomposable, interactive rather than imitative” is a shared agenda at Magenta and MIT, CMU, Sony CSL and the Metacreation Lab.

3.1 F4 Theories of co-creativity and creativity support

The creativity-support-tools agenda gave the field its first principles and its standard instrument. Shneiderman’s 2007 essay argued that creativity support tools should support exploratory search and collaboration, provide rich history-keeping (undo, versioning, replay), offer low thresholds with high ceilings and wide walls, allow many paths and styles, and be evaluated by long-term case studies rather than short lab tasks (Shneiderman 2007). Cherry and Latulipe’s Creativity Support Index (2014) is a validated questionnaire with six factors — Exploration, Expressiveness, Immersion, Enjoyment, Results Worth Effort and Collaboration — weighted by paired-factor comparisons to yield a 0–100 score; it has become the default instrument in music co-creation studies, used by Cococo, MMM-C and Amuse (Cherry and Latulipe 2014; Louie et al. 2020; Tchemeube et al. 2023; Yewon Kim et al. 2025). Shneiderman’s human-centered AI framework replaced the one-dimensional trade-off between automation and control with a two-dimensional space and argued for the high-control, high-automation quadrant — “supertools” rather than autonomous agents — with audit trails and explainability (Shneiderman 2020). The studio’s thesis that a compiler can be highly automated and highly controlled at once is, in this framework, a design target rather than a paradox.

Mixed-initiative and role frameworks describe who does what, when. The Mixed-Initiative Creative Interfaces workshop (CHI 2017) placed such systems between human-driven tools and autonomous computational creativity, defined them by a tight loop of constraining, suggesting and evaluating, and named the open challenges: letting non-programmers express formal constraints, legibility of AI decisions, fatigue, evaluation of co-creativity, and de-skilling (Deterding et al. 2017). Annotations are precisely a means for non-programmers to express constraints, so these are the studio’s research questions. Lubart’s roles — nanny (managing process), pen-pal (mediating human collaboration), coach (teaching techniques) and colleague (generating alternatives the human evaluates and refines) — remain the most-cited role vocabulary (Lubart 2005). Kantosalo and Toivonen, working from Wiggins’s creativity-as-search formalism, distinguished alternating co-creativity (turn-taking on one artefact) from task-divided co-creativity (agents own different sub-tasks in parallel) (Kantosalo and Toivonen 2016). Role preference has proved individual: Guzdial et al.’s turn-based Morai Maker level editor, across studies with more than 100 participants (91 in the controlled study), found designers split between wanting a friend, collaborator, student or manager, with the same agent rated helpful or annoying accordingly (Guzdial et al. 2019). Davis et al.’s Drawing Apprentice introduced participatory sense-making for turn-by-turn analysis and reported that users often could not distinguish the agent from a Wizard-of-Oz human (Davis et al. 2016). Wan et al.’s prewriting study with GPT-3 (N=15) proposed LLM-led ideation followed by human-led illumination and implementation, with humans holding initiative except when blocked (Wan et al. 2024).

Rezwana and Maher’s COFI is the most complete interaction vocabulary and the source of the field’s most consequential negative finding. It decomposes co-creation into collaboration dimensions — participation style (alternating or parallel), timing, task distribution, contribution type, creative process — and communication dimensions split into human-to-AI channels (direct manipulation, embodied, intentional) and AI-to-human channels (Rezwana and Maher 2023). Coding 92 systems, the authors found the field concentrated on generative ability and almost entirely lacked AI-to-human communication — explanations, intentions, confidence — which they argued distinguishes a partner from a tool (Rezwana and Maher 2023). Their Creative Penpal study compared a sketching partner that only drew with one that also communicated its intentions; the communicating version was reportedly perceived as more collaborative, engaging and reliable, though some found it intrusive (Rezwana and Maher 2022). The gap is visible in music: Cococo’s users developed “debugging” strategies to infer what the model was doing (Louie et al. 2020), and the systems that do communicate back — RefinPaint’s critic marking where to modify, ReaLJam’s committed-versus-tentative chord waterfall, the XAI-for-the-arts programme’s visualised latent dimensions — are recent exceptions treated under S4.4 (Ramoneda et al. 2024; Scarlatos et al. 2025; Bryan-Kinns et al. 2021).

Interactive machine learning supplies the theory of example-based specification. Fiebrink and Caramiaux argued that a learning algorithm should be understood as an interface, and that learning by demonstration lets a musician teach an idiosyncratic mapping in minutes and then “break the rules”; the Wekinator’s record–train–play–correct loop showed over years of workshops that examples and fast iteration beat parameter tweaking for non-programmers (Fiebrink and Caramiaux 2018). Scurto et al.’s Co-Explorer extended this to feedback-as-steering, with a reinforcement-learning agent adapting to like/dislike judgements in a synthesiser’s parameter space (Scurto et al. 2021), and Piano Genie, which decodes eight buttons into piano music so the human supplies contour and rhythm while the model supplies pitches, is the archetype of AI as instrument (Donahue et al. 2019).

Computational-creativity theory supplies the terms in which creativity can be evaluated at all. Boden’s distinction between combinational, exploratory and transformational creativity lets the studio say that its compiler performs exploratory generation inside a composer-defined space while the human supplies the transformational moves (Boden 2004). Ritchie’s eighteen criteria over an “inspiring set,” using typicality and quality ratings, ask whether output is both typical of the source material and good without copying it (Ritchie 2007). Colton’s tripod — skill, appreciation, imagination — stressed perception and framing, and appreciation, the ability to judge one’s own output against the brief, is the leg most generators lack (Colton 2008). Jordanous’s SPECS defines creativity for the domain from fourteen components, chooses standards and tests against them, with a case study on improvisation systems (Jordanous 2012). Karimi et al.’s four questions — who evaluates, what (product, process, interaction, experience), when, how — revealed that co-creative systems overwhelmingly evaluate user experience rather than the creativity of the collaboration (Karimi et al. 2018). These are taken up as instruments in E5; the table records the vocabulary the taxonomy adopts and where the studio sits.

Framework Vocabulary adopted Studio’s position
Rowe (Rowe 1993) score-/performance-driven; transformative/generative/sequenced; instrument/player score-driven, transformative, instrument; player agents in S5
Lubart (Lubart 2005) nanny, pen-pal, coach, colleague colleague at compile time; coach when annotating back
Kantosalo & Toivonen (Kantosalo and Toivonen 2016) alternating vs task-divided alternating on the score; task-divided by role
COFI (Rezwana and Maher 2023) participation, timing; human→AI and AI→human communication human-initiated; bidirectional (D5, D6)
Shneiderman (Shneiderman 2020) control × automation high-control, high-automation
Boden (Boden 2004) exploratory vs transformational AI exploratory; human transformational
Karimi et al. (Karimi et al. 2018) who / what / when / how evaluate interaction and process (E5)

3.2 F5 Human-centered music-AI programmes and design spaces

Anna Huang’s work is the most sustained articulation of a compatible philosophy, stated in her own words: creativity elicited “not through imitation but through interaction,” Coconet’s block-Gibbs procedure as support for “a nonlinear compositional process,” and the aim “to build systems that musicians can shape, negotiate, and jam with,” with visualisations that help them “understand and steer system behavior” (Huang 2025). The trajectory runs from ChordRipple’s chord recommendations at Harvard (Huang et al. 2016) through Magenta — Coconet, Music Transformer, the Bach Doodle and Cococo, whose study (N=21) raised controllability, comprehensibility, ownership and self-efficacy over a plain infilling baseline (Huang et al. 2017; Huang, Vaswani, et al. 2019; Huang, Hawthorne, et al. 2019; Louie et al. 2020) — to two field-defining studies. The AI Song Contest analysis of 13 teams (61 people) found that practitioners decomposed songwriting into building blocks, generated massively and curated because models were not steerable, and asked for interfaces that are “decomposable, steerable, interpretable, and adaptive” (Huang et al. 2020). Expressive Communication crossed two models with two interfaces (26 composers, 1,020 listener comparisons) and found that better steering interfaces and more expressive models each independently improved composer control, ownership and self-efficacy as well as listener-judged musicality — the two are complementary rather than substitutes (Louie et al. 2022). Since 2024 her group at MIT — where she is Robert N. Noyce Career Development Professor with a shared appointment in EECS and Music and Theater Arts — has turned toward RL-tuned jamming agents — ReaLchords, ReaLJam’s intention-displaying waterfall, the jam_bot built with Jordan Rudess, the concert case study Agents in Concert — and weekly co-design with working musicians (Yusong Wu et al. 2024; Scarlatos et al. 2025; Blanchard et al. 2025; Brade et al. 2026). It sits inside MIT’s Music Technology and Computation programme, launched Fall 2024 with Egozy as director, which held its first showcase on 13 May 2026 with a founding cohort of five and admitted ten students from more than a hundred applicants for 2026–27 (MIT News Office 2026); its ancestors are Vercoe’s studio and Machover’s hyperinstruments, both framing technology as expansion of human expression (Vercoe 1984; Machover 1992).

Chris Donahue’s Generative Creativity Lab (G-CLef) at CMU, founded 2023 to “empower and enrich human creativity and productivity with generative AI,” is the closest academic analogue of the studio’s loop — CMU’s music-technology activity otherwise splitting between the School of Music and the Dannenberg line (Carnegie Mellon University 2025) (Donahue 2026). Its line runs from Piano Genie (Donahue et al. 2019) through the Anticipatory Music Transformer, whose asynchronous conditioning lets a human pin arbitrary notes while the model fills the rest (Thickstun et al. 2024), to the deployed Hookpad Aria (Donahue et al. 2024), to Amuse (CHI 2025 Best Paper), which turned image, text and audio inspirations into chords via an editable keyword intermediate and rejection sampling against a harmonic prior, improving inspiration support, controllability, collaborative feel, expressiveness and exploration for ten songwriters with no time penalty (Yewon Kim et al. 2025), and to Decomposer (2026), which decompiles MIDI into executable Strudel programs so that repeats, voices and harmony become editable code — the founder’s “composition as program” run in the decompile direction (Kim, Gandhi, et al. 2026). Donahue holds the Dannenberg professorship, and the two CMU generations connect through Dai and Dannenberg’s finding that deep generators differ strikingly from human pop in repetition and structure, answered with user-editable structural skeletons (Dai, Yu, et al. 2022; Dai et al. 2021).

The CHI 2026 Design Space for Live Music Agents, a Donahue–Huang collaboration, is the first systematic map of real-time human–AI music collaboration, coding 184 systems into 31 dimensions; it is treated in detail under S5.4, and its dimensions overlap D4–D6 and D8 for the real-time corner (Kim, Brade, et al. 2026).

Sony CSL Paris represents a practitioner-embedded stance with a longer pedigree, from the Continuator and Flow Machines (Pachet 2003; Papadopoulos et al. 2016) to Deruty et al.’s six- to eighteen-month collaborations with six professional acts using DrumNet, BassNet, DrumGAN and related prototypes. Thematic analysis yielded “pull” over “push,” priming on the artist’s own audio, generate-and-curate and the exploitation of artefacts, alongside the friction of leaving the DAW and the lack of recognisable control; the authors proposed validating tools by workflow integration, task simplification, stimulation, identifiable contribution and commercial viability, and argued that studio pop practice is audio-first — a counterpoint the symbolic-first studio must answer by rendering stems (Deruty et al. 2022). RefinPaint, a UPF–Sony CSL Tokyo collaboration, extends the stance to critique (Ramoneda et al. 2024).

The Metacreation Lab at Simon Fraser has pursued the multi-track symbolic compiler with unusual continuity and measured it. The Multi-Track Music Machine became Calliope, in which users select bars and tracks for infilling, batch-generate up to 1,000 candidates and rank them under per-track controls for instrument, density, polyphony and duration (Tchemeube et al. 2022). Integrated into Cubase as the one-parameter MMM-C and evaluated with 18 expert composers, it scored SUS 71.4–75.7 across three tasks and highest on CSI enjoyment, but ease of control was 5.2/10 while desire for more control averaged 9.5/10 — the cost of under-parameterised control even inside the DAW (Tchemeube et al. 2023). The lineage continued into MMM4Live (Metacreation Lab (Pasquier, Philippe and Ens, Jeff and Tchemeube, Renaud Bougueng) 2023) and MIDI-GPT (AAAI 2025), with bar-level multi-track infilling and attribute controls under an Open RAIL-M licence (Pasquier et al. 2025).

A final programme is pedagogical and functions as a design stance. Egozy’s 21M.385 Interactive Music Systems at MIT has students build real-time musical software in Python with Kivy and the imslib library — synthesis and looping, MIDI sequencing, generative composition, gesture sensing, game design and visualisation — culminating in a public demo concert, with Guitar Hero as the case study of interactive systems’ cultural reach (Egozy 2016a). The stance is Harmonix’s: Guitar Hero (2005) and Rock Band (2007), co-founded by Egozy and Rigopulos out of Machover’s group, let non-musicians perform through simplified controllers and a scrolling note highway, reportedly selling more than 35 million units (Egozy and Rigopulos 2005); Egozy’s account of musical expression in those games, and later work on in-browser score following and AI-augmented instruments, articulates how agency survives inside constrained interfaces (Egozy 2016b). Miller’s ethnography argued the games are genuine performance and informal learning (Miller 2009), and Fuser and DropMix showed casual users building coherent arrangements from constrained stems (Harmonix Music Systems 2020). ConcertCue, streaming time-synchronised programme notes cued to musical events, is a deployed time-aligned annotation system aimed at listeners (Egozy 2018), and Cuthbert’s 21M.383 teaches composers to treat notation as data over the music21 corpus — the mindset behind composition as a program (Cuthbert 2023; Cuthbert and Ariza 2010). The note highway is the ancestor of ReaLJam’s waterfall and Piano Genie’s contour mapping (Scarlatos et al. 2025; Donahue et al. 2019), and the pedagogy’s insistence on low latency, visual clarity and playfulness is a design prior for Play mode.

3.3 Implications for the studio

The frameworks let the studio’s position be stated exactly, and the statement exposes where it departs from the literature. In Rowe’s terms the compiler is score-driven, transformative and in the instrument paradigm; in Lubart’s it is a colleague at compile time and a coach when it annotates back; in Kantosalo and Toivonen’s it alternates on the score and divides tasks by role; in Shneiderman’s it must occupy the high-control, high-automation quadrant (Rowe 1993; Lubart 2005; Kantosalo and Toivonen 2016; Shneiderman 2020). In COFI’s terms it is human-initiated and bidirectional — and this is the departure, because AI-to-human communication was almost entirely absent across 92 coded systems (Rezwana and Maher 2023). Evidence that such communication is wanted comes from Creative Penpal, from Cococo’s users reverse-engineering the model, and from ReaLJam’s pianists asking to see the agent’s plan, while RefinPaint shows what a critic that marks where to change looks like (Rezwana and Maher 2022; Louie et al. 2020; Scarlatos et al. 2025; Ramoneda et al. 2024). A compiler that returns annotations — what it did with each mark, where it is unsure, what alternatives it considered — is therefore both a theoretical requirement and an empirical gap (S4.4, D6).

The programmes converge on requirements the studio should treat as settled. Practitioners decompose, steer and curate and want interpretability (Huang et al. 2020); steering interfaces move ownership more than stronger models, and the two are complementary (Louie et al. 2022); one control parameter inside the DAW is not enough (Tchemeube et al. 2023); professionals pull rather than accept pushes, prime on their own material and refuse to leave their tools (Deruty et al. 2022); examples beat parameters for musicians (Fiebrink and Caramiaux 2018); initiative must be adjustable because role preference is individual (Guzdial et al. 2019); and ownership tracks the strength of the human’s authored input (Krol et al. 2025). The MICI challenges remain open and are the questions an annotation layer must answer (Deterding et al. 2017), while the live-agents design space shows the real-time corner well mapped and ecosystem questions addressed by one system in twenty (Kim, Brade, et al. 2026).

Two gaps remain. First, evaluation: co-creative systems overwhelmingly measure user experience, long-term case studies were called for in 2007, and longitudinal music studies remain rare (Karimi et al. 2018; Shneiderman 2007; Deruty et al. 2022). The studio should adopt the CSI with ownership, control and comprehension items, pair it with listener judgements in the Expressive Communication protocol, apply Ritchie’s criteria and SPECS’s components to compiled output, and log interventions as provenance from the first prototype (E1, E5) (Cherry and Latulipe 2014; Louie et al. 2022; Ritchie 2007; Jordanous 2012). Second, the surface: every programme reviewed here operates on a piano-roll, a lead sheet, a DAW clip or a live instrument; none has articulated a human-centered agenda for the engraved score, the surface on which composers in the classical and arranging traditions actually annotate. The frameworks are ready; the notation-native instance of them is the studio’s contribution to make.

4. Compose: getting musical ideas in (S1)

The composer’s loop begins with raw material that rarely arrives as clean notation: it is played on a keyboard, hummed into a phone, scribbled on paper, photographed, or triggered by an image, a film cue or a sentence. The Compose node (S1) collects the systems that turn such inputs into something a studio can hold — MIDI, a lead sheet, a chord chart, a score — across five channels: performance capture and transcription (S1.1), voice (S1.2), sketch and pen (S1.3), image, video and text (S1.4), and example audio or personal style (S1.5). The recurring result, stated first because it organises what follows, is that every loose modality that has succeeded with musicians lands in a symbolic, editable representation, while systems that map directly to audio offer no handle for correction; and that even the best transcribers err often enough that a correction interface is a design requirement rather than a courtesy (Donahue et al. 2022; S. Liu, Li, et al. 2024; Yewon Kim et al. 2025; Cavez et al. 2025).

4.1 S1.1 Performance capture and transcription

Audio-to-MIDI transcription is the most mature loose channel, and its architecture has been stable since Hawthorne et al. (2018) introduced Onsets and Frames: a CNN–BiLSTM predicts onsets and frame-wise pitch activity jointly, onsets gate the frames so no note begins without a detected attack, and velocity is predicted alongside. It more than doubled note-with-offset F1 on MAPS, and its failure modes — missed offsets, merged repeated notes — are inherited by every descendant. Gardner et al. (2022) generalised the recipe to mixtures with MT3, a T5-style encoder–decoder emitting MIDI-like tokens with instrument programs, trained jointly on MAESTRO, Slakh, Cerberus4, GuitarSet, MusicNet and URMP; joint training substantially improved low-resource instruments such as guitar. Bittner et al. (2022) went the other way with Basic Pitch, a very small instrument-agnostic CNN that outputs MIDI with pitch bends, ships in Python and TypeScript under Apache-2.0, and exposes onset and frame thresholds and a minimum note length as the user’s only correction knobs. For a symbolic-first studio the more consequential system is Sheet Sage: Donahue et al. (2022) fed Jukebox representations — following the JukeMIR finding that codified-audio language models learn transferable MIR features (Castellon et al. 2021) — into a melody transcriber, gaining 20% over spectrogram features and 77% over the best prior baseline, then composed it with beat, key and chord estimation to emit a lead sheet from arbitrary audio. Its 50-hour Hooktheory melody-and-harmony dataset later trained Amuse’s prior and Hookpad Aria (Yewon Kim et al. 2025; Donahue et al. 2024).

The large expressive-piano corpora that anchor symbolic modelling are themselves transcription products, which matters for provenance (B4): MAESTRO paired roughly 200 hours of Disklavier performances with audio under CC-BY-NC-SA (Hawthorne et al. 2019); GiantMIDI-Piano and ATEPP transcribed 10,855 and 11,674 recordings under CC-BY 4.0 (Kong et al. 2022; H. Zhang et al. 2022); Aria-MIDI scaled to 1,186,253 files (~100,629 hours) transcribed from YouTube, again non-commercial (Bradshaw and Colton 2025). Optical music recognition is the parallel path for material that starts on paper. The Sheet Music Transformer of Ríos-Vila et al. (2024) transcribed polyphonic pianoform and quartet images end-to-end into Humdrum **kern, cutting character error rate by 91.8% and line error rate by 89.1% on GrandStaff (53,882 images) and a new Quartets set (38,051); oemer (MIT) targets MusicXML from phone photographs, and Audiveris (AGPL-3.0) wraps its pipeline in an interactive OMR editor for correction before MusicXML 4.0 export. Jung et al. (2025) then unified score images, MusicXML, MIDI and audio under one tokenisation, trained on the >1,300-hour YouTube Score Video dataset, reducing OMR symbol error rate from 24.58% to 13.67% and demonstrating the first score-image-conditioned audio generation. In all of these the human controls nothing at inference; correction happens downstream in a DAW or notation editor, which is why the studio’s transcription step must terminate in an editable surface (Bittner et al. 2022; Ríos-Vila et al. 2024).

4.2 S1.2 Voice: humming and singing as input

The voice lineage is thirty years old and has taught one durable lesson: hummed pitch is unreliable, so systems either reduce it to a coarse representation or accept that a human must fix it. Ghias et al. (1995) converted a hum to a three-symbol up/down/same contour string matched approximately against a 183-song MIDI database, finding that 10–12 pitch transitions discriminated 90% of songs. Google’s Hum to Search replaced the symbolic intermediate with an embedding trained to land hums near studio recordings across more than 500k songs, using synthetic hums derived via SPICE pitch extraction (Frank 2020) — production-viable retrieval with no editable intermediate whatsoever. The transcription side has been quantified by HumTrans, a 56.22-hour dataset of 1,000 segments hummed twice each by ten music-trained students against reference melodies, on which the best off-the-shelf vocal transcriber reached only 6.8% note F1 on validation and 5.7% on test (S. Liu, Li, et al. 2024). Gupta et al. (2024) corrected HumTrans’s onset and offset labels and paired a CNN frame classifier with dynamic-programming decoding to reach an octave-invariant note-plus-onset F1 of 0.673 against 0.604 for the next-best method (MIR-ST500) — a real but modest gain that still leaves roughly one note in three wrong, and whose DP stage is the natural place to inject a composer’s declared key and meter as priors.

Products reached the same conclusion by other means. ScoreCloud (2014–) and Sing2Notes (2021–) transcribe singing straight to sheet music with quantised and unquantised MIDI and MusicXML export, and Sing2Notes ships an in-app Edit Mode; neither publishes accuracy, though Klangio claims more than four million transcriptions (Doremir Music Research AB and Klangio GmbH 2014). Vochlea’s Dubler 2 converts singing, humming and beatboxing to MIDI in real time with a key/scale lock, a visual note wheel, a “stickiness” slider that smooths spurious transitions, vowel-to-CC mapping and per-user training of up to twelve examples per beatbox sound (Vochlea Music 2021) — constrain, smooth, visualise and personalise rather than trust the tracker. At the audio end, SingSong adapted AudioLM to generate a full instrumental accompaniment from a sung vocal, mining (vocal, instrumental) pairs by source separation; listeners significantly preferred it to a retrieval baseline (the reported margin is given under S3.9), and the only control is to sing again (Donahue et al. 2023). It is the strongest demonstration that a hummed line implies harmony, groove and style, and the clearest case of an inspiring system with no correction handle. Two strands widen what the voice can specify. VocalSketch collected 4,429 vocal imitations from 248 contributors and found forced-choice identification of 0.80 for everyday sounds but only 0.42–0.54 for synthesiser sounds and 0.45 for instruments against 0.1 chance (Cartwright and Pardo 2015); Sketch2Sound made a vocal imitation a control signal, extracting loudness, spectral-centroid and pitch curves into a text-to-audio DiT through one linear layer per control, with training-time median filtering so the user chooses how tightly the output follows the gesture (Flores García et al. 2025). Tone Transfer had put the simplest version in a browser in 2020 — SPICE pitch plus a DDSP model re-render a hum as flute, saxophone, violin or trumpet (Magenta and Google AIUX teams 2020; Engel et al. 2020) — and Piano Genie’s eight buttons decoded to full piano performances remain the archetype of low-dimensional performed intent realised by a model (Donahue et al. 2019).

4.3 S1.3 Sketch, pen and scribble as input

Drawing as a score predates deep learning by decades. Xenakis’s UPIC (1977) let a composer draw arcs on a tablet with time on one axis and pitch on the other, and draw waveforms and envelopes too; IanniX later generalised it into an open-source graphical sequencer emitting OSC and MIDI (Xenakis and Saint-Jean 1977; Xenakis 1992). UPIC also carries a warning: its drawings map to sound, not notation, so a system must decide what a scribble means. Hyperscore made that decision explicit. Motives entered on a pitch–time grid are assigned colours; coloured strokes in a sketch window instantiate them, with stroke length as repetitions and shape as transposition contour; and a separate harmony line — flat for stability, rising for tension, spiked for modulation — drives re-harmonisation of the whole texture into orchestral notation (Farbood et al. 2004). In Toy Symphony workshops in five cities, with seven to twelve children each over five sessions, nearly every child completed a string-orchestra piece. Coughlan and Johnson (2006) supplied the theory, proposing ideation–evaluation cycles as the atomic unit of creative interaction and arguing that tools should support incomplete, ambiguous representations; Garcia et al. (2012) reportedly extended this to interactive paper on which composers lay out their own pen-sensitive staff, curve and timeline substrates linked to OpenMusic and Max.

The pen has since become a commercial notation surface and an object of HCI study. StaffPad (Surface 2015, iPad 2020) recognises handwriting one measure at a time, replaces ink with engraving, keeps a correction palette, and pushes parts and shared annotations to players’ tablets through ScoreSync (Hearn 2015). Cavez et al. (2024) interviewed nine professional composers and, through the Cognitive Dimensions of Notations, found that structured notation editors impose premature commitment and viscosity that block ideation and drive composers back to paper; their EuterPen prototype selectively relaxes syntactic and structural constraints so composers can move symbols freely and “make space on, between and around staves to insert additional content such as digital ink, pictures and audio samples” (Cavez et al. 2025). Participating composers called it compelling; there is no AI in it, which is exactly what makes it the closest existing model of the studio’s scribble-as-annotation step.

Sketch-conditioned generation is a smaller but well-evidenced line. Music SketchNet treated a sketch as a partial specification of a missing measure — pitch contour, rhythm pattern, or both — and factorised a VAE so each could be supplied independently; a 106-subject listening test found improved musicality over baselines (Chen et al. 2020). Draw and Listen! inpainted missing measures from a drawn pitch-contour curve and a note-density curve, beating rule-based and genetic baselines on 24,065 Irish tunes, and users without notation knowledge found it intuitive (Benetatos and Duan 2022). MIDI-Draw found that non-musicians drew over-oscillating curves and wanted rhythm variation, arguing for smoothing affordances and a separate rhythm lane (Namgyal et al. 2022). Drawlody is the best-evidenced system: a CNN–Transformer maps a simplified contour to a melody and displays the alignment between sketch and result; eighteen participants rated its user-friendliness 4.38/5 against 2.94 for a chord-based and 2.38 for a DAW interface, and interpretability 4.50/5, while musicality stayed below expert compositions (Liang and Wang 2024). The shared interaction contract — coarse gesture, plausible inference, instant display, gestural refinement — was set by Teddy’s inflation of 2D strokes into 3D models (Igarashi et al. 1999); the claim that pen input’s value lies in relaxing structure rather than recognising handwriting rests on the Paris-Saclay studies (Cavez et al. 2024, 2025).

4.4 S1.4 Image, video and text as inspiration or condition

Amuse is the reference design for turning a non-musical inspiration into editable material (Yewon Kim et al. 2025). An image, text or audio clip goes to a multimodal LLM (GPT-4o), which extracts music keywords the user can inspect, add or delete; the LLM proposes N diverse four-bar chord progressions; and a rejection sampler accepts a candidate only if u < P(x)/(M·Q(x)), where P is an LSTM chord prior trained on the 50-hour Hooktheory data, Q an LSTM fitted to 25,000 GPT-4o progressions, and M = 7.64. Audio inspirations go through a chord transcriber instead, and accepted chords paste into Hookpad for audition and editing. In a within-subjects study with ten songwriters (eight hobbyists, two professionals) writing an eight-bar chorus in 25 minutes, Amuse plus Aria beat Aria alone on inspiration support (6.20 vs 4.60, p < .01), controllability (5.80 vs 4.40), collaboration, exploration and expressiveness, with no difference in completion time or externally judged quality; 45 listeners found the rejection-sampled chords matched the LSTM prior on coherence, exceeded raw GPT-4o (p = .009) and were most preferred for keyword relevance (58%). The transferable moves are the human-legible intermediate and the small domain prior that disciplines a broad LLM; the cautionary finding is that participants expected the downstream model to know the inspiration too, so annotations must propagate through the whole compile chain (Yewon Kim et al. 2025; Donahue et al. 2024).

The wider image-to-music literature splits by output domain, and the split predicts usefulness to a composer. Vis2Mus, a rare symbolic system, found visual-to-music mappings approximately equivariant and let users steer generated accompaniment through an image’s brightness and contrast, with participants matching transformed music to transformed images at 62.5–68.2% against 50% chance (R. Zhang et al. 2022). MeLFusion injected text-to-image attention features into a music diffusion model, improving FAD by up to 67.98% over text-only on the 11,250-triplet MeLBench, but emits audio only (Chowdhury et al. 2024). Art2Mus is the honest negative result: an ImageBind encoder on frozen AudioLDM 2 lowered KL divergence, yet raters preferred plain AudioLDM 2, and the 2026 extension with 105,884 pairs found only marginal differences (Rinaldi et al. 2024). Video follows suit: V2Meow generated ten-second audio from frame features and an optional text prompt with 83.8% visual-relevance preference (Su et al. 2024); VidMuse scaled to ~360k video–music pairs with a long–short-term visual module (Tian et al. 2025); MuVi separated semantic alignment from rhythmic synchronisation, raising beat-hit score from 25.14% to 49.23% (R. Li et al. 2024); MuMu-LLaMA wrapped MERT, ViT and ViViT encoders around LLaMA with MusicGen and AudioLDM 2 decoders to accept any modality plus prompt-based edits, with 81.3% and 80.7% preference for image- and video-to-music (S. Liu, Hussain, Wu, et al. 2024). None yields anything a composer can edit. Video2Music is the exception: an Affective Multimodal Transformer decodes scene cuts, motion, emotion and semantics into a chord sequence, the user supplies key and an optional seed progression, and 21 raters scored it 4.2/7 against 3.5 for a Music Transformer baseline (Kang et al. 2024). MuVi’s semantic/rhythmic and VidMuse’s global/local splits are the two things a film composer actually marks — mood and hit points — and suggest a symbolic pipeline of detect, annotate, compile (R. Li et al. 2024; Tian et al. 2025).

Text-to-symbolic generation, the commonest inspiration channel, points the same way, and is treated as a compile-time control under S3.3: the systems that work best interpose an explicit, user-editable layer of attributes or tags between the sentence and the notes rather than mapping prose straight to music (Lu et al. 2023; Bhandari et al. 2025; S.-L. Wu, Carlton, et al. 2026; Xu et al. 2025). Across S1.4, inspiration is best routed through a visible intermediate — keywords, attributes, a chord chart — that the composer can override before anything is compiled.

4.5 S1.5 Example audio and personal style as reference

Musicians who think in references rather than theory terms want to steer by instance. Music Creation by Example studied 104 video creators and found that supplying an example song, then regenerating and mixing at stem level, was more natural than tag or text search (Frid et al. 2020). Cococo’s example-based similarity slider, one of four steering tools that lifted controllability from 3.5 to 5.9 and ownership from 3.8 to 5.2 among 21 novices, implemented the same idea as a soft prior on Coconet’s sampling (Louie et al. 2020). MusicGen-Style conditioned MusicGen on an excerpt through a discrete RVQ bottleneck with heavy dropout so that style is copied without content, balanced text against audio with double classifier-free guidance, and released weights under CC-BY-NC (Rouard et al. 2024); Neutone’s plugins reportedly run neural timbre models in real time inside a DAW with an SDK for artists to train on their own recordings (Neutone Inc. 2024).

Personal style is the symbolic counterpart. MidiMe trained a four-dimensional VAE over MusicVAE’s 256-dimensional latent from a single user MIDI file, in the browser in seconds, yielding a personal four-knob space of variations (Dinculescu et al. 2019). MIDI-RWKV introduced state tuning — fine-tuning only the initial recurrent state of a linear-time RWKV-7 model — so a composer’s handful of files personalise an edge-deployable infilling model, released under CC-BY 4.0 (Zhou-Zheng and Pasquier 2025). Dai, Ma, et al. (2022) analysed a reference song’s structure, melody, chords and bass style and generated a new song constrained to that template, with the user choosing what to imitate. In the terms of programming by demonstration, each generalises from a demonstrated example and lets the user correct the generalisation (Cypher 1993).

4.6 Implications for the studio

The central finding is architectural. Every loose modality that musicians have adopted terminates in an editable symbolic object: humming in MIDI or a lead sheet (Bittner et al. 2022; Donahue et al. 2022; Doremir Music Research AB and Klangio GmbH 2014), drawing in notes or a contour lane (Farbood et al. 2004; Liang and Wang 2024; Hearn 2015), images and video in keywords and chords (Yewon Kim et al. 2025; Kang et al. 2024), text in an attribute layer (Lu et al. 2023). Systems that skip the symbolic layer — SingSong, V2Meow, VidMuse, MeLFusion, Hum to Search — are impressive and offer the composer nothing to correct but the input (Donahue et al. 2023; Su et al. 2024; Frank 2020), and where a direct comparison exists, end-to-end conditioning was not even preferred (Rinaldi et al. 2024). The studio should route all eight modalities (D4) into notation or MIDI first and treat audio as a rendering target (S3.8); a symbolic SingSong — sung melody to chords and arrangement in notation — is an open and well-motivated target.

Second, transcription needs a correction interface, not a better model alone. Purpose-built humming transcription reaches ~0.67 note F1 and off-the-shelf models 0.43–0.60 on the same relabelled data — the single-digit F1 scores reported against HumTrans’s original labels reflect defective onset annotation rather than model incapability (S. Liu, Li, et al. 2024; Gupta et al. 2024); the best OMR still misreads more than one symbol in eight (Jung et al. 2025); Roman-numeral labelling sits at 51–62% on full labels (S2.2). The working patterns exist in products — scale lock and stickiness smoothing (Vochlea Music 2021), click-to-fix edit modes (Doremir Music Research AB and Klangio GmbH 2014), threshold sliders (Bittner et al. 2022), measure-granular recognition with a correction palette (Hearn 2015), an OMR editor before export (Ríos-Vila et al. 2024) — and composer-declared key and meter should enter decoders as priors, with every transcription shown for confirmation before it is compiled.

The gaps follow. Pen input should relax structure, not merely recognise handwriting: EuterPen’s ink, pictures and audio clips between staves is the model, and it awaits an AI that reads them (Cavez et al. 2025). Contour sketches deserve a first-class annotation type with separate pitch and rhythm lanes, smoothing affordances and an alignment view showing how the sketch was interpreted (Chen et al. 2020; Namgyal et al. 2022; Liang and Wang 2024). Amuse’s keyword intermediate must propagate through the whole compile chain (Yewon Kim et al. 2025). And no system yet accepts humming, sketch, image and example audio on the same score; the annotation layer that would make this possible is the subject of the next section.

5. Annotate: expressing intent on the material (S2)

Annotation is the studio’s central concept: a typed, musically anchored mark — on a bar, a staff, a region or a note — that the compiler must honour. Three literatures bear on it, and they have barely met. Musicology and notation engineering supply mature encodings for marks anchored to musical time (S2.1); music information retrieval supplies analysis models that can propose such marks for a human to accept or fix (S2.2); and controllable generation has converged on a control vocabulary — density, polyphony, register, chord per bar, section label, tension curve — that is exactly what a composer would annotate (S2.3). What is missing is the fourth piece: treating a composer’s multimodal marks on notation as instructions to a generator, for which the templates come from video and vision rather than music (S2.4). A short final subsection records the evidence that one well-placed human label can outperform automation (S2.5).

5.1 S2.1 Annotation encodings, standards and tools

Machine-readable score annotation is a solved problem in digital musicology, and the solutions share a shape: a typed label, an anchor in symbolic time, an optional span, free text. MEI encodes Western notation with editorial markup for readings, corrections, supplied text and genetic layers, and Verovio engraves it to SVG in which every glyph is an addressable element carrying its MEI id, alongside MIDI and a timemap for audio synchronisation (Pugin et al. 2014); MEI thus already has vocabulary for “this passage is a variant or an editorial suggestion” — for distinguishing an AI proposal from the composer’s text. mei-friend places an MEI code view beside a live Verovio rendering, commits encodings to GitHub and attaches Web Annotations to score elements (Goebl and Weigl 2023), a precedent for text–notation dual editing, git-based history (S4.5) and annotation anchored to notation ids. MusicXML 4.0 (W3C Final Community Group Specification, June 2021) remains the interchange format read by 250+ applications, but its annotation vocabulary stops at <direction> text; its JSON successor MNX had settled on .mnx.json in March 2026 yet was still deciding core encoding questions in August 2026 and cannot be treated as finished (W3C Music Notation Community Group 2021). ABC is compact, text-native and re-rendered on every keystroke by abcjs, at the cost of weaker polyphony and layout (Walshaw 2011; Rosen 2026). Humdrum makes annotation a parallel column: kern holds the notes and users add harm, text or dynam spines beside them, so labels and notes travel in one plain-text file consumable by seventy-odd tools and by an LLM (Huron 1995).

Analysts’ tools show what a good annotation interface looks like. Dezrann places labels by dragging left-to-right for a span or top-to-bottom for an instant, on a staff or in zones above and below it; each label carries a type, an onset and optional duration in measure-and-beat time, a tag and a comment, so the same .dez JSON aligns to score, waveform and video; by 2025 it held 35,000+ annotations over 1,500+ pieces in ten corpora with collaborative editing, under GPLv3+ code and ODbL data (Giraud et al. 2018). Sonic Visualiser is the audio-side counterpart: layers of instants, curves, notes and regions over a waveform, entered by clicking, tapping during playback or MIDI, with Vamp plugins that auto-generate beat, chord, key and segmentation layers the user then corrects (Cannam et al. 2010). Theorists have standardised the label grammars. RomanText is an off-score, human-writable text format — measure, beat, key, Roman numeral, comment — parsed by music21, in which When in Rome holds 2,000+ analyses of 1,500 works with conversion to on-score labels (Gotham et al. 2019; Cuthbert and Ariza 2010). The DCML standard is the on-score alternative: ~36,000 harmony, phrase and cadence labels for the sixteen Beethoven quartets were typed as text attached to notes in MuseScore, then extracted by the ms3 parser into tables, with the syntax defined by a regular expression that admits recursive applied chords (Neuwirth et al. 2018; Hentschel et al. 2021). This “annotate in the editor, parse to a table” workflow is the one the studio needs, and the regex grammar is directly reusable for validating harmonic instructions.

Performers annotate too, and their practices supply a taxonomy. Newzik’s LiveScores turn PDFs into interactive MusicXML and synchronise a conductor’s marks to an ensemble’s tablets, treating annotations as first-class objects separate from the score (Newzik SAS and forScore LLC 2026), as does StaffPad’s ScoreSync (Hearn 2015). Tactus analysed seventeen practice videos and ten interviews and distinguished performance-oriented annotations that persist (dynamics, bowings, breaths) from practice-oriented ones that are temporary (rhythm visualisations, accidentals, progress), then proposed system-authored feedback and feedforward marks alongside user-created ones (Fu and Cavez 2026). ConcertCue streamed program notes to audience phones during Boston Symphony and other performances, cued to specific musical events (Egozy 2018). Together these fix the fields a studio annotation record needs: type, anchor, span, author (human or system), lifetime (persistent or ephemeral) and free text.

5.2 S2.2 Machine-proposed annotations (analysis models)

Analysis models can propose labels for the composer to accept or fix, and their accuracy curve tells the studio how much to trust them. AugmentedNet read pitch-spelled MusicXML and predicted eleven tonal tasks jointly before assembling Roman numerals, reaching 82.9% on key, 67.0% on scale degree and 46.4% on the full Roman numeral; it was adopted for harmonic analysis in Sibelius (Nápoles López et al. 2021). ChordGNN modelled the score as a note graph built with partitura and pooled note-wise features to onset-wise predictions, reporting higher accuracy than AugmentedNet (Karystinaios and Widmer 2023); RNBert fine-tuned the MusicBERT symbolic language model for key-conditioned Roman-numeral prediction and reported state of the art by transfer from unlabelled pretraining (Sailor 2024) — evidence that one symbolic foundation model can serve generation and annotation heads alike. The segmentation-plus-labelling framing came from Chen and Su’s Harmony Transformer line and the BPS-FH dataset (Chen and Su 2021). Full-label accuracy near one in two is good enough to propose and not good enough to trust silently — the mixed-initiative posture the annotate step requires.

Structure analysis is more mature. MSAF bundled the classical boundary and labelling algorithms with mir_eval evaluation (Nieto and Bello 2016); All-In-One predicted beats, downbeats, section boundaries and functional labels (intro, verse, chorus, bridge, outro) jointly from source-separated spectrograms and set the state of the art on the Harmonix Set with fewer parameters (Kim and Nam 2023). For symbolic pop, Dai and Dannenberg extracted section/phrase repetition structure from 909 Chinese pop transcriptions with 93% phrase-boundary accuracy, finding that repeated phrases cover 50–90% of most songs and that V–I cadences close 94% of sections against 47% elsewhere (Dai et al. 2024), and showed that deep generators differ strikingly from human pop on these metrics (Dai, Yu, et al. 2022) — the empirical case for structure as an explicit, editable layer (Dai et al. 2021).
