Deeper theory has thinner tooling: the Duke Schenkerian dataset grew the machine-readable corpus from 41 to more than 140 excerpts with custom notation software for reductions and a heterogeneous-graph formulation (Ni-Hahn et al. 2024), while galant schema detection via skipgram pattern discovery reportedly remains open (Finkensiep et al. 2018). Older baselines still power products: madmom-era chord and key estimators reportedly plateaued near 80–85% major/minor accuracy, and symbolic phrase segmentation lacks a shared benchmark (Böck et al. 2016). Two further families act as critics rather than labellers. Voice-leading checkers — music21’s voiceLeading module and, reportedly, Palestrina Pal — are deterministic linters that flag parallel fifths and voice crossings, the natural “compiler warnings” for compiled output (Huang and Chew 2005; Cuthbert and Ariza 2010). SynTheory probed Jukebox and MusicGen layer by layer for tempo, meter, intervals, scales, chords and progressions and found them linearly detectable, varying by size and layer (Wei et al. 2024) — a method for checking whether a backbone understands the vocabulary the composer annotates with. Whether LLMs understand it is doubtful: MusicTheoryBench and MuChoMusic both report weak reasoning and over-reliance on the language modality (Yuan et al. 2024; Weck et al. 2024), so the LLM should orchestrate symbolic analysers rather than replace them (E6).

5.3 S2.3 Control vocabularies as annotation schemas

Controllable generation has, without coordinating, converged on the attributes a composer would write in the margin. FIGARO conditions a seq2seq Transformer on a per-bar description of expert features — instruments present, chords, time signature, note density, mean pitch, mean velocity, mean duration — plus learned VQ-VAE codes, so a composer can edit the bar-by-bar description and regenerate (Rütte et al. 2023): a formal annotation language in all but name. MusIAC added track-level note density, polyphony and occupation rate and bar-level tonal tension (tensile strain and cloud diameter) as infilling controls, finding the richer tokens improved perceived coherence over density alone (Guo et al. 2022). Composer’s Assistant 2 lets a composer create empty MIDI items on named REAPER tracks to mark what to generate, then set rhythmic-conditioning modes, horizontal and vertical onset density, pitch range and “rhythmic interest”; its listening study rated AI-completed excerpts comparably to fully human ones (Malandro 2024, 2023). MIDI-GPT, a ~20M-parameter bar-within-track model shipped inside Cubase and Ableton Live, conditions on instrument, ten instrument-relative density levels, polyphony range, a five-bin duration range and a style ontology (Pasquier et al. 2025). MuseCoco’s twelve-attribute layer (Lu et al. 2023), MuseMorphose’s eight-level bar-wise rhythmic-intensity and polyphony faders over an existing piece (Wu and Yang 2023), and Polyffusion’s separation of internal masks (what to keep) from external chord and texture conditions (what to steer) (Min et al. 2023) complete the symbolic inventory.

Two distinctions in this literature matter for an annotation compiler. The first is hard versus soft. SymPAC placed genre, section labels, tempo, chords and track list in prompt bars the user may partially specify and enforced them by finite-state constrained decoding rather than soft conditioning; twelve MIR researchers and producers rated it significantly above FIGARO and MMT on structure and arrangement (Chen et al. 2024). A composer who writes “Cmaj7 here” expects a constraint, not a suggestion, and the Anticipatory Music Transformer offers the same guarantee for notes: whatever is pinned becomes a control event the model must condition on, wherever in time it lies (Thickstun et al. 2024). The second is discrete versus continuous. Music ControlNet accepted melody, dynamics and rhythm curves specified for only part of the timeline — a melody for bars 1–4, free elsewhere — and was 49% more faithful to input melodies than MusicGen with 35× fewer parameters (S.-L. Wu et al. 2024); Sketch2Sound’s loudness, brightness and pitch curves from a vocal gesture are the same idea (S1.2) (Flores García et al. 2025). Music FaderNets grounded the high-level attribute arousal, learned from 1% labels, in low-level rhythm and note-density faders (Tan and Herremans 2020), showing how “more intense here” can be compiled to controllable features. Structure enters too: melody infilling with user-provided structural context let the user say which earlier phrase a gap should echo, and structure-aware fills beat structure-agnostic baselines (Tan et al. 2022). Finally, constraints must combine: Compositional Steering reportedly composed lightweight attribute controls (pitch range, density, key) into one product-of-experts distribution over a frozen Music Transformer (Young et al. 2021), and Cococo’s voice lanes, semantic sliders and example slider were soft priors on Coconet’s sampling that raised controllability from 3.5 to 5.9 among 21 novices (Louie et al. 2020). Read together, S2.3 already defines the schema — instrument, density, polyphony, register, chord, section, tension, dynamics curve, structural reference — and the two semantics, hard constraint or soft prior, that an annotation type must declare.

5.4 S2.4 Multimodal annotation as instruction

No fetched music system treats a composer’s natural-language, drawn, hummed or attached marks on notation as instructions to a generator; the templates come from neighbouring media. ExpressEdit is the most complete (Tilekbay et al. 2024). Its design was grounded in 176 multimodal edit requests from ten video editors, all using natural language and 78 of 97 visual ones sketched on frames. GPT-4 parses each request into four reference types — temporal (“whenever he discusses tips”), spatial (“top-left”, or the sketch), edit operation and parameters; temporal references are resolved positionally, against the transcript or against dense visual captions by embedding similarity, spatial references by Segment-Anything crops matched to text or sketch in CLIP space. On 50 ground-truth commands the pipeline reached temporal recall 0.68, spatial mIoU 0.56 and operation F1 0.82. Crucially, output is a set of edit suggestions the user accepts, rejects or adjusts, with an “Examine” panel showing how each reference was interpreted. Among ten novices, 45.98% of suggestions were accepted and 58.09% of final edits were modified suggestions; time split roughly a third each into describing, examining and manual editing; SUS was 75.7, the Examine feature was rated 6.1/7, sketches appeared in 26% of commands, and vague commands failed. Substitute bar, beat and section for timecodes, staff, voice and register for screen regions, reharmonise, thin and transpose for cut, zoom and crop, and audio similarity for CLIP, and the grammar transfers almost verbatim.

Two vision-side results fix the interaction and data models. DirectGPT wrapped an LLM in direct manipulation — a continuously visible object, toolbar commands that reuse prompt syntax, drag-and-select to compose prompts, undo — and users edited text, code and vector images 50% faster with 50% fewer and 72% shorter prompts than with chat alone (Masson et al. 2024); selection first, then instruction, is what makes a short annotation sufficient, and it is the modern form of Shneiderman’s continuous, reversible, immediately visible manipulation (Shneiderman 1983). InstructPix2Pix trained an (image, instruction) to edited-image model on triples synthesised by GPT-3 and Stable Diffusion with no human labelling (Brooks et al. 2023) — the recipe for bootstrapping the (score, annotation, edited score) dataset that annotation-driven symbolic editing lacks. The music-side pieces are partial. Libretto defined a text grammar with explicit onset slots, voice declarations and bar blocks, a 29-axis structural fingerprint calibrated to corpus percentiles, and an LLM agent that loops generate → measure → musician-readable feedback (“reduce harmonic instability”) → revise, lifting gap-filling pass rate from 12% single-shot to 39% and reaching 94% on full pieces with retrieval and refinement (Xu 2026); the feedback is generated by metrics, but it has exactly the form a composer’s margin note would take. Not that Groove showed that general LLMs can edit a drum groove in a compact text notation from specific, descriptive and stylistic instructions, verified by expert-authored unit tests that agreed with professionals at 89% TPR and 94% TNR, with the best model passing 68% (Zhang 2025) — test-based verification is the right tool for edit checking. DAWZY converted typed, spoken or hummed requests into reversible REAPER actions through three MCP tools; its 21-participant study rated enjoyment 4.48/5 but control lowest at 3.81, flagging the need for direct-manipulation fallbacks (Elkins et al. 2025). MuMu-LLaMA accepted prompt-based edits alongside any input modality, at the audio level (S. Liu, Hussain, Wu, et al. 2024), and Decomposer’s decompilation of MIDI into editable Strudel programs supplies a programmatic target on which such instructions could operate (S3.6) (Kim, Gandhi, et al. 2026). Two non-AI precedents complete the picture: EuterPen already hosts ink, pictures and audio clips between staves with no reader behind them (Cavez et al. 2025), and REAL, a small agentic video pipeline, contributed the anchoring rule that “the word is the identity, the timestamp is derived” — placements pinned to semantic events, timing re-derived at compile time, every quantity badged estimated or measured (This-Goober 2026). For music the event is the bar, beat or note, and the timestamp follows from the tempo map at compile time.

5.5 S2.5 Economics of human labelling

One targeted human annotation can outperform full automation by a wide margin. Just Label the Repeats aligned real performance audio to scanned sheet music with improved measure detection and onset-probability features, and replaced automatic detection of repeats and jumps with a clickable interface in which the human labels the repeat signs; measure-level alignment accuracy rose from 33% to 82% (Bukey et al. 2024). The same economics recur throughout the sections above: Amuse’s editable keywords (Yewon Kim et al. 2025), Dubler’s per-user trained sounds (Vochlea Music 2021), Sonic Visualiser’s auto-annotate-then-correct layers (Cannam et al. 2010), and AugmentedNet’s 46% full-label accuracy that makes correction essential (Nápoles López et al. 2021). The design rule is to ask the human for the few labels that are cheap for a musician and hard for a model — repeats, section boundaries, key, the phrase a gap should echo — and automate the rest.

5.6 Implications for the studio

The annotation layer should reuse rather than invent. The record shape is already fixed by Dezrann, MEI and Humdrum: a typed label anchored in symbolic time (measure and beat, or a note id), with optional span, staff scope, author, lifetime and free text (Giraud et al. 2018; Pugin et al. 2014; Huron 1995; Fu and Cavez 2026). Harmonic instructions can borrow the DCML regex grammar or RomanText syntax directly (Neuwirth et al. 2018; Gotham et al. 2019); Verovio’s id-addressable SVG makes click-to-annotate on an engraved score feasible, and MEI’s editorial vocabulary can mark AI proposals as variants distinct from the composer’s text (Pugin et al. 2014; Goebl and Weigl 2023). Because MNX is unfinished and MusicXML’s annotation vocabulary is minimal, a studio-internal annotation model with MusicXML 4.0 interchange is justified (W3C Music Notation Community Group 2021). The control-vocabulary literature then supplies the types: instrument, density, polyphony, register, chord, section label, tension, dynamics or rhythm curve, structural reference, example — each declared as a hard constraint enforced at decode time or a soft prior on sampling (Rütte et al. 2023; Chen et al. 2024; Thickstun et al. 2024; Louie et al. 2020). Analysis models should populate this layer proactively but tentatively, at the ~46% harmonic and ~93% phrase-boundary accuracies now achievable, so that the composer corrects rather than authors from nothing (Nápoles López et al. 2021; Dai et al. 2024; Kim and Nam 2023).

The open gap is annotations as prompts on notation. No system combines ExpressEdit’s reference grammar — when, where, what, how — with resolvers for musical modalities and inspectable, editable suggestions (Tilekbay et al. 2024); DirectGPT’s selection-then-instruction pattern has not been applied to a score (Masson et al. 2024); Libretto’s structural feedback loop is driven by metrics, not by a composer’s margin notes (Xu 2026); EuterPen’s multimodal marks have no reader (Cavez et al. 2025); and the (score, annotation, edited score) training triples that InstructPix2Pix’s synthetic recipe could produce do not exist (Brooks et al. 2023). A music annotation compiler that unifies these pieces — anchored to events with derived timing (This-Goober 2026), verified by unit-test-style checks (Zhang 2025) and voice-leading linters (Huang and Chew 2005), and explaining how each mark was interpreted (Tilekbay et al. 2024; Liang and Wang 2024) — is the studio’s most defensible research contribution, and the primitive on which the compile (S3) and edit (S4) stages depend.

6. Compile I: symbolic generation, infilling and arrangement (S3.1–S3.7)

The compile stage is where the studio’s thesis is tested. Given human-authored material — a melody, a chord chart, a few bars of one part — and annotations on it, the system must produce a fuller realisation that the composer can read, audition and change. The symbolic sub-tree of S3 is the natural home for this operation because its outputs are notation or MIDI, which are directly editable, rather than audio, which is not (D7). This section surveys seven nodes: symbolic encodings (S3.1); infilling and constrained regeneration, the primitive that implements “edit → compile” (S3.2); steering by attributes, text and rules (S3.3); structure-aware pipelines that behave like compilers with intermediate representations (S3.4); harmonisation, accompaniment and arrangement, including the commercial arranger engines that are the historical form of “compile” (S3.5); music LLMs and the music-as-code lineage that makes the metaphor literal (S3.6); and expressive performance rendering as the separable stage between score and sound (S3.7). Audio rendering (S3.8), text-to-song (S3.9) and understanding models as critics (S3.10) follow in the next section. Throughout, works are read against task framing (D2), control granularity (D3), editability of the intermediate (D7) and integration surface (D8).

6.1 S3.1 Symbolic representations and tokenizations

Four representational families have emerged since 2016, and the choice among them determines what a composer can later edit. The first is the MIDI-like performance event stream — NOTE_ON, NOTE_OFF, TIME_SHIFT, VELOCITY — over which Music Transformer generated minute-long piano continuations using a relative self-attention whose memory grows linearly rather than quadratically with length; it has no bars or beats, models what a pianist played rather than what a score says, and produced pieces roughly four times longer than the preceding recurrent models at state-of-the-art likelihood on Piano-e-Competition data (Huang, Vaswani, et al. 2019). The second family imposes a metrical grid. REMI added explicit Bar, Position (sixteen per bar), Tempo, Chord (60 types), Note-On, Velocity (32 bins) and Duration tokens so that a Transformer-XL trained on transcribed pop piano “knows” where beats fall, and objective beat-salience metrics and a listening study found better rhythmic and harmonic coherence than MIDI-like tokens (Huang and Yang 2020). Compound Word Transformer grouped the tokens of one time step into a typed “compound word” with a note family (pitch, duration, velocity) and a metric family (bar/position, tempo, chord), each predicted by its own head, converging five to ten times faster than REMI and fitting a full pop song of roughly ten thousand REMI tokens into a linear Transformer (Hsiao et al. 2021). OctupleMIDI packed eight fields (bar, position, instrument, pitch, duration, velocity, time signature, tempo) into one token per note for MusicBERT’s bar-masked pre-training on more than a million songs (Zeng et al. 2021; Chou et al. 2024); as understanding models these belong to the annotation stage (S2.2). The Multitrack Music Transformer’s six-tuple per note yielded 2.6–3.5 times more music per token than MMM or REMI+ across 64 instruments (Dong et al. 2023), while MMM serialised each track’s bars as their own sequence and concatenated tracks so that one bar of one track could be masked and regenerated (Ens and Pasquier 2020). Aria, trained on the 1.19-million-file Aria-MIDI corpus, used absolute onsets within five-second segments instead of cumulative time-shifts to avoid drift in long performances (Bradshaw and Colton 2025; Bradshaw et al. 2025).

The third family is text. folk-rnn trained a character-level LSTM on roughly 23,000 Irish session tunes in ABC and generated complete tunes as text that rendered directly to notation (Sturm et al. 2016), founding the lineage every “music LLM” now follows. ABC is a compact plain-text format with header fields for metre, unit length and key and a body of note letters with pitch and duration modifiers; abc2midi compiles it to MIDI, abcm2ps to engraving, and abcjs re-renders it in the browser on every keystroke (Walshaw 2011). ChatMusician treated ABC as “a second language” for a 7B LLaMA model (Yuan et al. 2024), and MuPT’s Synchronized Multi-Track ABC interleaves bars of the same index across voices with <|> delimiters so multi-part alignment survives tokenisation; in MuPT’s controlled comparison ABC outperformed MIDI-style tokens as an LLM target (Qu et al. 2024). The fourth family is image-like: the piano roll used by Coconet’s convolutional chorale model (Huang et al. 2017), Polyffusion’s eight-bar diffusion segments (Min et al. 2023), Whole-Song’s cascaded diffusion (Z. Wang et al. 2024) and GETMusic’s GETScore, which stacks tracks vertically with pitch and duration rows per track (Lv et al. 2023). Piano rolls make “select a region and regenerate it” a literal spatial mask, which is why they dominate diffusion-based infilling.

Le et al. mapped these trade-offs along time-slice versus event-based and elementary versus composite tokens, and discussed the data-scale disparity between text and music and the limits of the music-as-language analogy (Le et al. 2025); Ji et al. classified by representation, algorithm and task (Ji et al. 2023). MidiTok implements REMI, REMI+, MIDI-Like, TSD, Structured, CPWord, Octuple, MuMIDI, MMM and PerTok behind one MIT-licensed API with sub-word training, and reported that byte-pair encoding speeds up and improves symbolic models (Fradet et al. 2021); symusic, its C++20 I/O backend, parses MIDI and ABC “hundreds of times faster” than the legacy Python stack (Liao et al. 2024) (see B2).

One finding deserves emphasis because it is an absence: no verified system generates MusicXML or MEI natively. Generation lives in MIDI tokens, ABC or piano rolls, and even MetaScore, the largest score-origin dataset (963,000 MuseScore files with genre, composer, complexity and instrumentation metadata and LLM-generated captions), converts scores out to REMI+ and MMT-style tokens before its generators see them (Xu et al. 2025). MusicXML 4.0 remains the interchange standard for more than 250 applications while its JSON successor MNX was still unsettled in mid-2026 (W3C Music Notation Community Group 2021). A lossless bridge between an LLM-friendly textual intermediate and engraving-grade notation is therefore both a prerequisite for a notation-first studio and an open research problem (Part D, gap 2).

6.2 S3.2 Infilling and constrained regeneration

Infilling — mask a region at note, bar, track or span granularity, hold everything else fixed and regenerate the mask — turns a generative model into an editing tool and is the primitive the studio’s loop depends on (D2). Its modern formulation is Coconet, an orderless NADE over a four-voice piano roll trained to predict arbitrary masked notes and sampled with blocked Gibbs “rewriting”, so that any subset of a score can be held fixed and the rest inpainted. Huang et al. motivated the design by observing that “human composers write music in a nonlinear fashion, scribbling motifs here and there, often revisiting choices previously made,” and found that Gibbs rewriting beat ancestral sampling on likelihood and human evaluation because iterative revision corrects poorly modelled conditionals (Huang et al. 2017). Re-implemented in TensorFlow.js at about 400 KB, the same model powered the Bach Doodle, which harmonised user-drawn melodies for more than 55 million queries in three days and yielded a CC BY 4.0 dataset of 21.6 million harmonisations (Huang, Hawthorne, et al. 2019). DeepBach applied pseudo-Gibbs resampling to per-voice bidirectional LSTMs and — uniquely for its era — shipped as a MuseScore plugin in which a composer selected a region and requested re-harmonisation; in a discrimination test with roughly 1,270 participants its outputs were judged “Bach” nearly as often as real chorales (Hadjeres et al. 2017). Infilling Piano Performances reportedly extended hole-filling to the expressive performance-event domain (Ippolito et al. 2018); InpaintNet reframed a monophonic gap as a path through VAE latent space (Pati et al. 2019); and Music SketchNet factorised pitch and rhythm so a user could sketch a partial contour or rhythm for the missing measures and have both honoured (Chen et al. 2020) (see S1.3).

Multi-track, bar-scoped infilling begins with MMM’s BarFill variant, which let a user choose which tracks and bars to regenerate, each track’s instrument and a density level (Ens and Pasquier 2020). Chang et al. adapted XLNet’s permutation language model to fill gaps of variable length (up to 128 notes) in polyphonic piano, with a relative-bar positional encoding so the fill lands on the right beat (Chang et al. 2021). MusIAC combined track-level tokens (note density, polyphony rate, occupation rate) with bar-level tokens for tonal tension — tensile strain and cloud diameter from the spiral array — and found that these improved perceived coherence over density alone; tension is the first composer-meaningful bar-level handle beyond density (Guo et al. 2022).

The Anticipatory Music Transformer supplies the most general mechanism. Anticipation interleaves control events — a fixed melody, or any subset of notes the user wants preserved — into the event stream slightly before the moment they occur, so an ordinary autoregressive model learns to condition asynchronously on them. Because the controls are a subset of the notes themselves, one model trained on Lakh MIDI performs prompted continuation, span infilling and accompaniment given a fixed part; Thickstun et al. reported likelihoods comparable to plain autoregressive baselines and human ratings of twenty-second accompaniments comparable in musicality to human-composed music, and released 128M- to 780M-parameter checkpoints under Apache 2.0 (Thickstun et al. 2024). MIDInfinite ported the model to the browser through MLC and WebGPU at 51 notes per second on an M3 MacBook Pro, running faster than playback 72.9 percent of the time (86.3 percent with two seconds of buffering), which shows that an infilling model of this class can run client-side (Zhou et al. 2024).

Two lines carry infilling into the DAW (D8). Composer’s Assistant is a T5-style encoder–decoder (d = 384, ten plus ten layers) wired into REAPER: the composer creates empty MIDI items on named tracks to indicate which track-measures to fill. Version 1 offered per-track mono/poly tokens and implicit chord or rhythm conditioning through guide tracks, evaluated with 25 participants (Malandro 2023); version 2 added two rhythmic-conditioning modes, horizontal and vertical note-onset density, pitch controls and a “rhythmic interest” control, and found no significant listener-rated difference between fully human excerpts and excerpts co-completed with the tool. It was trained only on permissively licensed MIDI and released under CC BY 4.0 (Malandro 2024). The Metacreation Lab’s lineage ran from MMM through Calliope, a web app that batch-generates up to a thousand candidates for selected bars and tracks (Tchemeube et al. 2022), to MMM-C, a one-parameter Cubase plugin whose 18 expert composers rated ease-of-control at 5.2/10 and their desire for more control at 9.5/10 (Tchemeube et al. 2023), and to MMM4Live for Ableton (Metacreation Lab (Pasquier, Philippe and Ens, Jeff and Tchemeube, Renaud Bougueng) 2023). MIDI-GPT consolidated this into a roughly 20-million-parameter, six-layer Transformer with a 2,048-token context that infills at bar and track level for all 128 GM instruments, conditioned on instrument, ten instrument-relative density levels, polyphony range, a five-bin duration range and a MusicMap style ontology; trained on GigaMIDI, it ships inside Steinberg Cubase, an Ableton Live plugin, the Teenage Engineering OP-Z, Elias game-audio middleware and Calliope (Pasquier et al. 2025). MIDI-RWKV addressed offline inference and personalisation together: a compact linear-time RWKV-7 model for long-context multi-track infilling on edge devices, with state tuning (fine-tuning only the initial recurrent state) for style adaptation from a handful of a composer’s own files, released under CC BY 4.0 and framed around refining existing work (Zhou-Zheng and Pasquier 2025).

Diffusion models make masks spatial. Polyffusion separated internal control (an inpainting mask over an eight-bar piano roll — keep the melody and fill the accompaniment, or fill arbitrary regions) from external control (cross-attention on chord or texture embeddings); 36 listeners rated it significantly above Transformer baselines and it produced zero invalid notes in 160 samples (Min et al. 2023). GETMusic’s discrete diffusion denoises masked target tracks given intact source tracks, so any of the 665 source→target combinations over six tracks, and zero-shot infilling of arbitrary cells, work from one model (Lv et al. 2023). RefinPaint closed the loop from the other side: a feedback model identifies which notes are weakest in context (“where to modify”) and an inpainting model resamples them (“how”), iterated as proofreading; 15 annotators preferred it at 50, 30 and 10 percent fragment sizes and four amateur composers reported improved drafts (Ramoneda et al. 2024) (see S4.2, S4.4).

Two systems bracket the history of deployed infilling. FlowComposer (2016) was a web lead-sheet editor in which the user chose a style corpus from more than 12,000 lead sheets, entered a partial melody or chords, and had Markov chains with metrical constraints fill the rest; any region could be regenerated alone, with sliders for harmonic conformance, “inspiration” from the current piece versus the corpus, note duration and chord-change rate. Used on Benoît Carré’s “Daddy’s Car” and the musical Beyond the Fence, it produced the observation that composers rarely generated more than eight bars at a time, using the tool for fragments inside longer human compositions (Papadopoulos et al. 2016). Hookpad Aria (2024) is its modern counterpart: a copilot inside Hooktheory’s web lead-sheet editor, built on the anticipatory approach and trained on the crowd-annotated Hooktheory corpus, offering continuation, infilling of spans in the middle of existing material, and harmony-from-melody and melody-from-harmony. From its March 2024 beta it generated an acceptance rate of about 23 percent (the figures are given under E3) — the only acceptance telemetry for a notation-surface generative tool found in this review (Donahue et al. 2024) (see E3).

6.3 S3.3 Attribute-, text- and rule-conditioned symbolic generation

Where infilling controls what stays, conditioning controls what changes. The earliest handle was the latent space: MusicVAE’s hierarchical “conductor” decoder emitted one embedding per bar before decoding each bar, avoiding posterior collapse for sixteen-bar melodies, drums and trios, and exposed sampling, interpolation between clips and attribute vectors as user operations (Roberts, Engel, et al. 2018). Compositional Steering reportedly combined several lightweight attribute controls into a product-of-experts distribution over a frozen Music Transformer at sampling time (Young et al. 2021). Music FaderNets disentangled low-level faders (rhythm and note density) in a VAE and learned the high-level attribute arousal semi-supervised from one percent of labels, enabling arousal transfer on video-game MIDI (Tan and Herremans 2020). MuseMorphose encoded each bar of an existing pop-piano piece and decoded it under bar-level rhythmic-intensity and polyphony settings (eight levels each), rewriting a whole song’s texture while preserving its content and beating recurrent baselines on fidelity, control accuracy and diversity (Wu and Yang 2023).

A second line makes the condition legible. FIGARO’s “description-to-sequence” model conditions a Transformer emitting REMI+ on a per-bar description of expert features (instruments, chords, time signature, note density, mean pitch, velocity and duration) plus learned VQ-VAE codes; a composer can edit the description — change bar 9’s chords, add an instrument, raise density — and regenerate, and swapping learned codes yields style transfer (Rütte et al. 2023). MuseCoco split text-to-music into text-to-attribute understanding over about twelve attributes (instrument, rhythm, bar count, time signature, key, tempo, pitch range, emotion, genre) and attribute-to-music generation with a 1.2-billion-parameter decoder, so users can bypass text and set attributes directly; it reported roughly 20 percent higher control accuracy than baselines (Lu et al. 2023). Text2midi paired a frozen FLAN-T5 encoder with an 18-layer decoder (272M parameters, 159M trainable) fine-tuned on the 168,000-pair MidiCaps; it beat MuseCoco on tempo (65.8 vs 54.6 percent) and key accuracy (35.6 vs 14.6 percent) but eleven listeners rated it below ground truth and it struggled with instrumentation within a 2,048-token context (Bhandari et al. 2025). MetaScore’s tag-conditioned generator offered categorical control as an alternative to captions, rated comparable to Text2midi by 22 participants (Xu et al. 2025). Diff-Symbo, an autoregressive latent-diffusion model for long text-conditioned multi-track music trained with 19,345 LLM-generated templates, reported gains over GPT-4, MuseCoco and MMT but appeared in August 2026 without peer review (Lin et al. 2026). MIDI-LLM expanded Llama-3.2-1B’s vocabulary with about 55,000 MIDI tokens in the anticipatory encoding, trained in two stages (continued pre-training on music text and MIDI, then supervised fine-tuning on pairs) while preserving language ability and vLLM-compatible inference, reported a FAD of 0.173 against 0.818 for Text2midi with four- to fourteen-fold faster inference, and in an in-the-wild study with 58 musicians and roughly 4,000 generations found higher acceptance for lead-sheet generation (S.-L. Wu, Carlton, et al. 2026). The recurring lesson is that text should be compiled to an explicit, inspectable attribute layer the composer can override — an argument against opaque text-to-song that the vocabulary in S2.3 makes concrete.

A third line adds constraints to a frozen generator without retraining — the right mechanism when the set of annotations will grow. Stochastic Control Guidance steers a pre-trained latent diffusion model toward non-differentiable rules — pitch-class histogram, horizontal and vertical density, chord progression — by forward-evaluating the rule on candidates at each step; it improved adherence and quality over classifier guidance and supports editing an existing piece within a chosen time window (Huang et al. 2024). LC-Diff kept a frozen unconditional VAE and trained small conditional diffusion priors on its latent space as plug-in constraints over note density, pitch range, melodic contour and rhythmic complexity for monophonic melodies (10 million sequences from 176,000 files), with Pearson correlations above 0.8 between requested and realised values for every attribute (Pettenò et al. 2025). Across three generations — product-of-experts, latent priors, rule-guided diffusion — a new annotation type becomes a function rather than a retraining run.

6.4 S3.4 Structure-aware and hierarchical pipelines

Long-range form is where flat sequence models fail and where a compiler with intermediate representations is most needed. Music Transformer’s relative attention was motivated by structure — relative position lets the model recognise a motif transposed or displaced — and its samples showed repeated motifs and ABA-like form over a minute (Huang, Vaswani, et al. 2019). Museformer built a musical prior into attention: each bar attends finely to “structure-related” bars one, two, four, eight, twelve, sixteen, twenty-four and thirty-two bars back, chosen from bar-similarity statistics, and coarsely to summaries of the rest, modelling sequences more than three times longer than full attention with repetition curves closer to human music and higher ratings for long-term structure, though with no infilling or controls (Yu et al. 2022).

Dai and Dannenberg’s line makes structure explicit and editable. MusicFrameworks decomposed melody composition into section and phrase structure, a “basic melody” of one note per beat, a rhythm structure and a realised melody, with a network per stage, so that a user could alter chords, basic melody or rhythm and regenerate downstream; listeners preferred the results to POP909’s human melodies about half the time (Dai et al. 2021). “What is missing in deep music generation?” established four principles from Chinese and American pop corpora — hierarchical structure, song-specific repetition with a limited vocabulary, interaction between structure and rhythm, melody and harmony, and non-random repetition trends measurable by cross-entropy — and found that recent deep generators “often reveal striking differences from a structural perspective” (Dai, Yu, et al. 2022). Their analyser reached 93 percent phrase-boundary accuracy on 909 pop transcriptions and quantified priors a compile-time checker could enforce: repeated phrases covered 50–90 percent of most songs and V–I cadences closed 94 percent of sections against 47 percent elsewhere (Dai et al. 2024). Personalised imitation analysed a user-chosen reference first, then generated a new song constrained to its structural template (Dai, Ma, et al. 2022).

Whole-Song Hierarchical Generation is the most literal compiler in the corpus: four cascaded piano-roll diffusion models generate a pop song top-down — form and key, a reduced lead sheet, a lead sheet, and accompaniment — each conditioned on the level above and each an interpretable object the human can edit before the next level is compiled, with external encoders steering chords, rhythm and texture; a human study rated its full-song structure above flat baselines (Z. Wang et al. 2024). Melody Infilling with User-Provided Structural Context showed the idea at the scale of a gap: an attention-selecting module lets the composer say which earlier phrase the missing segment should echo, and structure-conditioned fills beat structure-agnostic ones (Tan et al. 2022). SymPAC trained a multi-track model solely on about one million audio tracks auto-transcribed for beats, chords, sections and instruments, controlled through prompt bars — a leading block of genre, section labels, tempo, chords and track list the user may fill fully or partially — and finite-state-machine constrained decoding that guarantees the output obeys both the grammar and the user’s constraints; twelve MIR researchers and producers rated it significantly above FIGARO and MMT on coherence, arrangement and structure (Chen et al. 2024). Hard constraints at decode time are what a composer expects when writing “Cmaj7 here”. Libretto made structure the medium of an agent loop: a text grammar with explicit onset slots, voice declarations and bar blocks, plus a 29-axis “structural fingerprint” calibrated to corpus percentiles, lets an LLM generate, measure, receive musician-readable feedback (“reduce harmonic instability”) and revise; gap-filling pass rates rose from 12 percent single-shot to 39 percent with the loop, and full-piece generation reached 94 percent with retrieval and refinement (Xu 2026).

6.5 S3.5 Harmonization, accompaniment and arrangement

The lead sheet is the natural “source code” of popular music, and the operations that realise it — harmonise a melody, write a melody over chords, produce a piano texture, orchestrate for a band — are the compile steps a songwriter most wants automated and least wants to lose control of. Chordripple, Huang’s earliest system, recommended next or replacement chords ranked by a learned chord embedding so suggestions were plausible yet “beyond the ordinary”, leaving authorship with the novice; the notes record an IUI 2016 study without re-verified details (Huang et al. 2016). Music Transformer’s melody-to-accompaniment mode was the earliest instance in this corpus of a neural “compile a lead sheet into a piano part” (Huang, Vaswani, et al. 2019).

Gus Xia’s Music X Lab built the most complete pipeline. AccoMontage turned a lead sheet into a full-length piano accompaniment by retrieving accompaniment phrases with dynamic programming over fitness and transition smoothness, then re-harmonising them to the target chords through a chord–texture disentangled VAE (Zhao and Xia 2021); AccoMontage2 added melody harmonisation and a GUI for chord style (Pop, R&B, Dark), texture density and rhythmic complexity, and 72 participants rated coherence and structure significantly above learning-only baselines (p < 0.05), musicality marginally (p = 0.053) (Yi et al. 2022). Retrieving human-made material and adapting it to the composer’s chords is compositionally transparent, and the phrase database could be the composer’s own past work. Q&A learned content from the mixture and style from individual tracks and re-rendered a piece for a user-chosen set of tracks — re-instrumentation, piano cover, piano-to-band orchestration, voice separation (Zhao et al. 2023). Structured Multi-Track Accompaniment Arrangement combined the two: piano arrangement by texture retrieval, then orchestration by infusing per-track function styles as vector-quantised codes whose long-term flow a multi-stream Transformer prior generates, yielding whole-song coherence with genre choice and hierarchical control — the closest existing system to an arranger workstation with an editable intermediate piano stage (J. Zhao et al. 2024).

Several works treat harmony as constraint satisfaction. Kaliakatsos-Papakostas et al. harmonised melodies from 17,476 Hooktheory lead sheets with BART and GPT-2 models accepting structure tokens and user-specified chords at given positions, and proposed B* decoding (beam search with A* and backtracking) to guarantee the requested chords appear; soft constraints were satisfied 50–63 percent of the time, B* 90–98 percent at roughly 400–1,900 model calls (Kaliakatsos-Papakostas et al. 2025). MelodyT5 unified seven melody-centric score-to-score tasks — generation, harmonisation, melodisation, segmentation, variation and others — in one ABC encoder–decoder pre-trained on MelodyHub’s 261,000 melodies, with multi-task transfer helping low-data tasks (S. Wu et al. 2024). Function Alignment joined two pretrained symbolic language models (a RoFormer trained on 357,000 Los Angeles MIDI files), one reading a reference and one writing a target, through a lightweight adapter, so chord recognition, metrical analysis, chord-conditioned melody, melody-conditioned chords and drums-to-song became one parameter-efficient “music-for-music” operation (Jiang et al. 2025). GETMusic’s 665 track combinations belong here too (Lv et al. 2023). On the audio side, Stemphonic generated a variable set of synchronised stems in one pass, conditioned on existing sub-mixes and per-stem activity patterns specifying when each instrument plays — arrangement-as-compile over audio (S.-L. Wu, Zhu, et al. 2026) (see S3.8).

The commercial arranger engines are the baselines and define what “compile from chords” has meant for three decades (see F3). The Korg i3 (1993) established the pro-arranger category with 48 styles, each with variations, fills, intros and endings, driven by left-hand chord recognition into drums, bass and accompaniment parts, plus a backing sequencer that captured the result as an editable song (Korg Inc. 1993). Band-in-a-Box has since 1990 turned chord symbols typed into a bar grid plus a style into a complete arrangement, from MIDI styles or from RealTracks — recordings of session musicians exceeding 4,400 hours in the 2026 release — with a notation view and DAW export (PG Music Inc. 2026). Logic Pro 11 (May 2024) brought the idea into a mainstream DAW: Session Players — Drummer plus a new Bass Player (eight players with complexity and intensity controls) and Keyboard Player (four styles) — follow the Chord Track and are editable as ordinary MIDI regions, with no published evaluation (Apple Inc. 2024). All three render parts from chords, style, key and tempo deterministically and editably; none accepts a sketch, a hummed line or an example recording, and none round-trips cleanly to notation. That gap is the studio’s opening, and the arranger’s virtues — instant, deterministic, parameterised, editable — are the bar a learned successor must clear.

6.6 S3.6 Music LLMs and music-as-code

The compile metaphor has a literal history in computer music into which the music LLMs fit. LilyPond compiles a text source into engraved PDF, SVG and MIDI under classical engraving rules, extensible in Scheme and GPL-licensed; its pain points — edit–compile latency and error messages phrased in terms of source rather than score — are exactly what a live, projectional studio should fix (Nienhuys and Nieuwenhuizen 2003). The ABC toolchain is the lightweight equivalent (Walshaw 2011). Nyquist made scores into programs with temporal combinators such as sim and seq, and became Audacity’s scripting layer (Dannenberg 1997). The audio languages offer two compile philosophies — Csound’s separation of an orchestra (instruments) from a score (events), and the live re-evaluation of SuperCollider and ChucK, whose “strongly timed” design lets code be replaced while sound continues — while Faust’s compilation of one DSL to C++, LLVM, WebAssembly and plugins shows that a single musical IR can target many outputs (Wang et al. 2015). Live coding is the most developed practice of music as continuously recompiled code: TidalCycles’ mini-notation, Strudel’s browser port with inline visualisations of what each pattern produced, Sonic Pi’s live_loop, and Gibber’s annotation of running code with live values (McLean 2014; Aaron et al. 2016). The computer-aided-composition environments are composers’ own compile tools: OpenMusic’s visual Lisp patches compute structures displayed in editable notation and piano-roll editors on a timeline “maquette” (OM 8.0 appeared in March 2026), and bach brings the same into Max in real time with bach.roll and bach.score objects editable by mouse or by patching (Assayag et al. 1999). Their dual editing — edit the program or edit the output — is the central unanswered UX question for the studio: which edits round-trip? Programming-languages research adds three pillars: Hazel’s semantics for incomplete programs, in which evaluation proceeds around typed holes so every editor state yields feedback; MPS’s projectional editing of one syntax tree in several notations; and Tanimoto’s liveness levels (Omar et al. 2019). A score with a [to be composed] region should likewise still compile and play. music21 (BSD-3) is the conversion and analysis backbone between MusicXML, MEI, kern, ABC, MIDI and RomanText (Cuthbert and Ariza 2010), and Nierhaus’ catalogue of Markov, grammar, evolutionary and cellular paradigms records what a rule-based backend can do without machine learning (Nierhaus 2009) (see F1).

The music LLMs write these textual scores directly. ChatMusician continually pre-trained LLaMA-2-7B on the 4-billion-token MusicPile to read and write ABC, conditioning on chords, motifs, form, style and text; it beat GPT-4 on the authors’ composition tasks, yet on its own MusicTheoryBench all LLMs including GPT-4 were reported near chance on reasoning (a figure recalled rather than re-verified; see E6) (Yuan et al. 2024). MuPT scaled ABC pre-training to 190M–4.23B-parameter decoders over 33.6 billion tokens with an 8,192-token context and proposed a symbolic-music scaling law under data repetition (Qu et al. 2024). NotaGen applied the full LLM recipe to classical scores — pre-training on 1.6 million pieces, fine-tuning on about 9,000 high-quality works, then reinforcement with CLaMP-DPO, which uses the CLaMP 2 music–text model as an automatic judge without human labels — under a period–composer–instrumentation prompt; A/B tests favoured it over baselines and in some comparisons over human pieces (Y. Wang et al. 2025). MelodyT5 is the score-to-score counterpart (S. Wu et al. 2024). Kumar et al. compared off-the-shelf LLMs with SFT- and DPO-adapted variants and a music-specialised baseline on ABC generation and understanding, finding that adaptation choices materially change outcomes and that standard metrics behave idiosyncratically for music (Kumar et al. 2026). The benchmarks are sobering: ZIQI-Eval’s 14,000-plus questions found all sixteen tested LLMs “poor” (J. Li et al. 2024), and ABC-Eval’s 1,086 items found frontier models above 90 percent on ABC syntax but near random on sequence-level understanding (Zhao et al. 2025) (see E6). Zero-shot natural-language editing was reliable only in a narrow domain: Not that Groove had eight LLMs edit drum grooves in a compact text notation from specific, descriptive and stylistic instructions, verified by expert-authored unit tests that agreed with professional musicians (89 percent true-positive, 94 percent true-negative), and the best model passed 68 percent (Zhang 2025). Test-based verification of edits is the transferable lesson.

Agentic architectures route around these limits. COSMIC (2021) reportedly parsed natural-language requests into intents routed to melody and lyric generators (Zhang et al. 2021); MusicAgent had ChatGPT decompose requests into sub-tasks executed by tools from Hugging Face, GitHub and web APIs, without a human-editable intermediate (Yu et al. 2023). ComposerX assigned six GPT-4 agents the roles of leader, melody, harmony, instrument, reviewer and arrangement to write polyphonic ABC from a brief with a review loop, reporting 98.2 percent generation success, a 0.77 preference for multi-agent over single-agent, and 32.2 percent of outputs judged human-composed against 55.4 percent for real pieces, with rater counts unreported (Q. Deng et al. 2024). Libretto’s measure-and-revise loop is the structure-aware member of the family (Xu 2026).

Decomposer runs the metaphor in reverse and is, for this project, the most consequential recent result. Kim et al. post-trained Qwen3-8B to convert MIDI into executable Strudel programs that re-render the input while exposing repeated patterns, voices, harmony and timing as editable code. Stage one was supervised fine-tuning on STRUDEL-SYNTH, 21,174 (Strudel, MIDI) pairs distilled from Claude Opus 4.6 — about thirty times the 688 public Strudel programs. Stage two was reinforcement learning with execution-based rewards computed from the input MIDI and the program alone, so unpaired MIDI could be used: a faithfulness reward (render and compare to the input) and a readability reward (penalise note-by-note transcription). The model compiled 99 percent of the time against 75–82 percent for frontier LLMs, reached onset F1 of 0.60 on Lakh against 0.27–0.28 for GPT-5.5, Claude Opus 4.6 and Gemini 3.5 Flash (0.58 against 0.21–0.23 on multi-instrument inputs), scored 0.61–0.74 on a readability rubric against 0.09 for a heuristic converter and 0.29–0.36 for frontier models, and generalised to GigaMIDI, Nottingham and NES-MDB; ablations exposed a faithfulness–readability trade-off and showed that RL without SFT plateaued. There was no user study (Kim, Gandhi, et al. 2026). Decomposer supplies a candidate intermediate representation in which human edits are semantic — change a loop, a transposition, a voice — and compilation is deterministic rendering; a forward model from program plus annotations to new program would close the loop.

6.7 S3.7 Expressive performance rendering

Between a finished score and sound lies a stage the studio can keep separate and editable: rendering notation into expressive timing, dynamics and articulation. VirtuosoNet took a MusicXML score with its dynamics, articulation and tempo markings and produced performance MIDI (tempo, velocity, onset deviation, articulation, pedal) through a hierarchical attention score encoder, a conditional-VAE style latent and a measure-to-note decoder, trained on 226 pieces and 1,052 Yamaha e-Competition performances aligned to scores; five piano students rated three pieces on seven criteria with significant gains over prior systems (Jeong et al. 2019). It established score markings as inputs to a learnable rendering stage. ScorePerformer introduced SPMuple tokens (eight score and four performance fields), an MMD-VAE Transformer with style embeddings at global, bar, beat and onset level, and direction-marking classifiers mapping words such as crescendo, staccato and ritardando to latent deltas, so a user can steer rendering with score-like directions at any granularity, trained on ASAP’s 212 pieces and 937 performances (Borovik and Viro 2023). Its successor PianoFlow applied flow matching to 2,968 hours of aligned score–performance MIDI with emotion and free-text controls at 355 notes per second; 26 listeners gave 708 pairwise ratings with a 67.1 percent win rate, preferring it over human recordings in 54 percent of comparisons (Borovik et al. 2025). DExter modelled performance parameters with diffusion conditioned on the score and on perceptual mid-level features, enabling steering and style transfer between performers (H. Zhang et al. 2024); RenderBox rendered score plus text (“play it tenderly, slightly rushed”) directly to multi-instrument audio with curriculum learning from synthesis to expressive performance (H. Zhang, Maezawa, et al. 2025) (see S3.8). Infilling Piano Performances showed that hole-filling applies to expressive data as well as notes (Ippolito et al. 2018).

MIDI-DDSP is the clearest published instance of a compiler with intermediate representations. Its hierarchy — notes to per-note expression parameters (volume, vibrato, brightness, attack) to DDSP synthesis parameters to audio — lets a user intervene at any level, writing a note, dragging an expression curve or editing a synthesis parameter while learned priors fill the rest; listening tests on URMP instruments rated its realism close to real recordings, and the expression controls were verified independently (Wu et al. 2022). The expression layer is an annotation surface between notation and sound. Underneath sits partitura, an Apache-2.0 library that parses MusicXML, MEI, kern and MIDI into a structured score, aligns scores to performances through Match files, extracts performance features and underlies the Basis Mixer renderer, the ACCompanion score follower and ChordGNN, still released in 2026 (Cancino-Chacón et al. 2022). For a studio in which the composer plays a passage on a stage keyboard to show how a phrase should feel, score–performance alignment is what turns that playing into an annotation.

6.8 Implications for the studio

The work in S3.1–S3.7 shows that the compile step need not be invented; it must be assembled from primitives that exist in mature form and connected to a representation none of them yet shares. Infilling at bar-by-track granularity with everything else as a hard constraint is available in small, deployable models — MIDI-GPT at 20 million parameters inside Cubase, MIDI-RWKV on edge devices with state tuning to a composer’s style, the Anticipatory Music Transformer running client-side — and anticipation in particular offers a single formulation of “everything I wrote is a constraint; fill the rest” that Hookpad Aria validated with a 23 percent acceptance rate on a notation surface (Thickstun et al. 2024; Pasquier et al. 2025; Zhou-Zheng and Pasquier 2025; Donahue et al. 2024). The control vocabulary these systems accept — instrument, horizontal and vertical density, polyphony, pitch and duration range, chord per beat, section label, tension, style — has converged across FIGARO, MusIAC, Composer’s Assistant 2, MIDI-GPT and SymPAC and is exactly what a composer would annotate; it should be adopted as the studio’s annotation schema rather than reinvented (see S2.3). Two further lessons are architectural. Hierarchy works: Whole-Song’s four editable levels, MusicFrameworks’ skeleton-to-surface cascade and Music X Lab’s lead sheet → piano texture → orchestral function pipeline show that a compiler whose intermediates are themselves musical objects gives the composer a place to intervene at every stage and yields the long-range structure Dai and Dannenberg showed flat generators lack (Z. Wang et al. 2024; Dai et al. 2021; J. Zhao et al. 2024; Dai, Yu, et al. 2022). And constraints should be hard where the composer means them to be — SymPAC’s constrained decoding and the B* harmoniser satisfy chord constraints 90–98 percent of the time where soft conditioning manages 50–63 percent — while steering should be retrofittable, as Stochastic Control Guidance and LC-Diff show, so a new annotation type becomes a function rather than a retraining run (Chen et al. 2024; Kaliakatsos-Papakostas et al. 2025; Huang et al. 2024; Pettenò et al. 2025).

The music-as-code lineage settles what “compile” means. LilyPond, Nyquist, Csound, OpenMusic and the live-coding languages establish that a composition can be a program whose rendering is deterministic and whose edits are semantic, and Hazel’s typed holes and MPS’s projectional editing describe how an incomplete score should still play and how one musical model should be viewable as notation, piano roll, chord chart and text at once (Nienhuys and Nieuwenhuizen 2003; Assayag et al. 1999; McLean 2014; Omar et al. 2019). Decomposer shows that a learned model can produce such a program from MIDI with 99 percent compile success and far higher faithfulness than frontier LLMs, which makes a programmatic intermediate (D7) a realistic design choice (Kim, Gandhi, et al. 2026). The LLM evidence cuts both ways: specialised ABC models compose well, while ZIQI-Eval, ABC-Eval and MusicTheoryBench show that general LLMs cannot yet be trusted to reason about notation and Not that Groove shows natural-language editing is reliable only in narrow domains under test-based verification (Y. Wang et al. 2025; J. Li et al. 2024; Zhao et al. 2025; Zhang 2025). The studio should therefore use an LLM as an orchestrator that compiles annotations into constraints for specialised symbolic models and verifies the result, in the manner of ComposerX’s reviewer and Libretto’s measure-and-revise loop, rather than as the composer’s direct interlocutor over a score (Q. Deng et al. 2024; Xu 2026). Expressive rendering should remain a separate, annotatable stage in the MIDI-DDSP and ScorePerformer mould, with partitura-style alignment turning the composer’s own playing into an expression annotation (Wu et al. 2022; Borovik and Viro 2023; Cancino-Chacón et al. 2022).

The gaps are as instructive as the achievements. No verified system generates MusicXML or MEI natively, and MetaScore converts scores out rather than in, so a lossless bridge between an LLM-friendly textual IR and engraving-grade notation is the first engineering task and an open research problem (Xu et al. 2025). Notation-editor integration is nearly absent — DeepBach’s 2017 MuseScore plugin and Hookpad Aria are the only examples found, against a rich DAW side — and MMM-C’s composers rated their desire for more control at 9.5 out of 10, which suggests that in-DAW integration is necessary but not sufficient and that the annotation layer is the missing channel (Hadjeres et al. 2017; Tchemeube et al. 2023). The commercial arrangers show what a compile engine must match — instant, deterministic, parameterised, editable — and what it must add: sketches, hummed lines and example audio as conditions, and clean round-tripping to notation (PG Music Inc. 2026; Apple Inc. 2024; Korg Inc. 1993). Almost every system here stops at offline metrics or a small listening study; only FlowComposer, the Bach Doodle and Hookpad Aria report deployment behaviour, and FlowComposer’s finding that professionals regenerate no more than eight bars at a time remains the strongest design prior for how the loop will be used (Papadopoulos et al. 2016; Huang, Hawthorne, et al. 2019; Donahue et al. 2024). Finally, the data behind the strongest models — Aria-MIDI under CC BY-NC-SA, MuseScore-derived corpora, Hooktheory — is often non-commercial or legally fraught, whereas Composer’s Assistant shows that a useful infilling model can be trained on permissively licensed MIDI alone; an open-source compile engine must be built on that footing from the start (Bradshaw and Colton 2025; Malandro 2024) (see B4, D10).

7. Compile II: audio rendering and audio-domain generation (S3.8–S3.10)

The symbolic compile stages surveyed in S3.1–S3.7 end with an editable score or an expressive MIDI performance; a composer still needs to hear it, increasingly as a produced arrangement rather than a General MIDI mock-up. This section covers the audio-domain literature the studio must both borrow from and define itself against, and it is organised deliberately out of taxonomic order. S3.9, text-to-music and full-song generation, comes first because it is the paradigm the studio inverts and because the 2023–2026 trajectory of its commercial and open-weights systems is itself evidence about what musicians want. S3.8 isolates the thinner line in which an audio model accepts symbolic conditions (chords, melody, MIDI, time-varying curves) or renders notation through interpretable synthesis, which is the studio’s actual rendering requirement. S3.10 asks whether music-understanding models can serve as compile-time critics. Works are coded throughout on domain (D1), control granularity (D3), editability of the intermediate representation (D7) and openness of weights and training data (D10).

7.1 S3.9 Text-to-music and full-song audio generation (the paradigm to invert)

Foundations (2020–2024). Jukebox compressed 44.1 kHz audio through a three-level VQ-VAE and modelled the top level with autoregressive Transformers of up to 5B parameters, producing minutes-long songs with singing conditioned on artist, genre and unaligned lyrics; it offered no symbolic conditioning, no editing beyond primed continuation, and needed hours of compute per minute of audio (Dhariwal et al. 2020; Castellon et al. 2021). The 2023 wave replaced raw-audio autoregression with neural codecs and latent diffusion. MusicLM cascaded a MuLan music–text embedding, semantic and acoustic tokens to generate 24 kHz music from text, released MusicCaps (5.5k expert-captioned 10-s clips) as the de-facto benchmark and, notably, could transform a hummed or whistled melody into the described style (Agostinelli et al. 2023); Noise2Music introduced LLM pseudo-labelling of training captions (Huang et al. 2023). Meta’s MusicGen collapsed the cascade into a single-stage Transformer over interleaved EnCodec streams (300M–3.3B, 32 kHz, ~30 s) conditioned on text and a chromagram of a reference melody; its MIT code and CC-BY-NC 4.0 weights made it the most-extended open backbone, underlying Coco-Mulla, Instruct-MusicGen, MusicGen-Style and MusicGen-Stem (Copet et al. 2023; Lin et al. 2023; Y. Zhang et al. 2025; Rouard et al. 2024, 2025). MAGNeT showed that non-autoregressive masked generation over the same tokens ran roughly 7× faster, and its re-mask-and-redecode procedure is inherently local-editable (Ziv et al. 2024).

The diffusion branch supplied the editing primitives. AudioLDM ran latent diffusion in a VAE-compressed mel space conditioned on CLAP embeddings and demonstrated zero-shot inpainting, style transfer and super-resolution without task-specific training (Liu et al. 2023; Wu et al. 2023); AudioLDM 2 added a “language of audio” intermediate representation into which any conditioning modality is translated before rendering, a pattern for one renderer serving many condition types (H. Liu et al. 2024). Stable Audio 2.0 generated 4 min 45 s stereo tracks with explicit timing conditioning (start time and total length) and audio-to-audio initialisation from a user’s clip, but its weights, trained on licensed AudioSparx data, were never released (Evans et al. 2024). Its open sibling Stable Audio Open (≈1.2B parameters, up to 47 s) was trained only on Creative Commons audio from Freesound and the Free Music Archive (≈486k recordings) under the Stability AI Community License, making it the cleanest-provenance base for later controllable adapters (Evans et al. 2025; Tsai et al. 2025; Hou et al. 2025; Flores García et al. 2025). JEN-1 and MusicFlow each showed that generation, inpainting and continuation can be one model, the former by mixing bidirectional and causal training, the latter by masked-prediction training of cascaded flow-matching networks; neither released weights (Li et al. 2023; Prajwal et al. 2024). Riffusion, a 2022 project that fine-tuned Stable Diffusion on spectrogram images, matters as a case study: it became the conversational, multi-turn ProducerAI in 2025, was acquired by Google in February 2026 and re-emerged in April 2026 as Google Flow Music on Lyria 3 with section-level “replace and extend” (Forsgren and Martiros 2022).

Commercial full-song platforms and their drift toward editing. These systems are the archetype the studio rejects: one prompt yields a finished mix, the human is prompt author and curator, and the intermediate representation is opaque audio (D7). Their feature histories nevertheless record sustained user pressure toward region- and part-level control. Suno’s model line ran from v3 (February 2024, 2-min clips) through v4 and v4.5 to v5 (September 2025) and v5.5 (March 2026, with user-trainable “Custom Models”) (Suno Inc. 2026). Around the generator it accreted Audio Input (June 2024), stem separation (July 2024, later 12-stem export), Covers (keep melody, change style), Replace Section (regenerate a time range, October 2024), Personas, Remaster and Add Vocals/Add Instrumental; in September 2025 it launched Suno Studio, a browser “generative audio workstation” with a multitrack timeline and per-section regeneration, and in August 2026 Studio 2.0 added MIDI import, recording and editing with a wavetable synthesizer (Suno Inc. 2026). The generator itself remained prompt- and audio-driven; MIDI lives inside the DAW, not as a generation condition. The Warner Music Group settlement of 25 November 2025 committed Suno to licensed models in 2026, deprecation of existing models, artist opt-in, a non-downloadable free tier and monthly download caps, reported to have taken effect on 3 September 2026 at 7 lifetime downloads on Free, 20 a month on Pro and 60 on Premier — figures documented in Suno’s subscription help pages rather than its release notes, and so carried here as reported rather than verified (Music Business Worldwide 2025b; Suno Inc. 2026). Udio, launched in April 2024, generated ~30-s segments extended forwards and backwards into songs and carried the earliest mainstream instance recorded in this corpus of audio inpainting of a user-selected region (Universal Music Group and Udio 2025). After settling with Universal on 29 October 2025 it disabled downloads and announced a 2026 subscription “walled garden” in which creations remain inside the platform, fingerprinted and filtered (Music Business Worldwide 2025a; Universal Music Group and Udio 2025). Both trajectories show the same two facts: users demanded region editing, and licensed audio generation arrived bundled with constrained ownership of the output.

Google’s line is the most studio-like of the closed systems. Lyria powered the Music AI Sandbox, co-designed with musicians in the YouTube Music AI Incubator, whose verbs are Create (text plus optional lyrics on a timeline with key and tempo), Extend and Edit (transform a whole clip or a selected region, fill gaps, blend transitions); multitrack views and inpainting arrived in October 2024 and Lyria 2 with 48 kHz stereo output in April 2025, all outputs SynthID-watermarked and trained, Google states, on music it and YouTube “have the right to use” (Google DeepMind 2025b; Google DeepMind and YouTube 2025). Lyria RealTime, exposed through the Gemini API in mid-2025, is a block-autoregressive streaming model steered by weighted text prompts plus BPM, key, density and brightness with a reported control latency of at most 2 s; its PromptDJ-MIDI demo maps physical MIDI controllers to those parameters, not to notes (Google DeepMind 2025a). Lyria 3 (February 2026) and Lyria 3.5 (mid-2026, reportedly 3-minute songs) power the Gemini app and Flow Music (Google DeepMind 2025a; Forsgren and Martiros 2022). ElevenLabs Music (August 2025) shipped lyric editing throughout a track, regeneration of a verse, chorus or bridge, 2–6-stem export and API inpainting, positioned as trained on licensed data (ElevenLabs 2025). Mureka reportedly marketed its O1 model in March 2025 as a “chain-of-thought” music model that plans structure before generating; these claims rest on vendor material (Kunlun Tech 2025; Kunlun Tech / Skywork AI 2025). Adobe’s Project Music GenAI Control (February 2024, with UC San Diego and CMU) framed the problem as “generate, then edit with musical parameters” (tempo, structure, intensity, reference melody, section remix) but never shipped; it productised Music ControlNet and DITTO (S3.8) (Adobe Research 2024; S.-L. Wu et al. 2024; Novack et al. 2024). Stable Audio 2.5 (September 2025) generated 3-minute multi-part tracks in under 2 s with API inpainting and audio-to-audio; its adversarial relativistic-contrastive (ARC) post-training also yielded the open-weights Stable Audio Open Small at roughly 75 ms for 12 s of stereo on an H100 (Novack et al. 2025). None of these platforms publishes model details, benchmarks or user studies; the evidence level (D9) is product claims.

Open-weights song models. Between January 2025 and early 2026 permissively licensed lyrics-to-song models made the commercial paradigm reproducible on consumer hardware. YuE (released January 2025) is a LLaMA-2-based two-stage LM (7B semantic, 1B acoustic) generating up to 5 min of multilingual song with track-decoupled prediction that interleaves vocal and accompaniment tokens and in-context style transfer from a 30-s audio prompt; it is Apache-2.0 but heavy (24 GB VRAM minimum, ~360 s per 30 s on an RTX 4090) (Yuan et al. 2025). DiffRhythm (ACL 2025) is a non-autoregressive latent DiT producing 4 min 45 s songs in about 10 s from timestamped lyrics and a text or audio style prompt, Apache-2.0 on 8 GB of VRAM, with extension and editing added in v1.2 (Ning et al. 2025). SongGen (ICML 2025) is a 1.3B single-stage autoregressive Transformer with a dual-track mode emitting separate vocal and accompaniment streams, trained on a fully open ~2k h pipeline, Apache-2.0, limited to 30-s English songs (Z. Liu et al. 2025). HeartMuLa (December 2025–February 2026) bundles a 3B open music LM with a 12.5 Hz codec, a lyric transcriber and a CLAP model under Apache-2.0, generating songs of up to 240 s at a real-time factor near 1; its claimed parity with Suno is self-reported (HeartMuLa Team 2026).

ACE-Step deserves the closest reading because it is the strongest open candidate for the studio’s arranger and renderer. ACE-Step 1.0 (ACE Studio and StepFun, May 2025) is a flow model over Sana’s deep-compression autoencoder latents with a linear Transformer, aligned through REPA to MERT features; it generated up to 4 min of music with vocals in about 20 s on an A100, reportedly some 15× faster than LM-based systems, and shipped a menu of editing operations rather than one-shot generation: variations, repainting (local regeneration of a time range), lyric editing that keeps melody and accompaniment, lyric-to-vocal, singing-to-accompaniment, voice cloning and remixing through LoRA controllers (Gong et al. 2025; Y. Li et al. 2024). ACE-Step 1.5 (technical report January–February 2026; 4B XL release April 2026) restructured the system as a hybrid LM planner plus diffusion Transformer: a 0.6B–4B language model converts the request into a “song blueprint” (metadata, lyrics, captions, via chain-of-thought) that conditions a DiT decoder; intrinsic RL alignment removes the external reward model; an 8-step turbo variant renders a full song in under 2 s on an A100 and under 10 s on an RTX 3090; memory ranges from under 4–6 GB to 24 GB or more; backends include CUDA, ROCm, Apple MLX and CPU; and it ships a REST and Python API and a VST3 plugin (Gong et al. 2026). New capabilities include cover generation, repainting, vocal-to-accompaniment, track separation, multi-track layering, lyric-structure control, BPM/key/time-signature control, over 50 languages and LoRA fine-tuning from a few songs. The licence is MIT. Two gaps matter: there is no MIDI or chord input to the generator, and no training-data statement, so provenance is unknown (D10). Its planner is in effect a compiler front-end from intent to blueprint; a notation-first studio would replace that planner with the composer’s score and annotations and retain the decoder.

Stem-level “listen and respond” generation. A distinct strand frames generation not as producing a song but as adding a part that fits what is already there, the unit of co-creation closest to arranging. SingSong source-separated a large corpus into (vocal, instrumental) pairs and trained AudioLM-style conditional generation to accompany a new a-cappella input; listeners preferred its accompaniments to retrieval baselines in a reported ~66% of pairwise comparisons (Donahue et al. 2023). StemGen (ICASSP 2024) is a non-autoregressive masked Transformer that takes an existing multi-stem context and generates one new stem of a requested category, evaluated by key, tempo and chroma coherence with the context; no weights were released (Parker et al. 2024). Multi-Source Diffusion Models (ICLR 2024 oral) learned the joint score of bass, drums, guitar and piano on Slakh2100 so that a single model performs generation, separation and source imputation, filling in missing stems given the others (Mariani et al. 2024; Manilow et al. 2019). Diff-A-Riff (Sony CSL, ISMIR 2024) generated a single 48 kHz accompaniment track to fit a given mix, steerable by a CLAP audio reference and/or text and designed for DAW workflows (Nistal et al. 2024). MusicGen-Stem trained one codec per stem (bass, drums, other) so that one stem can be regenerated while the others are held fixed (Rouard et al. 2025), and Instruct-MusicGen added fusion modules to frozen MusicGen (about 8% extra parameters, 5k steps) so that it follows “add, remove or separate a stem” instructions, matching task-specific models on Slakh and MoisesDB (Y. Zhang et al. 2025). Stemphonic, which generates a variable set of synchronised stems in one pass under per-stem activity patterns, is the arrangement-oriented endpoint of this line (S3.5) (S.-L. Wu, Zhu, et al. 2026). In D2 terms these systems occupy the response/accompaniment framing, in which the human’s material is a hard constraint, which is why they rather than the full-song generators are the natural audio analogue of the studio’s compile step.

7.2 S3.8 Audio rendering and symbolic-conditioned audio generation

The studio’s rendering requirement is narrower and harder than text-to-music: given notation, chords or MIDI, produce audio that honours them, with enough control to re-render one region after an edit. Only a handful of models accept structured musical input. JASCO (Hebrew University and Meta, ISMIR 2024) is a flow-matching text-to-music model that takes time-aligned chord progressions and a melody salience matrix as symbolic controls, plus a separated drum track and a full-mix embedding as audio controls, through information-bottleneck layers with temporal blurring; a human study found quality comparable to baselines with “significantly better and more versatile controls” (Tal et al. 2024). It is the clearest open example of lead-sheet-style conditioning of an audio model, but it produces roughly 10-s clips at 32 kHz under CC-BY-NC weights. Coco-Mulla fine-tuned under 4% of MusicGen’s parameters on fewer than 300 songs to accept chord progressions, drum MIDI and pitch content alongside text, a cheap recipe for teaching an existing audio LM to read a composer’s chords (Lin et al. 2023). Mustango (NAACL 2024) injected chords, beats, key and tempo parsed from the prompt through a music-domain-informed UNet module and released MusicBench (52k music–text pairs with MIR-extracted theory captions), improving chord, beat, key and tempo accuracy over MusicGen and AudioLDM 2 (Melechovsky, Guo, et al. 2024).

A second family conditions on time-varying curves, which maps directly onto what a composer might draw over a passage. Music ControlNet (CMU and Adobe, TASLP 2024) attached ControlNet-style adapters to a spectrogram diffusion model for melody (chroma), dynamics (loudness) and rhythm (beat and downbeat) curves; crucially, controls may be partially specified in time, pinning melody for bars 1–4 and leaving the rest free, and the model was 49% more faithful to input melodies than MusicGen with 35× fewer parameters and 11× less training data, though no code was released (S.-L. Wu et al. 2024). MuseControlLite (ICML 2025) reproduced these controls as 85M-parameter decoupled cross-attention adapters on Stable Audio Open, using rotary positional embeddings for time-varying conditions (melody accuracy 56.6% to 61.1%), and added native audio inpainting and outpainting with 6.75× fewer trainable parameters than a Stable Audio Open ControlNet; open and CC-trained, it is arguably the best starting point for a studio prototype’s audio stage (Tsai et al. 2025; Evans et al. 2025). The Melody+Text ControlNet for Stable Audio’s DiT reportedly used a top-k constant-Q melody representation with curriculum learning to balance melody against text (Hou et al. 2025). Sketch2Sound, which extracts loudness, brightness and pitch curves from a vocal imitation with a tunable looseness, belongs to the same family and is treated under S1.2 (Flores García et al. 2025).

DITTO reframes all of these as one mechanism. Rather than training adapters, it optimises the initial noise latents of a frozen text-to-music diffusion model through any differentiable feature-matching loss, obtaining inpainting, outpainting, looping, intensity-curve, melody and musical-structure (self-similarity) control from one model and outperforming training-based baselines including Music ControlNet; DITTO-2 distilled the model and optimised through a one-step surrogate for a 10–20× speed-up, faster than real time, and added targets such as maximising CLAP text adherence (Novack et al. 2024; Wu et al. 2023). For the studio this is the general “annotation → constraint” mechanism: any composer annotation with a computable target (a hummed contour, a dynamics sketch, a form diagram) becomes a loss on a frozen renderer without retraining per annotation type. Text2FX applies the same logic to interpretable production parameters, optimising differentiable EQ and reverb so that the processed audio’s CLAP embedding matches a prompt (S4.3) (Chu et al. 2025).

The most composer-aligned rendering lineage remains DDSP. DDSP made synthesizer and effect modules differentiable so that networks output interpretable parameters (fundamental frequency, loudness, harmonic distribution) instead of waveforms, enabling independent pitch and loudness control and timbre transfer with small models (Engel et al. 2020). MIDI-DDSP (ICLR 2022) built a three-level hierarchy on it, notes → per-note performance parameters (vibrato, dynamics, articulation, brightness) → synthesis parameters → audio, for monophonic URMP instruments under Apache-2.0; a user may write notes, edit an expression curve or adjust synthesis directly, or let learned priors fill in, the clearest published instance of a compiler with editable intermediate representations (D7 multi-level editable) (Wu et al. 2022). DDSP-VST packaged the approach as MIDI-playable neural synthesizer and re-timbring effect plugins with a trainer that builds a personal timbre from “a couple minutes of audio,” and Tone Transfer put humming-to-instrument in the browser (S1.2) (Magenta team 2022; Magenta and Google AIUX teams 2020). Its limitation is monophony. MusicHiFi, reportedly a cascade of three GANs for vocoding, bandwidth extension and mono-to-stereo upmixing, addresses a back-end concern: many controllable models output 16–32 kHz mono, and a modular finishing stage can lift them to 44.1 kHz stereo (Zhu et al. 2024).

Finally, real-time MIDI-steerable audio arrived in 2026. Magenta RealTime 2 (June 2026) moved the open “live music model” from 2-s chunks to 40-ms frames with roughly 200 ms control latency on Apple Silicon (230M and 2.4B models) and accepts MIDI notes (Auto-Strum and precise-onset modes) alongside text and audio style prompts; it was trained on about 71k hours of mostly instrumental stock music paired with MT3-inferred MIDI labels, the only open audio model in this corpus whose training pairs audio with transcribed symbolic data (Lyria Team et al. 2025; Gardner et al. 2022). Its short context precludes long-form structure, but it is the first credible open building block for rendering or jamming on the studio’s symbolic material inside a plugin (S5.3).

Table 7.1 condenses the capability matrix for the systems most relevant to rendering, coding each on openness (D10), symbolic conditioning (D3/D4), region editing (D2 infilling), stem behaviour and real-time operation.

Only JASCO, Coco-Mulla, Mustango, Music ControlNet, MuseControlLite, MIDI-DDSP and Magenta RealTime 2 accept structured musical input; no commercial full-song system accepts MIDI or chords as a generation condition.

7.3 S3.10 Music-understanding models as compile-time critics

A compiler that emits audio needs a way to check its output: did the rendering keep the key and tempo, follow the chords, land the annotated dynamics? The music-understanding literature offers encoders, text–audio embeddings and audio-language models, but none is yet reliable enough to be trusted silently. The encoder layer is the most mature. JukeMIR showed in 2021 that Jukebox’s intermediate representations transferred to tagging, genre, key and emotion tasks with linear probes and seeded the Sheet Sage melody transcriber (S1.1) (Castellon et al. 2021; Donahue et al. 2022).
