MERT (ICLR 2024) trained HuBERT-style masked prediction against an RVQ-VAE acoustic teacher and a constant-Q musical teacher for pitch and harmony, at 95M and 330M parameters, reaching state-of-the-art or competitive results across 14 MIR tasks; its CC-BY-SA 4.0 weights became the default backbone for audio-side annotation and are used inside MU-LLaMA, MusiLingo and ACE-Step (Y. Li et al. 2024; S. Liu, Hussain, Sun, et al. 2024; Z. Deng et al. 2024; Gong et al. 2025). MuQ replaced MERT’s teachers with a light Mel residual vector quantiser and beat MERT and MusicFM with only 0.9k hours of pretraining; its companion MuQ-MuLan is an open contrastive music–text model, the missing open analogue of MusicLM’s MuLan (Zhu et al. 2025; Agostinelli et al. 2023). LAION-CLAP, contrastive text–audio encoders trained on LAION-Audio-630K, is the de-facto text conditioner (AudioLDM, Stable Audio Open, Diff-A-Riff) and evaluation metric, and because it defines a shared text–audio space it lets text annotations act as loss functions in DITTO-2 and Text2FX (Wu et al. 2023; Liu et al. 2023; Nistal et al. 2024; Novack et al. 2024; Chu et al. 2025).

The audio-LLM layer, where critique would happen, is weaker. MU-LLaMA (ICASSP 2024) joined MERT to LLaMA through an adapter for music question answering and captioning and introduced MusicQA (S. Liu, Hussain, Sun, et al. 2024). LLark (Spotify, ICML 2024) paired a Jukebox-5B encoder with Llama-2 on instruction data auto-generated from open datasets’ annotations, producing key, tempo and instrumentation facts conversationally with high human agreement on captions; training code was released, weights were not (Gardner et al. 2024). MusiLingo reportedly used a single projection layer between MERT and Vicuna (Z. Deng et al. 2024). SALMONN (ICLR 2024) fused Whisper and BEATs encoders through a Q-Former into Vicuna, handling speech, audio events and music in one model, a candidate front-end for parsing spoken and hummed annotations (Tang et al. 2024). General models such as Qwen2-Audio (7B, Apache-2.0), Qwen2.5-Omni and Gemini 2.5 describe genre, instruments and mood and roughly estimate tempo, and Gemini is what Google pairs with Lyria, but they are not music-specialised; only Qwen2-Audio’s report was verified directly (Chu et al. 2024). OpenMU (Sony) is the one benchmark mixing the modalities the studio cares about: OpenMU-Bench (≈1M examples) covers captioning and reasoning but also lyrics, ABC-notation understanding and tool use, and the OpenMU model outperformed MU-LLaMA on it (M. Zhao et al. 2024).

Two results temper the picture. MuChoMusic (ISMIR 2024) built 1,187 human-validated multiple-choice questions on 644 tracks from MusicCaps and Song Describer, spanning theory, instrumentation, structure and cultural reasoning, and evaluated five open audio LLMs including MU-LLaMA, MusiLingo, SALMONN and Qwen-Audio; the central finding was over-reliance on the language modality, models often answering without using the audio (Weck et al. 2024; Manco et al. 2023). The second is the field’s one user-centred data point. Ronchini et al. (CMMR 2025) had 17 music producers from seven countries use a tool combining MusicGen with HT-Demucs six-stem separation, followed by interviews and thematic analysis; the themes were “creative misalignment” between intent and output, integration pain concentrated on tempo, key and beat alignment, strong demand for control and editability, and a split between ideation (94% would use it) and production (few would) (Ronchini et al. 2025; Copet et al. 2023). The alignment problems named by working producers are precisely the ones symbolic conditioning solves by construction, and the field’s evaluation practice, FAD, CLAP score and small MOS panels, does not measure them (E4) (Kilgour et al. 2019; Chung et al. 2025).

7.4 Implications for the studio

The first lesson is that the paradigm being inverted has been inverting itself. Every commercial platform surveyed began as prompt-to-song and added, under user pressure, stems, section replacement, covers, personas, timelines and finally MIDI editing; Google moved from a Sandbox with region edit and extend to real-time steering to conversational section-level refinement; the open ACE-Step line shipped repaint, lyric-edit, cover and vocal-to-accompaniment operations and a planner that turns intent into a structured blueprint before decoding (Suno Inc. 2026; Google DeepMind 2025b; Forsgren and Martiros 2022; Gong et al. 2026). What none of them did is make notation the source of truth or accept symbolic material as a generation condition; Suno’s MIDI lives inside its DAW, and Udio’s licensed platform keeps the output inside a walled garden (Suno Inc. 2026; Universal Music Group and Udio 2025). The studio’s differentiators are therefore concrete: compile from the composer’s score and annotations, expose editable intermediate representations in the MIDI-DDSP sense, emit portable notation, MIDI and stems, and state the provenance of any audio model it ships (Wu et al. 2022; Evans et al. 2025).

The second lesson is that the rendering stage the studio needs exists only as research-grade pieces, and the pieces can be assembled. Symbolic conditioning of audio is demonstrated by JASCO, Coco-Mulla, Mustango and Music ControlNet; native region inpainting by MuseControlLite, JEN-1, MusicFlow and ACE-Step; part-at-a-time generation by SingSong, MSDM, Diff-A-Riff, MusicGen-Stem and Stemphonic; training-free imposition of arbitrary computable constraints by DITTO; per-note editable synthesis by MIDI-DDSP; and MIDI-steerable real-time rendering by Magenta RealTime 2 (Tal et al. 2024; Lin et al. 2023; Tsai et al. 2025; Mariani et al. 2024; Novack et al. 2024; Wu et al. 2022; Lyria Team et al. 2025). A plausible open stack is a CC-trained base (Stable Audio Open) with MuseControlLite-style adapters for curves, a Coco-Mulla-style adapter for chords and drum MIDI, DITTO for ad-hoc annotation constraints, per-stem regeneration, and an ACE-Step decoder where vocals or full-band production are required, with weight licences checked separately from code licences because MusicGen-family weights are non-commercial. The gaps are equally concrete: no open model renders polyphonic notation with MIDI-DDSP’s level of control; symbolic-conditioned models produce 10–47-s clips; Magenta RealTime 2 has no long-form structure; and no verified model accepts a score, as opposed to chords or a melody curve, as its condition.

The third lesson concerns the critic. MERT, MuQ and CLAP are dependable feature extractors for key, tempo, chord and text-adherence probes, but MuChoMusic’s finding that audio LLMs answer from text rather than sound means their judgments cannot close the loop unsupervised (Y. Li et al. 2024; Zhu et al. 2025; Weck et al. 2024). A symbolic-first studio has an advantage that audio-first products lack: it can verify a rendering against the score it compiled from, using transcription and MIR probes on the output and reserving the LLM for phrasing the discrepancy back to the composer (S4.4). Ronchini et al.’s producers asked for exactly this alignment guarantee, and their study remains the only user evidence in an otherwise metric-driven field, a gap the studio’s own evaluation programme (E1, E4) should fill (Ronchini et al. 2025).

8. Edit: revising and iterating (S4)

Editing is the stage at which the studio’s thesis is either honoured or lost. If the composer can only accept or reject what the machine produced, the loop degenerates into curation whatever the interface promises; if the composer can select a region, say or draw or sing what should change, inspect the machine’s proposal, alter it, and keep a record of what happened, then generation is genuinely subordinate to authorship. This section surveys the general HCI paradigms for machine-mediated editing (S4.1), symbolic transformation, variation and proofreading of music the composer already wrote (S4.2), audio editing and inpainting (S4.3), the neglected reverse channel in which the system explains or previews what it intends (S4.4), and the history, alternatives and branching machinery that makes iteration recoverable (S4.5). Two asymmetries structure it: editing is far better developed in audio than in symbolic music — eleven primary works in S4.3 against two in S4.2, though the symbolic surface is where edits are semantically meaningful — and the human→AI direction far better developed than the AI→human direction, which has two primary entries in total, a finding independently established by Rezwana and Maher (2023).

8.1 S4.1 Editing paradigms from HCI at large

The normative baseline is Shneiderman (1983), whose three properties of direct manipulation — continuous visual representation of the object of interest, physical actions in place of complex syntax, and rapid, incremental, reversible operations whose effect is immediately visible — were formulated explicitly against command languages. Prompt-based music generation is a command language with a probabilistic interpreter: the object of interest is not continuously represented (it exists only as the last rendered output), the actions are syntax rather than gestures, and operations are neither incremental nor reversible. Judged by those criteria the dominant paradigm of 2023–2026 music AI is a regression, and the question is what it takes to recover direct manipulation without losing the expressive reach of language.

DirectGPT is the most precise answer available. Masson et al. (2024) built a direct-manipulation layer over a large language model in which generated objects remain continuously represented and selectable, toolbar operators reuse prompt syntax, outputs can be dragged and selected to compose the next prompt, and every operation is undoable; the system translates manipulation into an engineered prompt rather than asking the user to write one. Across text, code and vector-graphics editing tasks, participants were reported 50% faster, issuing 50% fewer prompts and prompts 72% shorter than with a conversational baseline. The mechanism behind the shortening is the load-bearing insight: when the selection carries the scope and the referent, the utterance need only carry the intent — “make bars 17–24 of the second violin part sparser” collapses to a selection plus “sparser.”

Instruction-conditioned editing supplies the model-side counterpart. Brooks et al. (2023) trained a conditional diffusion model mapping an (image, edit instruction) pair to an edited image in one forward pass, and solved the absent-dataset problem by synthesising training triples — GPT-3 generating instructions and edited captions, Stable Diffusion with prompt-to-prompt generating the before/after image pairs — so no human editing labels were required, establishing instruction editing as a trainable task rather than an inference-time trick. Its transfer to music is a data-generation recipe as much as an architecture: the missing corpus for annotation-driven symbolic editing is a set of (score, annotation, edited score) triples, bootstrappable by applying known transformations in reverse and having a language model describe them.

ExpressEdit shows what the interface around such a model should look like. Its pipeline and study figures are given under S2.4; what matters here is the contract it establishes. Tilekbay et al. (2024) let users describe an edit in language and sketch on the artifact, parsed the request into typed references, resolved each with a modality-appropriate resolver, and returned suggestions the user could accept, reject or adjust, with a panel exposing how each reference had been interpreted. The distribution of outcomes is the lesson: fewer than half of suggestions were accepted as offered, and a clear majority of the edits that survived into the final result were modified suggestions. Most accepted work was therefore neither the machine’s proposal nor the human’s unaided edit but the human’s revision of the proposal — which means the unit of delivery must be an editable draft, not a committed change.

Three older lineages fill out the paradigm. Igarashi et al. (1999) established the sketch-interface contract in 3D modelling: accept an ambiguous gesture, infer a plausible complete object, display it immediately, and let further gestures refine it, with no dialog boxes in the loop. Cypher (1993) collected the programming-by-demonstration systems built on the premise that knowing how to perform a task should suffice to create a program performing it, the user’s role being to correct over- and under-generalisation of the demonstrated example; read musically, an attached reference recording is a demonstration and “voice the chords like this” a generalisation request whose intended aspect the composer must be able to name. The live- and projectional-programming line contributes three further properties: Tanimoto’s liveness levels, describing how immediately edits are reflected in running output; Hazel’s typed holes, which give a dynamic semantics for incomplete programs so that evaluation proceeds around the holes and every editor state yields feedback; and projectional editors such as JetBrains MPS, which edit an abstract syntax tree directly and project it into several synchronised notations at once (Omar et al. 2019). A score with explicit “to be composed” regions that still compiles and plays, re-renders on every annotation, and is editable as notation, piano roll, chord chart or text is the musical reading of those properties, and what makes “compile” more than a metaphor (see S3.6 and Kim, Gandhi, et al. (2026)).

The transferable pattern across these lineages is one contract: select a region of the artefact, express intent in whatever modality is handiest, and receive inspectable, editable suggestions rather than a committed change. Each clause carries weight independently — selection makes utterances short and referents unambiguous, modality freedom lets a composer hum a counter-line rather than describe it, and suggestion-rather-than-commitment preserves the reversibility Shneiderman required while matching ExpressEdit’s evidence that revision is the normal case. Conversational music systems violate all three clauses, and their evaluations register it: Loop Copilot’s eight users rated it usable (SUS 75.3 ± 15.3) and valued it for inspiration but complained of limited musical-attribute control and poor prompt responsiveness, and asked for DAW integration (Zhang et al. 2023).

8.2 S4.2 Symbolic editing, variation and proofreading

The strongest single piece of evidence for the studio’s stance comes from a study of a deliberately modest tool. Krol et al. (2025) built Rhapsody Refiner, which takes a musician’s own MIDI and produces variations by selectively masking notes and re-predicting them with MusicBERT under user-set parameters; it cannot compose from scratch, and that dependence on strong human input was a design commitment, not a limitation. It was evaluated in a four-week ecological study with eight practising musicians — songwriters, producers, educators, instrumentalists — combining tutorials, independent in-the-wild use, journals, logs, interviews and thematic analysis. The findings’ asymmetry is the point. Participants valued the tool for generating “moments, not whole ideas”; because output depended on material they had authored, they reported ownership of both artefact and process; and they explicitly rejected automatic completion, declining to have the system finish their work even when it could. Its imperfections and randomness were reported as productive, prompting creative problem-solving rather than read as errors. The generalisation is narrow but exactly the one the studio needs: transforming authored material preserves creative ownership, whereas supplying unauthored material displaces it — a distinction invisible to any evaluation measuring only output quality, and consistent with Suh et al. (2021)’s warning that a generous generative model can shift collaborators from co-composing to co-producing.

Attribute-conditioned rewriting is the best-developed technical form of “transform what I wrote.” MuseMorphose encodes each bar of an existing pop-piano piece to a latent with a Transformer VAE and decodes it under user-set bar-level attributes — rhythmic intensity and polyphony, eight levels each — rewriting a song’s texture bar by bar while preserving its content; Wu and Yang (2023) reported gains over MusicVAE-style RNN baselines in fidelity, attribute-control accuracy and diversity across full songs. Music FaderNets learned disentangled “faders” for rhythm and note density in a VAE whose Gaussian-mixture latent captured arousal semi-supervised from only 1% labelled data, grounding a high-level intention (“more intense here”) in inspectable low-level features (Tan and Herremans 2020). FIGARO and MuseCoco apply the same manoeuvre at compile rather than edit time (see S3.3) (Rütte et al. 2023; Lu et al. 2023), and the Latent Fourier Transform generalises it to timescale, masking latent frequency bands as an “equalizer for musical patterns” that keeps phrase-level shape while varying surface detail, on qualitative evidence (Wang and Huang 2026).

Natural-language editing of symbolic music has exactly one careful study. Zhang (2025) asked whether general-purpose LLMs can edit an existing drum groove, with no training, from instructions of three kinds — specific (“add a cymbal on beat 1”), descriptive (“reduce hi-hat activity”) and stylistic (“make it funkier”) — representing the groove in a compact textual “drumroll” notation of one line per instrument and one character per sixteenth. Its methodological contribution is the evaluation: in place of ground-truth edited grooves, which do not exist and would be non-unique anyway, it used expert-authored unit tests, which agreed with professional musicians at 89% true-positive and 94% true-negative rates; the best of eight models (gpt-4.1-mini) passed 68%, and reasoning-tuned models helped. Two lessons follow: zero-shot instruction editing is feasible in a narrow, densely quantised domain and should not be assumed to transfer to voiced polyphony, and verification of an edit is a test-satisfaction problem, which fits the compiler framing exactly — an edit request is a specification, and specifications can be checked.

Three further works reach the same primitive from the compile side and are treated at length under S3.2, each contributing one lesson to the editing side. RefinPaint separates where to change from how, pairing a critic that identifies the weakest notes in context with an inpainting model that resamples them, so proofreading becomes iterative rather than wholesale (Ramoneda et al. 2024). Composer’s Assistant 2 marks the fine-grained-control endpoint, letting the composer mark partial track-measures and constrain the refill along roughly ten named musical axes (Malandro 2024). FlowComposer supplies the interaction datum that matters most here: shipped in 2016 with region-level regeneration and conformance sliders, it was observed to serve fragments, composers rarely generating more than eight bars at a time (Papadopoulos et al. 2016). Cococo’s steering tools produced the same behaviour with measured effects on ownership and control (Louie et al. 2020).

The node is nonetheless thin. Only two works surveyed here have symbolic editing as their primary contribution — MuseMorphose and “Not that Groove” — and one covers drum grooves only; everything else is an infilling system repurposed for editing, an attribute-conditioned generator, or a study of a research prototype. There is no symbolic analogue of InstructPix2Pix: no model trained on (score, instruction, edited score) triples, no public corpus of such triples, no benchmark for polyphonic edit fidelity. Given that a composer’s edits are the operations for which the symbolic surface exists, this is the largest gap on the editing side.

8.3 S4.3 Audio editing and inpainting

Audio went the other way, and quickly. The zero-shot branch manipulates a pretrained generator’s conditioning or noise trajectory. MusicMagus edits generated music by translating the text-embedding difference between a source and target word — “piano” to “guitar” — into a latent-space move, with cross-attention constraints preserving unedited attributes; it beat zero-shot and some supervised baselines on style and timbre transfer but was limited on real recordings (Y. Zhang et al. 2024). That limitation motivated inversion: Manor and Michaeli (2024) applied edit-friendly DDPM inversion to AudioLDM2, giving ZETA for text-based editing of real recordings with structure preserved and ZEUS for unsupervised discovery of semantic edit directions by PCA over posterior-mean directions, which yielded musically meaningful axes — adding or removing an instrument’s participation, melodic improvisation — with no text at all. MelodyFlow adapted ReNoise latent inversion to flow matching with regularisation for fast zero-shot editing of existing stereo clips that changes genre and instrumentation while preserving melody and structure, outperforming ReNoise and DDIM inversion on editing consistency (Le Lan et al. 2024). Text2FX is the outlier: rather than regenerate audio, it optimises differentiable audio-effect parameters (EQ, reverb) so the processed signal’s CLAP embedding matches a prompt such as “warmer,” leaving interpretable, further-editable parameters behind (Chu et al. 2025).

The trained branch is stronger. AUDIT was the first instruction-following audio editor, trained on (instruction, input audio, output audio) triplets to add, drop, replace, inpaint and super-resolve, establishing the natural-language-edit-over-a-clip pattern for general audio (Wang et al. 2023). InstructME specialised it to music — add, remove, extract, replace and remix instruments, multi-round — and, importantly for a composer-centred tool, injected a chord-progression matrix as a condition so edits stay harmonically faithful (Han et al. 2024). Instruct-MusicGen reached comparable capability far more cheaply, adding audio- and text-fusion modules to a frozen MusicGen at roughly 8% extra parameters and 5,000 steps to follow “add, remove or separate a stem,” matching task-specific models on Slakh- and MoisesDB-derived benchmarks with open code (Y. Zhang et al. 2025). SongEditor extended editing to full songs with vocals, supporting segment-wise editing (regenerate a section with modified lyrics) and track-wise editing (vocals versus accompaniment) (Yang et al. 2025). MuseControlLite is the most practical open recipe: 85M-parameter decoupled cross-attention adapters with rotary positional embeddings on Stable Audio Open, giving time-varying melody, rhythm and dynamics control plus inpainting and outpainting at 6.75× fewer trainable parameters than a ControlNet baseline, melody accuracy rising from 56.6% to 61.1% with RoPE (Tsai et al. 2025). Adjacent designs make the mask structural rather than temporal: MusicGen-Stem trains one codec per stem so one stem can be regenerated against fixed others (Rouard et al. 2025), MAGNeT’s re-mask-and-redecode is inherently local (Ziv et al. 2024), and melody-conditioned ControlNet branches on Stable Audio’s diffusion Transformer reportedly support “keep my melody, re-render everything else” from top-k constant-Q peaks (Hou et al. 2025).

Industry converged on the same primitive. Adobe Research (2024) framed the problem publicly as generate-then-edit-with-musical-parameters (tempo, structure, intensity, section remix, reference melody) — technically a productisation of Music ControlNet and DITTO, never shipped (S.-L. Wu et al. 2024; Novack et al. 2024). Stable Audio 2.5 exposes inpainting as an API — upload audio, pick a region, generate the fill — with three-minute generation in under two seconds via adversarial relativistic-contrastive post-training, whose open sibling renders twelve seconds of 44.1 kHz stereo in roughly 75 ms on an H100 (Novack et al. 2025). The full-song platforms accreted region editing under user pressure: Suno’s Replace Section, Udio’s region inpainting, ElevenLabs’s verse/chorus regeneration and ACE-Step’s repainting are one operation under four names (see S3.9) (Suno Inc. 2026; Universal Music Group and Udio 2025; ElevenLabs 2025; Gong et al. 2025).

Two conclusions follow. Region editing is table stakes in audio: every serious system of 2025–2026, open or closed, supports selecting a span and regenerating it, and sub-second inference makes doing so interactive. And natively mask-trained models beat post-hoc tricks. Inversion editing is elegant and needs no paired data, but trades quality against fidelity to unedited material, works best on model-generated audio, and offers no control surface beyond the prompt; models trained with masks or instruction triplets (AUDIT, InstructME, Instruct-MusicGen, SongEditor, MuseControlLite) expose an explicit editing interface, preserve context by construction, and — in InstructME’s case — hold the composer’s harmony fixed while doing it. The studio’s audio stage should therefore rest on a mask-trained, control-adapted open renderer, not on inversion of a text-to-music model.

8.4 S4.4 AI→human feedback, explanation and plan preview

Rezwana and Maher (2023) coded 92 existing co-creative systems against the COFI framework and found that the field concentrates on AI generative ability and almost entirely lacks AI→human communication — explanations, statements of intent, expressions of confidence — which they argue is precisely what distinguishes a partner from a tool. Their companion study compared two versions of a co-creative sketching system, one contributing only sketches and one also communicating its intentions and reactions; the communicating version was perceived as more collaborative, engaging, partner-like and reliable, though some found it intrusive (Rezwana and Maher 2022). This taxonomy’s tally corroborates the diagnosis: two primary entries here against eleven for audio editing.

What little exists points in consistent directions. RefinPaint’s feedback model answers where to modify before the inpainter answers how — an AI-produced annotation on the composer’s own score, and the only music system surveyed that marks locations rather than replacing content (Ramoneda et al. 2024). Cococo’s users, given voice lanes, semantic sliders, an example-similarity slider and multiple alternatives, spontaneously developed “debugging” strategies to work out what the model was doing; against a generate-and-accept baseline in a within-subjects study with 21 novices, comprehensibility rose to 5.3 from 3.2 and controllability to 5.9 from 3.5 (Louie et al. 2020). That users invent debugging behaviour in the absence of explanation is an argument for supplying it. The XAIxArts line makes controls legible from the model side by regularising MeasureVAE’s first four latent dimensions onto rhythmic complexity, note range, note density and average interval jump, with visualisations of each (Bryan-Kinns et al. 2021); Scurto et al. (2021) inverted the channel, letting like/dislike feedback steer an agent’s parameter-space exploration.

The plan-preview pattern is the most directly transferable. ReaLJam displayed the accompaniment agent’s upcoming chords in a waterfall view split into a committed window that cannot change and an adaptive window that may; five of six experienced pianists wanted the visible preview, and tunable protocol parameters absorbed the wide variation in their other preferences (Scarlatos et al. 2025). ExpressEdit’s Examine panel is the non-real-time equivalent, showing how each temporal, spatial, operational and parameter reference was resolved, and was rated 6.1 of 7 (Tilekbay et al. 2024). Side Views generalises both as persistent, on-demand previews of what an operation would do before committing (Terry and Mynatt 2002). Constraint validators occupy the same slot from the checking side, as in MultiVerse’s rule-based enforcement of creator-authored lyric constraints (Wang et al. 2026), as does test-based verification of edits (Zhang 2025).

The compiler framing has an unusually natural answer here, which is reason to keep the metaphor. A compiler does not silently emit a binary: it emits diagnostics naming a location, a rule and a reason, and a build log recording what it did with which inputs. The musical analogue is a per-compile log stating which annotation governed which bars, which model or solver handled each span, which alternatives were considered, where the system was least confident, and which constraints it could not satisfy — every line anchored to a score element identifier and therefore clickable. This subsumes RefinPaint’s where-to-modify as a diagnostic, ReaLJam’s committed/tentative split as a preview, ExpressEdit’s Examine as reference resolution, and Cococo’s debugging as an intended workflow rather than a coping strategy. Nothing in the surveyed literature implements it for music.

8.5 S4.5 History, versioning, alternatives and branching

The HCI foundation is the alternatives-and-subjunctive lineage collected by Terry and Mynatt (2002) Side Views’ persistent on-demand previews of possible outcomes, Juxtapose’s parallel authoring of interface variants with linked editing and runtime tuning, and Lunzer and Hornbæk’s subjunctive interfaces for viewing and controlling alternative scenarios side by side — all resting on the claim that near-term experimentation, variation and comparison are core creative needs that premature commitment suppresses, with lab studies reporting more alternatives explored under parallelism. Grossman et al. (2010) attacked provenance instead: Chronicle records a graphical document’s full video and event history so a user can click any region of the final artefact, see the workflow, tools and settings that produced it, and play the sequence back; its study found this useful and easy to use. Luminate updated alternatives for LLMs by having the model first generate a design space’s dimensions and then populate a structured grid users browse, filter and combine; fourteen professional writers found it useful for exploration (Suh et al. 2024).

Music-specific machinery is sparse but real. Foscarin et al. (2019) defined a diff procedure for scores at the graphical-notation level, converting each bar and voice to a tree, combining sequence edit distance over bars with tree edit distance over beaming and tuplet hierarchies, and rendering two Verovio scores side by side with differences highlighted; validated against OMR output, it was motivated by collaborative editing and version control. It is the only research artefact for musical diff found in this review, and without one there is no way to show a composer what a compile changed. On the product side, Flat.io auto-syncs every edit, creates a major version at most every fifty modifications, lets free users restore the ten most recent major versions and paid users scrub every individual change with a video-like slider, marks change locations on a score scrollbar with per-contributor colour coding, and exports any historical state to MusicXML or MIDI (Tutteo Ltd (Flat.io) 2026). Fine-grained, attributable linear notation history is therefore a solved delivery problem; the DAW-project analogues, Splice Studio’s git-like commit history and Blend’s project forking, are both reportedly defunct, suggesting limited pull for file-level versioning of opaque projects. mei-friend takes a third route, pairing an MEI code view with live Verovio rendering, committing encodings to GitHub, and attaching standards-based Web Annotations to score elements by identifier — at once a projectional-editing pattern, a git history for symbolic music and an annotation anchor, on thin published documentation (Goebl and Weigl 2023).

What does not exist anywhere is branch and merge with musical semantics. Score diffs exist, per-edit linear histories exist in products, alternatives interfaces exist in generic creative tools, and generation systems produce candidate sets — Cococo’s alternatives, Calliope’s batch generation of up to a thousand candidates for selected bars (Tchemeube et al. 2022), Magenta Studio’s Interpolate (Roberts et al. 2019) — but no system lets a composer fork a passage into two arrangements, develop both, and merge the horn writing of one into the string writing of the other with conflicts reported musically. Luminate’s dimension-first grid and Chronicle’s click-to-provenance likewise have no music analogue.

Provenance logging is also where the editing loop meets the authorship argument, which raises the stakes above convenience. Micchi et al. (2021) documented more than twenty-five distinct human interventions in one AI-assisted song — structure from a Markov model, chords from an LSTM, lyrics from GPT-2, human filtering of candidates, human-composed verse melodies, human arrangement and production — and argued that deliberate selection and modification of machine material constitutes authorship, releasing the result under CC BY-SA. The U.S. Copyright Office’s 2025 copyrightability report reached a compatible legal position: prompts alone do not confer authorship, but human-authored expressive inputs, creative selection and arrangement, and modifications of AI output are protectable to the extent of the human contribution, case by case (U.S. Copyright Office 2025). A studio that logs every annotation, compile, accepted or rejected suggestion and manual edit against score element identifiers is therefore not merely building an undo stack but generating the evidentiary record on which the composer’s claim rests. Chronicle’s click-to-history, Flat’s contributor colouring, mei-friend’s commits and Foscarin et al.’s diff are its four components, and no system combines them.

8.6 Implications for the studio

The clearest design conclusion is that the edit stage should be a selection-scoped suggestion pipeline, not a chat window and not a regenerate button. The contract recurring across DirectGPT, ExpressEdit, Teddy, programming by demonstration and every successful music system in S4.2 is identical: the composer selects a region of the score, expresses intent in whatever modality is nearest to hand — typed instruction, sketch on the staff, hummed line, reference clip, slider — and receives inspectable, editable proposals with their interpretation exposed, which the composer modifies before accepting. Proposals must therefore land in the editable notation surface, carry the reference resolution that produced them, and be revisable without a second round-trip through the model. FlowComposer’s decade-old observation that composers rarely regenerate more than eight bars, and Rhapsody Refiner’s participants valuing “moments, not whole ideas,” together fix the default edit unit at a phrase — not a section and never a song.

The technical gap the studio is best placed to close is symbolic instruction editing. Audio has mask-trained editors, instruction-tuned editors, inversion editors, region-editing APIs and shipped product features; symbolic music has attribute-conditioned rewriting on piano, infilling repurposed as editing, and one careful study of zero-shot natural-language editing confined to drum grooves. The route through is visible even though no one has taken it: build a (score, annotation, edited score) corpus by InstructPix2Pix’s synthetic-triple recipe, inverting known musical transformations and having a language model describe them; adopt ExpressEdit’s four-part reference grammar as the annotation schema, with temporal references resolving to bars, beats and formal sections, spatial references to staves, voices and registers, operations to a closed vocabulary of musical edits and parameters to sliders and reference examples alike; adopt “Not that Groove”’s unit-test methodology as the verification layer, so an edit request becomes a checkable specification; and keep the compile step deterministic where a program-based intermediate representation allows. Because the studio is symbolic-first, its edits are semantically inspectable in a way audio edits never are, which is what makes testing and diffing possible at all.

The half of the loop no existing system builds is the return channel, and it is the half a compiler makes obvious. Every component of the build log sketched in S4.4 exists somewhere — RefinPaint’s where-to-modify, ReaLJam’s committed-versus-tentative preview, Side Views’ on-demand previews, Chronicle’s click-to-provenance, Foscarin et al.’s score diff so that reviewing an AI edit resembles reviewing a patch — and none has been assembled for music; above them sits the branching layer nobody has built, forked arrangements of one annotated passage compared along explicit musical dimensions as in Luminate and merged with conflicts reported musically. That log is also the authorship record. Between Micchi et al. (2021)’s argument from documented intervention and the Copyright Office’s position that human selection, arrangement and modification are protectable to the extent of the human contribution (U.S. Copyright Office 2025), a studio keeping a complete, inspectable trail of who changed what, when and under which annotation produces not merely a better editing experience but the proof that the composer wrote the piece.

9. Play: real-time co-performance (S5)

The compose–annotate–compile–edit loop is turn-taking: the composer acts, the compiler responds, the composer edits. Play is the branch of the same loop in which the response must arrive within a beat. It matters to this studio for two reasons. The founder is a keyboardist, and an arrangement that can be played with on a Nord Stage over MIDI is a different test of a compiled score than one that can only be auditioned. And the real-time literature is the oldest continuous line of “machines that listen and respond” in music; it has already worked out vocabulary (Rowe’s score-driven versus performance-driven input, instrument versus player paradigm), interaction patterns (anticipation displays, kill-switches, per-user protocol settings) and training recipes (RL against coherence rewards with an offline teacher) that the turn-taking studio can reuse (Rowe 1993; Scarlatos et al. 2025; Yusong Wu et al. 2024). In the taxonomy’s terms Play is D5 simultaneous rather than human-initiated, and it forces latency and future-visibility trade-offs that offline compilation can ignore. This section moves from score following (S5.1) through improvisation partners (S5.2) and RL-tuned or streaming neural models (S5.3) to the design spaces, pedagogy and games that frame the practice (S5.4).

9.1 S5.1 Score following and accompaniment

The score-driven pole of Rowe’s taxonomy was established in a single year by two systems. Dannenberg’s on-line accompaniment algorithm (ICMC 1984) matched a live monophonic performance against a stored score by dynamic programming and drove accompaniment playback from the resulting tempo estimate, demonstrated live with trumpet (Dannenberg 1984). Vercoe’s Synthetic Performer, demonstrated at IRCAM the same year, reportedly tracked a flautist through pitch and fingering sensors and learned the player’s tempo tendencies across rehearsals; it heads an MIT line that runs through Csound’s orchestra-and-score compile model to the present (Vercoe 1984). Dannenberg’s Human-Computer Music Performance programme (2011–2014) generalised from soloist-plus-accompanist to the computer as a performing partner in popular-music ensembles: beat- and section-level synchronisation, human cueing roles and “active scores,” machine-readable, structure-aware scores that can be re-sequenced live with repeats and cues, with the Accomplice keyboard-accompaniment project among its offshoots (Dannenberg et al. 2014). Active scores are an early representation designed for both humans and machines to edit and follow, and thus a direct ancestor of an annotated, compilable score. At MIT, Egozy’s group has reported real-time in-browser score following by online time warping (Caren and Egozy, JAES 2025), and ConcertCue streams time-synchronised programme notes to audience phones cued to musical events, deployed with the Boston Symphony Orchestra and other ensembles (Egozy 2016b, 2018). ConcertCue addresses listeners rather than generators, but its data structure, typed content anchored to score positions and delivered at performance time, is the one the studio’s annotation layer needs (S2.1). The evidence level throughout S5.1 is engineering demonstration and deployment rather than controlled study, and by the taxonomy’s own count it is the thinnest node in Play.

9.2 S5.2 Improvisation partners

The performance-driven, player-paradigm pole has an equally long history. George Lewis’s Voyager, begun in 1987, listens through pitch-to-MIDI and generates multiple independent voices that may respond to, ignore or initiate against the human improviser; Lewis frames it explicitly as not an instrument the human controls but a partner rooted in Afrological improvisation aesthetics, the far pole of the control axis (Lewis 2000). Biles’s GenJam (ICMC 1994) evolved populations of jazz licks over a chord progression with a human “mentor” supplying real-time good/bad judgments as the fitness function, later trading fours with the player; it is remembered for exposing the fitness bottleneck of human-in-the-loop evaluation and is the earliest formalisation of annotation as fitness, the primitive form of the studio’s annotate-then-regenerate loop (Biles 1994). Pachet’s Continuator learned a variable-order Markov model of a player’s MIDI phrases in real time and continued each phrase in the same style when the player stopped, with reported studies by Addessi and Pachet in which this reflexive interaction increased children’s engagement; Flow Machines generalised the idea to Markov constraints, style plus hard constraints on meter, harmony and position, and to lead-sheet generation in FlowComposer (S3.2) (Pachet 2003; Papadopoulos et al. 2016). IRCAM’s OMax and Somax2 practise “stylistic reinjection,” learning a Factor Oracle over the live stream and improvising by recombining the performer’s own material, with Somax2 adding reactive listening to the current harmonic context; the approach needs no external corpus, an ethically clean route to style (Assayag et al. 2006).

Two recent systems show what neural models add. Notochord (AIMC 2022) is an autoregressive model over MIDI sub-events (instrument, pitch, time, velocity) trained on Lakh MIDI with under 10 ms latency; because each sub-event is sampled separately, a performer can fix or constrain any of them, yielding harmonisers, improvisers and likelihood-based interfaces from one MIT-licensed model, the clearest real-time analogue of editing a note in the score and letting the model re-condition (Shepardson and Magnusson 2022). The jam_bot (ISMIR 2025) is a stage system built with Dream Theater keyboardist Jordan Rudess: a Music Transformer fine-tuned on Rudess’s own recorded basslines, chords and melodies, prompted into distinct roles (lead, accompaniment, call-and-response), driven by a low-latency scheduler, with a preview screen of upcoming decisions, restart and kill-switch controls to preserve performer agency, and a kinetic sculpture externalising the model’s state; it premiered in September 2024 after a year of weekly co-design and was evaluated by performance rather than controlled study (Blanchard et al. 2025; Huang, Vaswani, et al. 2019). It demonstrates personalised symbolic models fine-tuned on one artist’s material with explicit role assignment, and repeats the pattern of previewing AI intent before it commits (Huang 2025).

9.3 S5.3 RL-tuned and live neural models

Two earlier neural systems set the terms. Piano Genie (IUI 2019) decoded eight buttons in real time into plausible 88-key piano music through an RNN autoencoder with a discrete bottleneck constrained so that button contour maps to pitch contour; the human supplies rhythm, phrasing and rough contour and the model compiles exact pitches, the real-time cousin of humming or scribbling a line (Donahue et al. 2019). Co-Explorer (TOCHI 2021) used deep reinforcement learning to explore a synthesizer’s parameter space while adapting to the sound designer’s like/dislike feedback, evaluated through observational studies and a workshop with professional sound designers, a concrete design for feedback-as-steering (Scurto et al. 2021).

The ReaLchords line is the most complete recent programme. ReaLchords (ICML 2024) is an online chord-accompaniment model for a live melody: frame-based at sixteenth-note resolution with a 5,041-chord vocabulary, pretrained by maximum likelihood on about 38k Hooktheory melody–chord pairs and RL-fine-tuned with self-supervised reward models scoring harmonic and temporal coherence plus a KL term distilling from an offline teacher that sees the future melody, “forcing anticipation” (Yusong Wu et al. 2024). Note-in-chord ratio rose from 37.0% for the online MLE model to 54.3%; the RL model recovered from a tritone transposition at beat 17 within two to three beats where MLE never did; and ten musicians in 192 pairwise comparisons significantly preferred it (Yusong Wu et al. 2024). ReaLJam (CHI EA 2025) wrapped it in a web interface in which the agent continually predicts how the performance will unfold and shows upcoming chords in a waterfall descending toward the keyboard, committed chords opaque and revisable predictions translucent, with round-trip latency under 100 ms at 150 BPM (Scarlatos et al. 2025). In a study with six experienced musicians over 60-minute sessions, enjoyment averaged 4.3/5 but perceived adaptation only 2.7/5; RL-trained models beat the pretrained one on every measure; five of six wanted visible chord previews; participants valued surprise that still matched expectation and wanted genre control and structure awareness (agents ignored four-bar phrase structure); and preferences for protocol settings varied so strongly by individual that per-user customisation was concluded to be essential (Scarlatos et al. 2025). GAPT (ICLR 2026) diagnosed reward hacking in this recipe, coherence rewards driving collapse to repetitive, low-coverage chords, and added a co-evolving discriminator separating policy trajectories from real data; it traced a Pareto frontier of roughly 85% note-in-chord against Vendi-score diversity, and 12 expert musicians rated it highest on adaptation quality, adaptation speed and perceived control and agency, significantly beating ReaLchords on speed and agency (p < 0.05); the family is now an open PyTorch stack (Y. Wu, Brade, et al. 2026). Streaming Generation for Music Accompaniment (ISMIR 2026) carried the idea into audio: a decoder-only Transformer over DAC tokens at 50 Hz listens to an incoming stem and emits an accompanying stem across 18 General MIDI instrument families from Slakh2100, formalising two design knobs, future visibility (a latency offset from −4 to +4 s) and output chunk size; on 1,024 clips with COCOLA coherence, Beat-F1 and FAD plus blind listening, larger future visibility improved coherence at the cost of required inference speed, and naive maximum-likelihood streaming training proved insufficient without future context (Y. Wu, Wang, et al. 2026; Manilow et al. 2019).

The “live music model” line addresses audio at the ensemble level. Magenta RealTime (June 2025) generates a continuous 48 kHz stereo stream in 2-s chunks with a 10-s context over SpectroStream tokens, steered by weighted blends of MusicCoCa text and audio embeddings, trained on roughly 190k hours of instrumental stock music and released as open weights; Lyria RealTime is the hosted sibling with tempo, density and brightness controls and control latency of at most 2 s, whose PromptDJ-MIDI demo maps physical controllers to those parameters (Lyria Team et al. 2025; Google DeepMind 2025a). Magenta RealTime 2 (June 2026) cut control latency about 15-fold to roughly 200 ms with 40-ms frames and causal sliding-window attention, runs in real time on Apple Silicon (230M and 2.4B models) with a C++ engine, DAW integration and a suite of example applications built on that engine, and accepts MIDI note input, holding a chord makes the ensemble follow the harmony, with a drums toggle (Lyria Team et al. 2025). Live Music Diffusion Models (May 2026) reached similar latency by a different route, repurposing the 340M Stable Audio Open Small into a block-wise streaming generator with KV-caching and “ARC-Forcing” adversarial post-training that curbs error accumulation without RL; it offers text-conditioned streaming, sketch-based synthesis from time-aligned top-k CQT loudness controls extracted from the musician’s live audio, and stem-conditioned jamming with adjustable future visibility (−2 to +2 s), deployed through ONNX and JUCE as a “generative delay” effect on a gaming laptop at about 30 ms latency; three musicians (saxophone, guitar, cello) described it as a musical partner rather than an effect, with acknowledged EDM bias from MTG-Jamendo training (Novack et al. 2026, 2025; Evans et al. 2025). On the symbolic side, MIDInfinite ported the Anticipatory Music Transformer to the browser through WebGPU, generating 51 notes per second on an M3 MacBook Pro and staying ahead of playback 72.9% of the time (86.3% with 2 s of buffering) (Zhou et al. 2024; Thickstun et al. 2024). Aria-Duet placed the 650M Aria piano model in a turn-taking duet on a Disklavier, motivated explicitly by rejecting text prompting as an asynchronous workflow (Bradshaw et al. 2025).

9.4 S5.4 Design spaces, practice and play

The field acquired a systematic map in 2026. A Design Space for Live Music Agents (CHI 2026) reviewed 184 systems (153 papers and 31 videos from 731 screened, 89.8% inter-annotator agreement) into 31 dimensions and 165 codes across four aspects: Usage Context (user and agent roles, topology), Interaction (I/O modality, planning, temporal structure, control mode and scope, system initiative, agency framing as tool, partner or hybrid), Technology (model class, learning, adaptation, latency emphasis, integration) and Ecosystem (authorship, economics, culture); it found a trend from reactive accompanists toward proactive partners, recurring control-versus-novelty and coherence-versus-diversity trade-offs, and that only about 5% of systems discuss ecosystem or policy, and it is published as a living annotated database (Kim, Brade, et al. 2026). Agents in Concert (IUI 2026) is a reflective case study of deploying multiple agents in a real concert with Rudess and the MIT Chamber Chorus, documenting engineering, rehearsal and performer-agency lessons; its full text was not retrieved and its content is inferred from the related jam_bot coverage (Brade et al. 2026; Blanchard et al. 2025). Both sit within MIT’s Music Technology and Computation programme and Huang’s research group, whose stated method is weekly co-design with musicians (MIT News Office 2026; Huang 2025). Behind them lies Rowe’s 1993 framework, still the standard vocabulary: score-driven versus performance-driven input, transformative versus generative versus sequenced response, and the instrument paradigm (extending a performer’s gesture) versus the player paradigm (an artificial musician with its own personality) (Rowe 1993). The systems in S5.1 are score-driven and in the instrument paradigm; Voyager, OMax and jam_bot are performance-driven players; ReaLchords and Magenta RealTime 2 are performance-driven but transformative, following the human’s line.

The practice side is anchored in games and pedagogy. Harmonix, founded in 1995 by Egozy and Rigopulos out of Machover’s Media Lab group, whose director’s stated goal was “making every human being into a musician,” let non-musicians perform music through simplified controllers and a scrolling note highway in Guitar Hero (2005) and Rock Band (2007), reportedly selling more than 35 million units (Egozy and Rigopulos 2005; Machover 1992). Egozy’s own account describes how these games designed for musical expression within game constraints through scoring, freestyle sections and the note-highway abstraction (Egozy 2016b). Kiri Miller’s ethnography argued that such games constitute a form of musical performance and informal learning of rhythm, structure and listening while analysing their limits, and remains the canonical reference for game-based music learning (Miller 2009). Harmonix’s DropMix (2017) and Fuser (2020) reportedly turned arrangement by stem selection into a game mechanic, time-stretching and pitch-shifting stems from different songs to a common key and tempo and scoring drops on downbeats, evidence that casual users assemble coherent arrangements when the system handles alignment (Harmonix Music Systems 2020). The note highway is the direct visual ancestor of ReaLJam’s waterfall and Piano Genie’s contour mapping (Scarlatos et al. 2025; Donahue et al. 2019). Egozy’s MIT course 21M.385 Interactive Music Systems has students build real-time Python/Kivy systems, synthesis, sequencing, MIDI and gesture input, note-highway and rhythm-game interfaces, culminating in a public demo concert, a tested curriculum for teaching composers to build musical software (Egozy 2016a). Two CMU systems extend “generate to fit what is playing” beyond composition: Music-aware Virtual Assistants (UIST 2024) re-sang spoken notifications in harmony with the current music and were rated a better fit, less intrusive and more delightful than standard alerts, and RISE (ISMIR 2025) re-sequenced existing songs in real time to match workout intensity through structure analysis and recurrence-matrix cutpoints, with transitions rated 4.3/5 against 4.5/5 for unmodified music by six raters on 514 clips and preferred by 11 of 12 users (A. Wang et al. 2024, 2025). RISE in particular is non-destructive, structure-aware rearrangement of audio, a Play-time analogue of editing form.

9.5 Implications for the studio

Play is not a separate product but the same compiled material under a latency budget, and the literature specifies that budget. ReaLJam achieved under 100 ms round trip for symbolic chords, Magenta RealTime 2 about 200 ms control latency for audio on a laptop, Live Music Diffusion Models about 30 ms as a plugin, and Streaming Accompaniment made explicit that coherence rises with future visibility while inference must run faster to pay for it (Scarlatos et al. 2025; Lyria Team et al. 2025; Novack et al. 2026; Y. Wu, Wang, et al. 2026). The arrangement the studio compiles should therefore be playable in the two modes Rowe’s taxonomy names: a score-driven instrument mode in which the composer plays or sings against the compiled score and the system follows, the Dannenberg–Vercoe line now available in a browser through online time warping, and a performance-driven player mode in which a ReaLchords-class agent or a Magenta RealTime 2 ensemble responds to what the composer plays on the Nord Stage (Rowe 1993; Dannenberg 1984; Egozy 2016b; Yusong Wu et al. 2024). The ReaLchords recipe, MLE pretraining on lead-sheet data, RL against coherence rewards, distillation from an offline teacher that sees the future, and GAPT’s discriminator against collapse, is open in PyTorch and its chord-symbol output plugs directly into a lead-sheet workflow; jam_bot shows that the same class of model can be fine-tuned on one composer’s own material and assigned roles (Y. Wu, Brade, et al. 2026; Blanchard et al. 2025).

The interaction lessons transfer beyond real time. Every successful system in this section externalises the agent’s intent before it commits, ReaLJam’s committed-versus-tentative waterfall, jam_bot’s preview screen and kill-switch, Notochord’s per-sub-event constraints, and the studies show why: adaptation was rated only 2.7/5 even when enjoyment was 4.3/5, five of six musicians wanted to see upcoming chords, and perceived control and agency were the measures on which GAPT most clearly won (Scarlatos et al. 2025; Blanchard et al. 2025; Shepardson and Magnusson 2022; Y. Wu, Brade, et al. 2026). This is the AI→human communication direction that COFI found almost absent across co-creative systems (S4.4), and the studio’s compile step should adopt the same visual grammar for previewing what it is about to change (Rezwana and Maher 2023). ReaLJam’s finding that protocol preferences varied strongly by individual and did not track objective ratings argues for per-composer settings rather than a single tuned agent, and the Design Space’s trade-offs, control versus novelty and coherence versus diversity, are the same axes the offline compiler must expose as annotations (Kim, Brade, et al. 2026).

The gaps are visible in the node counts. Score following is the thinnest node in Play, with no recent system that follows a compiled, annotated score and re-synchronises an AI ensemble to a composer’s rubato, even though active scores anticipated exactly that representation (Dannenberg et al. 2014). The RL-tuned agents produce chord symbols rather than arrangements, ignore four-bar phrase structure and lack genre control; the live audio models have no long-form structure and, in the open case, no vocals; and the evidence base is small (N = 6, 12 and 3 interactive participants across the three interactive studies, plus a ten-musician listening panel for ReaLchords) and drawn almost entirely from expert improvisers (Scarlatos et al. 2025; Y. Wu, Brade, et al. 2026; Novack et al. 2026; Yusong Wu et al. 2024). Only about 5% of live-agent systems discuss authorship or economics, which matters for a studio whose Play mode will generate material a composer may later keep (Kim, Brade, et al. 2026). The games lineage suggests the remedy on the practice side: Harmonix, Miller and Fuser show that system-handled alignment and clear anticipatory visuals let non-experts perform and arrange with agency, which is the population the studio’s Play mode should be evaluated with next (Egozy and Rigopulos 2005; Miller 2009; Harmonix Music Systems 2020).

10. Evidence and evaluation (E)

Every design claim made so far in this review — that infilling preserves ownership, that annotations beat prompts, that composers want to stay in notation — is either an empirical claim or an aesthetic preference dressed as one. This section collects the evidence that lets the distinction be drawn: controlled studies of co-creative music systems (E1), field studies of practitioners (E2), deployed systems with published usage data (E3), metrics for generated music (E4), creativity-evaluation frameworks (E5), and benchmarks probing whether models know any music theory (E6); it closes with a concrete evaluation protocol. The organising finding is a mismatch. The generative-model literature is measured almost entirely with distributional audio metrics that correlate weakly with human judgement, while the interaction literature — which measures what the studio actually claims to improve — consists of a few dozen studies with median N in the low teens, one four-week field study, and one deployed system with telemetry.

10.1 E1 Controlled studies of co-creative music systems

The most load-bearing study here is Cococo (Louie et al. 2020), because it isolates the interface from the model. Louie, Coenen, Huang, Terry and Cai built a four-voice editor over Coconet (Huang et al. 2017) with four AI-steering tools — voice lanes restricting generation to chosen voices and time spans, semantic sliders (conventional↔︎surprising, happy↔︎sad), an example-based similarity slider, and multiple alternatives for audition — all implemented as soft priors on the sampling distribution rather than by retraining. A within-subjects study with twenty-one novice composers (two 15-minute pieces, one per interface), preceded by a formative study with eleven, compared Cococo against a conventional generate-and-accept infilling interface. On seven-point scales Cococo scored higher on controllability (5.9 vs. 3.5), comprehensibility (5.3 vs. 3.2), self-efficacy (5.9 vs. 3.7), sense of collaboration (5.9 vs. 4.0), creative expression (5.5 vs. 3.8) and ownership (5.2 vs. 3.8), all at p<0.01. Participants built pieces bit-by-bit, voice by voice, in generate–audition–edit loops rather than accepting whole outputs, and developed explicit strategies for debugging the model. The authors’ conclusion — that generative capability “may need to be partitioned into smaller, semantically meaningful tools” — is the closest thing in this literature to a specification for an annotation layer.

Expressive Communication (Louie et al. 2022) asked whether interface work survives model improvement. Its 2×2 design crossed model (PerformanceRNN vs. Music Transformer (Huang, Vaswani, et al. 2019)) with interface (a “Radio” condition curating ten randomly generated whole phrases vs. a “Steering” condition permitting chunk-by-chunk composition with semantic filters), in a task requiring composers to communicate a given card’s feeling. Twenty-six composers aged 24–60 produced over 100 pieces, and twenty listeners made 1,020 head-to-head comparisons. Both factors mattered and neither dominated: the better model produced pieces that better evoked the target feeling (p<0.001) and sounded more musical (p<1e-5), while the steering interface also improved evocation and decisively raised ownership (4.8 vs. 2.42) and control (5.0 vs. 2.58) at p<1e-6. Steering mattered most when the target emotion fought the model’s prior — that is, it compensated for model bias. The authors’ formulation, that “more expressive models and more steerable interfaces are important and complementary,” is the warrant for building interaction machinery during rapid model progress; the study’s structure (composer self-report plus blind listener judgement of communicative intent) is a reusable protocol.

Amuse (Yewon Kim et al. 2025) supplies the equivalent evidence for multimodal annotation. Its within-subjects study compared Amuse-plus-Aria against Aria alone (Donahue et al. 2024) with ten songwriters (eight hobbyist, two professional) writing an eight-bar chorus in 25 minutes, analysed by Wilcoxon signed-rank test. Inspiration support rose from 4.60 to 6.20 (p<0.01) and task alignment from 4.30 to 6.10 (p=0.016); the CSI facets of controllability (4.40→5.80), collaboration (3.90→5.80), exploration (4.40→6.10) and expressiveness (4.20→6.00) all improved at p<0.05. Two nulls matter as much: completion time was unchanged (17.4 vs. 17.6 minutes) and externally judged quality did not differ, though self-rated quality rose (5.30→6.40, p=0.023). A listening study with forty-five raters and 900 pairwise judgements found Amuse’s progressions on a par with an LSTM harmonic prior for coherence, ahead of raw GPT-4o (p = .009) and most preferred for keyword relevance in 58% of judgements. The honest reading is that multimodal inspiration changes the experience of composing measurably and the artefact not at all.

The MMM-C study (Tchemeube et al. 2023) quantifies the cost of the opposite design choice. Integrating the Multi-Track Music Machine (Ens and Pasquier 2020; Tchemeube et al. 2022) into Cubase as a plugin whose only exposed parameter was temperature, Tchemeube and colleagues ran a three-part mixed-methods study with eighteen expert composers (eight hobbyist, ten professional). Usability was acceptable (SUS 71.4–75.7 across three tasks) and acceptance moderate (TAM ease 3.1–3.3/5, usefulness 3.4–3.7/5), CSI enjoyment strongest at 3.85/5. But ease of control scored 5.2/10 against a desire for more control of 9.5/10 — the largest measured control deficit in this literature, and a direct measurement of what an under-parameterised compile step feels like. Since the model was strong and the DAW integration real, the complaint is squarely about the control channel.

Smaller studies triangulate individual primitives. ChordRipple’s chord-substitution study found adventurous recommendations significantly increased chord novelty (p=0.0039) (Huang et al. 2016). Drawlody (Liang and Wang 2024) evaluated sketch-to-melody with eighteen participants (six musically trained), reporting user-friendliness of 4.38/5 against 2.94 for a chord-based interface and 2.38 for a DAW, and interpretability of 4.50/5 — notable because that score attaches to a visualisation of how the sketch was mapped to the melody, i.e. to feedback about the compile step rather than output quality. RefinPaint (Ramoneda et al. 2024) ran a listening study with fifteen annotators across fragment sizes of 50%, 30% and 10%, finding its critic-plus-inpainter loop preferred at all sizes, with four amateur composers reporting improved drafts. Composer’s Assistant 2 (Malandro 2024) found no quality difference between real music and pieces co-composed with its infilling controls. Loop Copilot (Zhang et al. 2023) evaluated conversational orchestration of audio backends with eight users (SUS 75.3±15.3, TAM 4.09±1.09), drawing the complaints that recur throughout: limited attribute control, unpredictable prompt responsiveness, no DAW integration. MultiVerse (Wang et al. 2026) compared creator-authored constraints against prompting with ten songwriters, who preferred explicit constraint authoring despite its cost in iteration speed. ComposerX had 32.2% of its outputs judged human-composed against 55.4% for genuine human pieces, without reporting rater counts (Q. Deng et al. 2024), and MIDI-LLM’s in-the-wild deployment covered fifty-eight musicians and roughly 4,000 generations, with lead-sheet generation most accepted (S.-L. Wu, Carlton, et al. 2026).

Music Creation by Example (Frid et al. 2020) found, across 104 video creators, that example-based input is more natural than tag or text search for people who think in references rather than theory terms. Two studies go beyond turn-taking. ReaLJam (Scarlatos et al. 2025), built over the RL-tuned ReaLchords accompanist (Yusong Wu et al. 2024), ran six experienced pianists through hour-long sessions across a baseline plus eight modified settings. RL-tuned agents beat the pre-trained baseline on all measures; enjoyment averaged 4.3/5 but perceived adaptation only 2.7/5; five of six wanted the agent’s upcoming chords displayed before commitment; and — the finding most relevant to shipping defaults — preferences across the other settings varied so widely between individuals that no configuration won. AI as Social Glue (Suh et al. 2021) studied thirty participants in fifteen stratified pairs co-composing with and without Cococo’s model, and found it acted as a role transformer: pairs drifted from co-composing to co-producing, evaluating output rather than authoring material.

The only longitudinal study in this cluster is Rhapsody Refiner (Krol et al. 2025), a tool deliberately unable to compose from scratch: it masks notes in the musician’s own MIDI and re-predicts them with MusicBERT (Zeng et al. 2021) under user-set parameters. Eight practising musicians used it for four weeks with journals, interaction logs and interviews, analysed thematically. Participants valued it for “moments, not whole ideas,” rejected automatic completion, and reported that its dependence on their own input produced ownership of both process and artefact; imperfection prompted problem-solving rather than frustration. This is the strongest available evidence for the studio’s central bet, and a demonstration of the study design the field lacks.

Against all of this sits the CMU experiment by Oros, Telang and Randall (Oros et al. 2025), the field’s sharpest cautionary result and its weakest-documented one. In a randomised design, 140 musically trained participants each composed a 15-second melody on a small keyboard; a randomly assigned group could use Udio (Universal Music Group and Udio 2025) to generate audio “for inspiration,” and separate listeners rated all melodies on creativity, enjoyment and musicality. As reported, AI-assisted melodies were slower, used fewer notes and were judged less creative, and human-only melodies outperformed the AI inspirations themselves. These claims need care: as of September 2026 the work exists only as a conference poster (November 2025) and a university news story (January 2026), with no public paper, no effect sizes, no rater counts, and no disambiguation of “slower” (tempo or composition time); the related SSRN working paper “Help That Hurts” (Oros et al. 2026) was verified only through its Crossref record. Taken as indicative, the study says that bolting a prompt-to-audio generator beside a composition task can degrade trained composers’ output. It says nothing about an integrated symbolic co-creator, which is why the studio should be evaluated with the same randomised, blind-rater design rather than cite the result rhetorically.

10.2 E2 Field studies and practitioner perspectives

The requirements literature here is qualitative and remarkably consistent. Its founding document is the AI Song Contest study (Huang et al. 2020), a thematic analysis of the thirteen teams (61 people, 2–15 per team, median 4) that entered the 2020 contest. Teams used a median of four model types and “rarely attempted end-to-end generation”; instead they decomposed songwriting into melody, harmony, bass, drums, lyrics and structure, generated massively (one team 450 melodies, another 10,000 lyric lines) and curated post hoc because the models were not steerable, authoring structure by hand because small models lacked holistic context. The call for interfaces that are “decomposable, steerable, interpretable, and adaptive” is a requirements list written by practitioners before the tools existed. HAISP (Morris et al. 2024) turned that into a resource, coding the process descriptions contest teams submitted — which tools for which task, what control creators retained — from 34 submissions in 2023 and, in 2025, the 2024 cohort, whose comparison surfaced differences in creative agency between general-purpose and fine-tuned custom tools.

I Keep Counting (Micchi et al. 2021) documents the first-person counterpart, logging more than twenty-five human interventions in one contest entry and arguing that deliberate selection and modification of AI material constitutes authorship. The deepest professional study remains Sony CSL’s (Deruty et al. 2022), a reflection on 6–18-month collaborations with six professional acts using in-house prototypes. Its findings read as design constraints: practitioners want “pull” (explicit queries) not “push” (unsolicited suggestions); they want the system primed on their own audio; they generate and curate, navigate latent spaces, and exploit artefacts as aesthetic material. The frictions named are leaving the DAW, the absence of recognisable controls, and the lack of visualisation. The paper also mounts the strongest challenge to a symbolic-first studio, arguing that in-studio popular-music practice is audio-first and that audio tools fit it better than MIDI tools — a claim to answer with rendering and stems rather than dismiss (see S3.8). Newman, Morris and Lee (Newman et al. 2023) interviewed six professional creators for 60–90 minutes each with two-coder analysis; their design implications nearly restate the studio’s stance: confine AI to ideation rather than intentional decisions; provide interoperability through MIDI, WAV and MusicXML export or in-software integration; separate “influences” (ideas) from “mechanisms” (housekeeping); and prefer open-source, explainable models to black boxes.

Complaints about text-to-audio tools are now quantified. Ronchini and colleagues (Ronchini et al. 2025) gave seventeen producers from seven countries an hour with a custom interface pairing MusicGen (Copet et al. 2023) with HT-Demucs stem separation. Text-to-music was seen as an ideation aid, not production-ready: 94% would use it in ideation, while the barriers were intent–output misalignment, failure to align to a project’s tempo, key and beat grid, and limited editability, plus ethical concerns about copyright, compensation and homogenisation from Western-centric training data. Fu and colleagues (Fu et al. 2025) followed three teams of novice producers through a ten-week course (nine of twenty interviewed) and identified an emergent stage they call “collaging and refinement” — the labour of integrating heterogeneous AI outputs into a coherent track, precisely the assembly step this review calls compile, and one no tool supports. Ford and colleagues (Ford et al. 2024) had eight composer-researchers each compose with a different AI tool while pausing hourly to reflect on screenshots, and found reflection clustered around curation: decisions concentrate where alternatives are compared, which argues for surfacing history and alternatives, not only generation (see S4.5). Cavez and colleagues’ interviews with nine professional composers (Cavez et al. 2024) analysed notation software through the Cognitive Dimensions of Notations, documenting premature commitment, viscosity and continued reliance on paper — justification for a loosely structured annotation layer over a strict symbolic model, and the motivation for their EuterPen prototype (Cavez et al. 2025) (cross-ref S1.3).

Sturm, Ben-Tal and colleagues’ folk-rnn case study (B. L. Sturm et al. 2019) tracked an ABC-notation model through years of use by traditional and contemporary musicians, in concerts, an album and a musical, concluding that generated tunes are raw material requiring editing, arrangement and performance, and that the relevant evaluation is usefulness to practice rather than likelihood; its companion paper (B. L. T. Sturm et al. 2019) adds the engineer’s duty to document limitations and evaluate for fairness and transparency, not accuracy alone. An early wave of studies on generative AI in composition teaching is also appearing (Oros et al. 2026), whose “help that hurts” finding — AI ideation reducing the quality and originality of student composition output — is plausibly the same experiment written up, since it shares the authors, the finding and the period; it should not be read as independent corroboration of the CMU poster. But these papers were verified only through Crossref records, appear in venues of mixed quality, and their designs could not be checked; treat them as signals worth replicating, not results.

10.3 E3 Deployed systems with usage data

Only four systems in this review report deployment usage data. Hookpad Aria (Donahue et al. 2024) is the most directly relevant: a copilot inside a web lead-sheet editor supporting continuation, infilling of spans in the middle of existing material, and harmony-from-melody or melody-from-harmony, built on anticipatory conditioning (Thickstun et al. 2024). From its March 2024 release it had generated 318,000 suggestions for 3,000 users, of which 74,000 were accepted into songs — roughly 23% acceptance over the reporting period to early 2025. This is the best public evidence that span-level suggestions inside a notation editor are wanted and used, and it makes acceptance rate a cheap, ecologically valid signal — the authors’ “data flywheel for music co-creation.” The Bach Doodle (Huang, Hawthorne, et al. 2019) answers the scale question: users entered two-bar melodies on a simplified staff and Coconet harmonised them in four voices in about two seconds in-browser, drawing over 55 million queries in three days. Its by-product is the more durable contribution — a CC BY 4.0 dataset of 21.6 million harmonisations from 8.5 million sessions, with melody, harmonisation, user rating and playback count: a unique record of how people rate AI harmonisations of their own material. FlowComposer (Papadopoulos et al. 2016) contributes the oldest and most actionable professional usage observation: composers using its constrained lead-sheet regeneration rarely generated more than eight bars at a time, using it for fragments inside longer human compositions. Casini and colleagues (Casini et al. 2025) provide the negative image, clustering text embeddings of user prompts, metatags and generated lyrics from Suno and Udio over May–October 2024; the resulting map of how people try to steer a text-to-song system — including the peculiar metatag strategies users invent to obtain control the interface withholds — catalogues the pain a symbolic annotation layer is meant to remove.

10.4 E4 Metrics for generated music

Automatic metrics for generated music are, on current evidence, adequate for regression testing and inadequate for judging quality. The default has been Fréchet Audio Distance (Kilgour et al. 2019), a Fréchet distance between Gaussians fitted to VGGish embeddings of reference and generated audio — proposed for music enhancement and validated only against ratings of enhancement artefacts, then adopted wholesale for generative evaluation. Gui and colleagues (Gui et al. 2024) documented its failure modes — sample-size bias, VGGish as a poor embedding, weak reference sets — and prescribed a better recipe: extrapolate to infinite sample size, use CLAP-LAION-music embeddings and music-specific references, and compute per-song FAD for outlier detection. KAD (Chung et al. 2025) abandons the Gaussian assumption entirely for an MMD kernel distance that is distribution-free, unbiased, convergent at small sample sizes and GPU-scalable, reporting stronger alignment with human perception. Fréchet Music Distance (Retkowski et al. 2025) is the first widely reported distributional metric over symbolic music in this corpus. More useful for a compiler are Yang and Lerch’s interpretable symbolic features (Yang and Lerch 2020) — pitch-class histograms and transition matrices, note-length histograms, inter-onset intervals — compared as distributions against a reference set via KL divergence and overlap area, and released as mgeval. These are metrics a composer can read, which suits them to compile-time diagnostics rather than scoring.

For text-conditioned output the standard adherence measure is the CLAP score (Wu et al. 2023), a cosine similarity between prompt and audio embeddings, evaluated against caption corpora: MusicCaps’ 5,500 expert-captioned ten-second clips (Agostinelli et al. 2023) and the Song Describer Dataset’s 1.1k crowd captions over 706 CC-licensed tracks (Manco et al. 2023), the latter created partly because MusicCaps is YouTube-derived and licence-constrained. MusicEval (C. Liu et al. 2025) supplies expert reference ratings — 2,748 clips from 31 systems over 384 prompts, rated by fourteen professionals for 13,740 ratings on overall impression and text alignment — plus a CLAP-based MOS-predictor baseline, and became the basis of AudioMOS Challenge 2025 Track 1 (W.-C. Huang et al. 2025). That challenge (24 teams) and the ICASSP 2026 Automatic Song Aesthetics Evaluation challenge established MOS prediction as a research line, including Audiobox Aesthetics’ four axes (production quality, complexity, enjoyment, usefulness). Such predictors are pre-filters that rank candidate renders before the composer hears them — useful inside a compile loop, worthless as a claim about the work.

The strongest results here measure the metrics themselves. Music Arena (Yonghyun Kim et al. 2025) runs live pairwise human-preference evaluation of text-to-music models with LLM-based routing across heterogeneous model signatures, collects listening behaviour and free-text feedback, and releases rolling open data — infrastructure the studio could reuse. MAD (Y.
