Huang et al. 2025) showed FAD to be inconsistent and weakly correlated with human judgement, proposed MAUVE Audio Divergence over self-supervised embeddings, and released the MusicPrefs dataset; MAD reached an average rank correlation of 0.84 against FAD’s 0.49 on synthetic tests, and 0.62 against FAD’s 0.14 with MusicPrefs. Grötschla and colleagues (Grötschla et al. 2025) gathered 15,000 pairwise comparisons from 2,500 raters over 6,000 songs from twelve state-of-the-art generators, producing the first large human-preference ranking and documenting how weakly FAD, KAD, CLAP and related metrics track it; Zhang and colleagues (H. Zhang, Liang, et al. 2025) independently found aesthetic and distributional metrics inconsistent with human preference across five leading systems. The survey by Kader and Karmaker (Kader and Karmaker 2025) names poor metric–perception correlation, cross-cultural bias and absent standardisation as the open problems. The conclusion the studio should adopt explicitly: distributional audio metrics are sanity checks — did this build regress? — and never evidence that a co-creative tool works.

Three further instruments serve purposes a co-creative studio needs. MiRA (Batlle-Roca et al. 2024) combines audio-similarity metrics (CLAP, Discogs-EffNet, cover-identification embeddings) to detect replication of training data in generated audio above roughly 10% — the open baseline for auditing any renderer the studio ships, alongside work on whether watermarks in training data survive into outputs (Epple et al. 2024). Echoes of Humanity (Figueiredo et al. 2025) contributes a template for listener discrimination: a blind, Turing-like pairwise test with randomised crossover over real-world Suno outputs and human songs, controlling pairwise similarity. Its causal finding — that discrimination rises when the paired items are similar, and that the cues cited are mostly vocal and technical — means any “can listeners tell compiled from hand-arranged” test must control pair matching or it measures nothing. At population scale, the Deezer/Ipsos survey (Deezer and Ipsos 2025) of 9,000 adults across eight countries found 97% failed to distinguish fully AI-generated tracks blind, while 80% wanted labelling — undercutting “listeners cannot tell” as a design goal and supporting provenance metadata as a feature (see C3).

10.5 E5 Creativity and co-creativity evaluation frameworks

The instruments for measuring creative support predate music AI and are better developed than the metrics above. The Creativity Support Index (Cherry and Latulipe 2014) is the workhorse: a validated survey scoring a tool on six factors — Collaboration, Enjoyment, Exploration, Expressiveness, Immersion, Results Worth Effort — from paired agreement items, with paired-factor comparisons weighting each by task importance to yield a 0–100 score. It is used, whole or by facet, across this literature (Louie et al. 2020; Tchemeube et al. 2023; Yewon Kim et al. 2025), making per-facet reporting the pragmatic choice. Karimi and colleagues (Karimi et al. 2018) supply what CSI alone misses: four questions — who evaluates, what is evaluated (product, process, interaction, user experience), when and how — organising a survey whose central finding is a warning that co-creative systems overwhelmingly evaluate user experience and under-measure the creativity of the collaboration or of the artefact. For the studio that means measuring how annotations change compiled outputs, not only how composers feel about annotating.

The computational-creativity tradition contributes complementary standards. Jordanous’s SPECS (Jordanous 2012) prescribes defining creativity for the domain from fourteen empirically derived components — originality, value, intention and emotional involvement, domain competence, variety — before testing against it. Ritchie’s eighteen criteria (Ritchie 2007) formalise the relation between an “inspiring set” and an output set using ratings of typicality and quality — the structure of a studio compile, where the composer’s material is the inspiring set and the question is whether outputs are typical of it and good without being copies. Colton’s creative tripod (Colton 2008) holds that a system is perceived as creative when it exhibits skill, appreciation and imagination; appreciation — evaluating one’s own output against the composer’s stated constraints — is the missing leg in nearly every generator surveyed here and a design goal for the compiler’s critic. Boden’s distinctions (Boden 2004) fix the vocabulary: psychological versus historical creativity, and combinational, exploratory and transformational creativity. In those terms the studio’s claim is modest and testable — the compiler performs exploratory generation inside a space the composer defines and transforms. Kantosalo and Toivonen’s modes (Kantosalo and Toivonen 2016) give the interaction lens: the loop is alternating at the artefact level but task-divided at the role level, and the two plausibly require different measures. Finally, COFI’s survey of ninety-two co-creative systems (Rezwana and Maher 2023) found almost no AI→human communication — both a gap and a measurement problem, since no instrument evaluates the quality of such an explanation.

10.6 E6 Music-theory competence benchmarks

If an LLM is to sit at the front of the compiler, interpreting textual annotations and dispatching to symbolic operators, its musical competence is a load-bearing engineering parameter. The benchmarks are discouraging. MusicTheoryBench, released with ChatMusician (Yuan et al. 2024) — a LLaMA-2-7B continually pretrained on MusicPile’s roughly 4B tokens of ABC notation and music text — tests college-level theory knowledge and reasoning by multiple choice; ChatMusician beats LLaMA-2 and GPT-3.5 zero-shot, while GPT-4’s accuracy on the reasoning split is reported near random (a figure recalled rather than re-verified). ZIQI-Eval (J. Li et al. 2024) scales the knowledge question to over 14,000 questions in ten categories and fifty-six sub-categories, reporting all sixteen LLMs tested as poor. ABC-Eval (Zhao et al. 2025) localises the failure: across 1,086 items in ten sub-tasks over ABC scores, seven models including GPT-5, Gemini and DeepSeek exceed 90% on syntax while scoring near random on sequence-level tasks such as emotion — they parse the notation and cannot hear it. WildScore (Mundada et al. 2025) extends the question to multimodal reasoning over real score images, finding clear gaps.

The audio side is no better grounded. MuChoMusic (Weck et al. 2024) built 1,187 human-validated multiple-choice questions over 644 tracks from MusicCaps and the Song Describer Dataset, covering theory and instrumentation knowledge as well as cultural and functional reasoning, and found five open audio-language models over-relying on the language modality — answering plausibly without really listening. That matters for any pipeline where an audio-LLM interprets a hummed phrase or a pasted reference recording as an annotation. SynTheory (Wei et al. 2024) asks whether generative backbones encode theory at all, using a synthetic MIDI-and-audio dataset isolating tempo, time signature, notes, intervals, scales, chords and progressions and probing Jukebox and MusicGen layer by layer: the concepts are linearly detectable, with detectability varying by model size and layer — a usable diagnostic before trusting a backbone with an annotation vocabulary. Controlled comparisons of adaptation strategies show that such choices materially change outcomes and that standard metrics behave idiosyncratically for music (Kumar et al. 2026), and surveys of music foundation models (Ma et al. 2024) frame the conclusion architecturally: LLMs are best used as orchestrators around specialised symbolic models and deterministic analysers, with their theory claims verified rather than trusted.

10.7 An evaluation protocol for the studio

The literature above supports a specific, affordable instrument set; what matters is stating what each part measures and what it cannot see. The core comparative instrument should be the Creativity Support Index reported per facet rather than as a single number (Cherry and Latulipe 2014), since facet-level reporting is what makes results commensurable with Cococo, MMM-C and Amuse (Louie et al. 2020; Tchemeube et al. 2023; Yewon Kim et al. 2025). Alongside it belong the four constructs this literature repeatedly shows moving when control granularity changes — ownership, controllability, comprehensibility and self-efficacy — using the seven-point item wording from Cococo and Expressive Communication so effects can be compared against published baselines of 5.9 versus 3.5 for controllability and 5.2 versus 3.8 for ownership (Louie et al. 2020, 2022). This battery measures whether an annotation layer changes experienced agency. It cannot see whether the music got better: Amuse raised self-rated quality while externally judged quality did not move (Yewon Kim et al. 2025). Self-report alone will flatter any well-designed interface.

The artefact question needs blind paired listener comparisons on a communicative task, following Expressive Communication: the composer is given a target to convey, listeners judge in forced-choice pairs whether the intent came across and how musical the result is, and the analysis crosses interface with model conditions so the two are not confounded (Louie et al. 2022). Two refinements are mandatory: pairs must be similarity-matched, since Echoes of Humanity showed causally that discrimination rises when items resemble each other (Figueiredo et al. 2025); and rater pools must be large, with Grötschla and colleagues’ 15,000 comparisons from 2,500 raters as the scale to aspire to and Music Arena as reusable infrastructure (Grötschla et al. 2025; Yonghyun Kim et al. 2025). This measures whether compiled output communicates and pleases. It does not measure whether the composer would put their name on it, and it under-weights structure and long-range form, since paired tests use short excerpts — the weakness structural analyses identify in deep generators generally (Dai, Yu, et al. 2022).

Deployment telemetry is the cheapest and most ecologically valid instrument, and Hookpad Aria establishes the metric: suggestions offered, suggestions accepted, the ratio (Donahue et al. 2024). For an annotation-driven compiler the analogue is richer — acceptance rate per annotation type, edit distance between compiled output and what the composer kept, recompile cycles before acceptance, and the span length of accepted material, read against FlowComposer’s observation that composers rarely want more than eight bars at once (Papadopoulos et al. 2016). Such telemetry captures revealed preference continuously and at scale, and can seed a public dataset as the Bach Doodle’s did (Huang, Hawthorne, et al. 2019). Its blind spot is severe: acceptance measures local attractiveness, not whether the finished piece is good or whether the composer’s voice survived, and a system that maximises acceptance can drift into the curation trap Social Glue documented (Suh et al. 2021).

That blind spot is what a longitudinal in-practice study is for, and Rhapsody Refiner’s four-week ecological design — a small cohort of practising musicians, journals, interaction logs, interviews and thematic analysis (Krol et al. 2025) — is the model, with Sony CSL’s 6–18-month embedded collaborations as the more ambitious form (Deruty et al. 2022). Only this design can observe whether composers use the compiler for “moments, not whole ideas,” whether ownership persists past novelty, and whether the tool changes what they write; it is also the only way to test the failure the CMU experiment gestures at under conditions where the assistance is integrated rather than bolted on (Oros et al. 2025). Four supporting instruments complete the set cheaply: interpretable symbolic diagnostics as compiler regression tests (Yang and Lerch 2020), with Fréchet Music Distance for distributional drift (Retkowski et al. 2025); KAD or MAD rather than raw FAD for any audio renderer, strictly as sanity checks (Chung et al. 2025; Y. Huang et al. 2025; Gui et al. 2024); MiRA-style replication audits plus provenance logging of every human intervention as authorship evidence (Batlle-Roca et al. 2024; Micchi et al. 2021); and SynTheory-style probing with MusicTheoryBench, ABC-Eval and MuChoMusic as acceptance tests on any model considered for the compiler’s front end (Wei et al. 2024; Yuan et al. 2024; Zhao et al. 2025; Weck et al. 2024). The plan should be written against Karimi’s four questions, so the interaction and the process are evaluated and not only the experience (Karimi et al. 2018), and against SPECS’ requirement that “creative support” be defined for this domain before it is measured (Jordanous 2012).

10.8 Implications for the studio

Read together, this evidence licenses three of the studio’s core commitments and refuses a fourth. It licenses region-scoped, constraint-driven regeneration over whole-output generation: Cococo’s ownership and control gains, Expressive Communication’s finding that steering compensates for model bias, MMM-C’s measured control deficit under a one-slider design, and Rhapsody Refiner’s finding that transforming authored material sustains ownership all point the same way (Louie et al. 2020, 2022; Tchemeube et al. 2023; Krol et al. 2025). It licenses multimodal annotation as a steering channel, through Amuse’s within-subjects gains, Drawlody’s usability margin over chord and DAW baselines, and example-based input’s advantage over text search (Yewon Kim et al. 2025; Liang and Wang 2024; Frid et al. 2020). And it licenses notation-surface integration, through Aria’s 74,000 accepted suggestions, FlowComposer’s eight-bar usage pattern, and professional composers asking for MusicXML interoperability and control preservation (Donahue et al. 2024; Papadopoulos et al. 2016; Huang, Hawthorne, et al. 2019; Newman et al. 2023). What it does not license is any claim that the music will be better: Amuse found no difference in externally judged quality, Composer’s Assistant 2 found parity with real music rather than superiority, and the CMU experiment leaves live the hypothesis that assistance can subtract (Yewon Kim et al. 2025; Malandro 2024; Oros et al. 2025). The studio’s defensible claim is about agency, control and the shape of the work, and should be evaluated as such.

The gaps are as instructive as the findings. Sample sizes are small and skew novice: the median controlled study here has under twenty participants, and the professional studies are mostly interview-based (Newman et al. 2023; Deruty et al. 2022; Cavez et al. 2024). There is one four-week field study of a music co-creation tool (Krol et al. 2025) and one deployed system with public acceptance telemetry (Donahue et al. 2024); nothing in between, and nothing longitudinal on a notation-native compiler. No study evaluates an annotation as the unit of analysis — no published work reports whether a composer’s typed, drawn or hummed instruction was honoured, the studio’s central measurable claim, and the instrument for it does not exist. Nor is there any established way to evaluate the quality of an AI’s explanation of what it did, which COFI shows is unmeasured because it is unbuilt (Rezwana and Maher 2023).

Practically, the studio should instrument itself from the first release rather than plan a study later. Acceptance telemetry per annotation type, recompile counts and kept-span lengths cost little to log and answer questions no questionnaire reaches; the CSI-plus-agency battery and a similarity-matched paired listening protocol belong in standing infrastructure, run at every major interaction change; and one longitudinal cohort, on the Rhapsody Refiner model, should run continuously. Two of the gaps above are themselves candidate contributions: an annotation-fidelity measure — did the compiler honour this mark, and can the composer tell — and an evaluation of AI→human explanation quality on notation, for which RefinPaint’s “where to modify” critic and ReaLJam’s commitment display are the only prior art (Ramoneda et al. 2024; Scarlatos et al. 2025). Either would give the studio empirical claims no existing system can make.

11. Substrate: what an open-source studio would be built on (B)

The preceding parts of the stage view describe what a composer-centred studio should do; this section surveys what it would be made of: notation engines and editors (B1), symbolic toolkits (B2), the runtime carrying MIDI, audio and plugin integration (B3), the corpora any learned component must be trained on — whose licences, not sizes, govern what an open project may ship (B4) — and the pedagogy of music-as-computation (B5). Versions and dates are reported where the underlying notes verified them and hedged where they did not.

11.1 B1 Notation engines and editors

Browser engraving. Verovio is the strongest open route to an engraving-quality, annotatable score in a browser: a C++20 library (dual LGPL-3.0/GPL-3.0, v6.2.1 May 2026) compiled to WebAssembly, reading MEI natively plus MusicXML, Humdrum and ABC and emitting SVG, MIDI and a timemap for score–audio synchronisation (Pugin et al. 2014). Two properties matter for annotation: every rendered glyph carries an element id mapping back to the source encoding, making click-to-annotate and scribble overlays anchored to symbolic elements feasible rather than merely graphical; and MEI encodes editorial layers, so the difference between what the human wrote and what a machine proposed can live in the score file (Pugin et al. 2014). mei-friend demonstrates the pattern: MEI code beside a live Verovio rendering, GitHub-backed commits, and W3C Web Annotations on element ids (Goebl and Weigl 2023). Interchange must nonetheless be MusicXML 4.0, implemented by over 250 applications; its JSON successor MNX remained unfinished through August 2026, and neither has an annotation model beyond directive text (S2.1) (W3C Music Notation Community Group 2021).

The alternatives trade annotation depth for simplicity. OpenSheetMusicDisplay (BSD-3, v2.0.0 June 2026) lays out MusicXML automatically but is explicitly a renderer, not an editor (OpenSheetMusicDisplay contributors and Zanchi 2026), sitting on VexFlow (MIT), a low-level engraver still the right building block for chord grids and sketch overlays (Cheppudira and VexFlow contributors 2024). alphaTab (MPL-2.0) is one of the few open engines shipping notation and synchronised SoundFont playback together (Kuschny 2026), and abcjs re-renders ABC to SVG on every keystroke with playback — the cheapest text-to-notation round trip, and important because ABC is what specialised music LLMs emit most fluently (S3.6) (Rosen 2026; Walshaw 2011). LilyPond is the batch, text-compiled engraving benchmark and literal ancestor of the compile metaphor (Nienhuys et al. 2026; Nienhuys and Nieuwenhuizen 2003), while Humdrum contributes an encoding idea rather than an engine: parallel spines put a **harm column beside the notes in one plain-text file (Huron 1995).

MuseScore is the editor to integrate with, not to compete against. MuseScore Studio 4.x is a GPL-3 C++/Qt 6.9 editor with SMuFL fonts, VST3 hosting, MusicXML and MIDI interchange, MuseSounds libraries behind the proprietary Muse Hub and a QML plugin API extended in 4.6 (Muse Group 2026, 2025). Two facts shape integration: the “extensions framework” meant to replace that read-mostly API was deferred, leaving external scripted manipulation a pain point; and 4.7 (2026) shipped a beta MP3-to-MSCZ converter, Muse Group’s only shipping AI feature, its announced Cantai choir integration delayed (Muse Group 2026, 2025). MuseScore Studio 5, begun December 2025, targets DAW-like automation, a piano-roll, audio staves and MIDI mapping: the notation editor is converging on the DAW from the opposite direction to Suno Studio (Muse Group 2026; Suno Inc. 2026). The most-used free notation editor is thus open source, speaks the interchange formats, feeds licence-clean corpora and is entering the studio’s territory with no generative capability — an installed base for a studio that treats MSCZ and MusicXML as first-class.

The commercial landscape and the web platforms. MakeMusic ended development and sales of Finale on 26 August 2024 and steered users to Dorico Pro at a $149 crossgrade (MakeMusic, Inc. 2024); Dorico 6 and 6.2 added proofreading and engraving refinements but no machine learning (Steinberg Media Technologies 2025), and Sibelius through February 2026 added themes, engraving rules and scripting, again with none (Avid Technology 2026). On the web, Flat offers a collaborative editor with REST and embed APIs and per-edit version history, Noteflight a comparable but less open one (Tutteo Ltd. (Flat) 2026; Tutteo Ltd (Flat.io) 2026); Soundslice aligns notation to reference recordings through explicit syncpoints behind a Data API, the machinery an “insert example audio” annotation needs (Holovaty and Soundslice 2026); and Hookpad’s lead-sheet editor, with colour-coded Roman numerals and theory hints, hosts the Aria co-writer (C1, E3) (Donahue et al. 2024).

11.2 B2 Symbolic toolkits

music21 is the reference analysis layer. Cuthbert and Ariza’s toolkit parses MusicXML, MIDI, ABC, Humdrum **kern and MEI into a hierarchical Stream model, writes MusicXML, MIDI and LilyPond, supplies key finding, RomanNumeral analysis, chord identification and voice-leading, and bundles a corpus including all 371 Riemenschneider Bach chorales, Palestrina and the Viennese quartets; v10.5.0 (June 2026) is BSD-3-Clause on Python 3.11 or later (Cuthbert and Ariza 2010). Two limits bound its role: pure-Python parsing is too slow for corpus-scale preprocessing, and MusicXML round-tripping preserves pitch, rhythm and spanners but not layout, with MEI export absent (Cuthbert and Ariza 2010; Liao et al. 2024). It is an analysis backend and vocabulary source, not a real-time engine or a lossless notation store.

The processing stack has consolidated. symusic is an MIT C++20 core with nanobind bindings that loads and saves MIDI and ABC and applies vectorised transforms, reporting parsing hundreds of times faster than mido, pretty_midi and miditoolkit, and now serving as MidiTok’s I/O backend (Liao et al. 2024). MidiTok (MIT) implements ten tokenisations — REMI, REMI+, MIDI-Like, TSD, Structured, CPWord, Octuple, MuMIDI, MMM and PerTok — with BPE and Unigram training on the Hugging Face backend; its MMM-style per-track-bar scheme maps onto region-scoped editing, making tokeniser choice an interface decision as much as a modelling one (S3.1, S3.2) (Fradet et al. 2021; Ens and Pasquier 2020). partitura (Apache-2.0, v1.9.0 May 2026) covers the axis the others do not, parsing MusicXML, MEI, **kern and MIDI into a Score with note arrays and computing score-to-performance alignment — the primitive connecting a passage played on a keyboard to the notation it annotates (Cancino-Chacón et al. 2022; Karystinaios and Widmer 2023). MusPy, unmaintained since 2022, is now a design reference for dataset management, representations and objective metrics (Dong et al. 2020).

Legacy interoperability and adjacent toolkits. The older stack — mido, pretty_midi, miditoolkit and note-seq with its NoteSequence protobuf, a proven design for an internal representation — cannot be discarded because public checkpoints assume it; these details are recorded as partially verified (Raffel and Ellis 2014; Huang and Yang 2020; Hsiao et al. 2021). Magenta.js showed small symbolic models run client-side, shipping TensorFlow.js ports of MusicVAE, Coconet and Piano Genie that powered the Bach Doodle and Cococo, but it is effectively frozen, so new browser deployments should plan on ONNX Runtime Web or WebGPU (Roberts, Hawthorne, et al. 2018; Huang, Hawthorne, et al. 2019; Louie et al. 2020; Lyria Team et al. 2025). Muskits-ESPnet is the open path from a symbolic line plus lyrics to sung audio (Yuning Wu et al. 2024).

11.3 B3 Runtime and integration

Web MIDI is the binding platform constraint. In 2026 the Web MIDI API works in Chrome 43+, Edge 79+, Opera and Firefox 108+ behind a site-permission add-on, with global coverage around 81 per cent, but Safari on desktop and iOS supports it not at all and has published no roadmap, Apple citing fingerprinting (Wilson et al. 2026). For a studio whose compose path includes playing material in from a keyboard this is decisive: a purely web deployment excludes Apple users from live input, making a desktop shell an architectural requirement. Web Audio, by contrast, is universal, AudioWorklet permits sample-accurate WebAssembly DSP off the main thread, and Tone.js (MIT) supplies the transport layer.

Synthesis is ready in the browser and headless. spessasynth is a pure TypeScript SF2/SF3/DLS synthesizer running in an AudioWorklet that also plays MIDI and renders offline to WAV, under Apache-2.0; FluidSynth (LGPL-2.1) remains the reference SoundFont synthesizer for offline rendering in research pipelines and SFZ supplies a redistributable sample body, these last two partial (spessasus 2026; Liao et al. 2024; Dong et al. 2020). A compiled score can thus be auditioned in the client and rendered headlessly for evaluation (E4) (Kuschny 2026).

Plugin protocols and integration precedents. Composers work in DAWs, so a compiler that cannot appear inside one is an island. Only one protocol was verified directly: CLAP, announced June 2022 by u-he and Bitwig, an MIT-licensed C ABI with per-note modulation, supported by Bitwig, REAPER and FL Studio; VST3, Audio Units, ReaScript, Ableton Link’s tempo synchronisation over a LAN and Max for Live are recorded as recalled rather than re-verified (u-he et al. 2022). The deployment precedents matter more. Magenta Studio’s five Max for Live devices operate on the selected clip under Apache-2.0, establishing “the AI operates on the clip you selected, inside your DAW” as the reference architecture (Roberts et al. 2019); MMM4Live carried the Metacreation lineage to the same surface but stayed a closed beta without a published study (Metacreation Lab (Pasquier, Philippe and Ens, Jeff and Tchemeube, Renaud Bougueng) 2023; Ens and Pasquier 2020); and Composer’s Assistant in REAPER and MIDI-GPT in Cubase and Ableton are the fullest expressions of the pattern (S3.2) (Malandro 2024; Pasquier et al. 2025). AbletonMCP, an MIT-licensed Model Context Protocol server plus Remote Script, adds conversational control over tracks, clips, notes and arrangements (Ahuja 2025). The founder’s own instrument sits at the easy end: the Nord Stage family reportedly transmits on a global MIDI channel with program change and class-compliant USB-MIDI, making it an ordinary Web MIDI device in a Chromium host — though this entry is unverified recall (Clavia DMI AB 2023).

11.4 B4 Datasets and corpora, and what their licences permit

Generation papers routinely report dataset sizes and omit licences; for an open project the licence is the more consequential number, because it decides whether a trained artefact can be released at all.

Licence class Corpora (size; licence) What a released artefact may do
Permissive (CC0 / CC-BY) PDMX, >250k public-domain MusicXML (CC-BY 4.0) (Long et al. 2025); OpenScore Lieder, 1,200+ proofread songs (CC0), and String Quartets (Gotham and Jonas 2022); GiantMIDI-Piano, 10,855 pieces, ~1,237 h (Kong et al. 2022); ATEPP, 11,674 performances, ~1,000 h (H. Zhang et al. 2022); Bach Doodle, 21.6M melodies (Huang, Hawthorne, et al. 2019); Lakh MIDI, roughly 176,600 files (reportedly CC-BY on the collection) (Raffel 2016) Release weights; subject to the Lakh caveat
Non-commercial (CC-BY-NC-SA) MAESTRO, ~200 h / 1,276 performances (partial) (Hawthorne et al. 2019); Aria-MIDI, 1.19M files / ~100,629 h transcribed piano (Bradshaw and Colton 2025); Hooktheory/TheoryTab via Sheet Sage, ~50 h of functional-harmony analyses aligned to audio (Donahue et al. 2022); DCML corpora (partial) (Hentschel et al. 2021); MidiCaps (partial) (Melechovsky, Roy, et al. 2024) Published experiments and prototypes only
Restricted or murky MetaMIDI, 436,631 files (registration, no redistribution) (Ens and Pasquier 2021); Los Angeles MIDI, ~405k files (provenance unstated) (Lev 2024); MetaScore, 963k MuseScore-sourced scores (Xu et al. 2025); Wikifonia (site closed 2013) (Wang et al. 2020); musescore.com scrapes (Xmader 2021) Nothing shippable; research-only at best

The Lakh caveat is the most widely ignored fact in the symbolic literature: the CC-BY licence covers the collection, while the files are user transcriptions of copyrighted songs, so commercial use is a grey zone and quality is uneven (Raffel 2016). PDMX is the clean alternative — public-domain-only MusicXML from musescore.com with ratings enabling quality filtering, released CC-BY and shown to improve multitrack generation when rating-filtered, which its authors call the largest available copyright-free symbolic dataset (Long et al. 2025); for a notation-first studio it is doubly attractive because it is score-level, carrying dynamics, articulations and lyrics.

Analysis corpora. For machine-proposed harmonic annotations (S2.2), two corpora supply both training data and a format. When in Rome holds roughly 2,000 Roman-numeral analyses of about 1,500 works in RomanText (.rntxt, parsed by music21), the largest open training set for the task and the basis of AugmentedNet and RNBert (Gotham et al. 2019; Cuthbert and Ariza 2010; Nápoles López et al. 2021; Sailor 2024). The DCML corpora instead store expert chord, phrase and cadence labels inside MuseScore files as staff text (Hentschel et al. 2021; Neuwirth et al. 2018) — annotations living in the score file being exactly the pattern the studio needs.

Captions, chords and audio. MidiCaps pairs over 168,000 Lakh-derived files with LLM-generated captions from extracted tempo, chord, instrument and mood features (Melechovsky, Roy, et al. 2024); MusicCaps and Song Describer are the audio-side caption sets used by nearly every text-to-music model (Agostinelli et al. 2023; Manco et al. 2023); and MetaScore’s 963,000 MuseScore-sourced scores were converted to REMI+/MMT tokens rather than generated as notation (Xu et al. 2025). Chordonomicon adds over 666,000 chord progressions with section, genre and harmonic-function labels, its licence partial (Kantarelis et al. 2024). Slakh2100’s 2,100 Lakh files rendered into 145 hours of stems give exact symbolic-to-stem pairs for rendering evaluation, alongside MusicNet, MedleyDB and MUSDB18, all partially verified (Manilow et al. 2019). Among the small classics POP909’s melody-versus-accompaniment split is a direct analogue of compiling a piano part from a lead sheet (Wang et al. 2020; Huang et al. 2017), and HumTrans’s 56.22 hours of humming, whose baselines reach roughly 6.8 per cent note F1, quantify why an interactive correction step is mandatory (S1.2) (S. Liu, Li, et al. 2024; Gupta et al. 2024; Cartwright and Pardo 2015).

The shipping rule follows. Anything released with weights should be trained on the permissive tier — PDMX and OpenScore for notation, GiantMIDI-Piano and ATEPP for expressive piano, the composer’s own material for personal style — with per-file licence metadata retained; the non-commercial tier is legitimate for published experiments provided the boundary is documented; the restricted tier is for reading about, not training on. Composer’s Assistant, trained only on permissively licensed MIDI, shows a competitive infilling model can be built inside these constraints (Malandro 2024).

11.5 B5 Education and the pedagogy of music-as-computation

Two MIT courses bracket the design space. Cuthbert’s 21M.383 Computational Music Theory and Analysis teaches symbolic computational musicology entirely through Python and music21 notebooks, from score representation and Stream hierarchies through MusicXML to corpus statistics on the Bach chorales, Roman numerals and algorithmic composition; one session treats the 371 chorales as a queryable dataset — the corpus that trains Coconet — and the materials are CC-BY-NC-SA on OpenCourseWare (Cuthbert 2023; Cuthbert and Ariza 2010; Huang et al. 2017). It is a tested pedagogy for treating notation as data and analysis as a program. Egozy’s 21M.385 Interactive Music Systems is its interactional complement, with students building real-time Python and Kivy applications using FluidSynth-backed synthesis, MIDI sequencing, generative composition, gesture sensors and game-style graphics (Egozy 2016a); its notation-free architecture is the deliberate inverse of the studio’s stance. Egozy’s writing on the Harmonix titles analyses how they designed for musical expression within game constraints, and his in-browser score following is a component for aligning live playing to notation (Egozy 2016b, 2018).

Music-as-code platforms show the metaphor scales to novices. TunePad has learners write Python in notebook-like cells producing beats, basslines, chords and melodies with real-time playback, with Playbooks as combined tutorial-and-code documents; its cell interface is a direct precedent for a composition-as-program editor (Horn et al. 2022). EarSketch has students place licensed loops on tracks and impose algorithmic structure through fitMedia and makeBeat, with reported gains in intent to persist in computing especially among under-represented students, its claimed million-plus reach recorded as partial (Freeman et al. 2019). Sonic Pi puts live_loop at the centre — code edited while it sounds — an edit-while-playing loop worth borrowing for annotate-compile cycles (Aaron et al. 2016), and Hookpad’s colour-coded Roman numerals and chord hints are a pedagogy embedded in a composition tool (Donahue et al. 2024).

Practice apps and games. The commercial tutor ecosystem — Yousician, Simply Piano, Flowkey, Melodics, Piano Marvel, SmartMusic, Chordify and Moises — rests on real-time pitch and onset detection, gamified progression and, increasingly, source separation and chord recognition that turn any recording into practice material; efficacy studies are few and the entry is partial, but these products set expectations for immediate feedback on playing and singing, and Moises shows stem separation as a route for ingesting example audio (Yousician et al. 2026; Moises Systems 2025). Miller’s ethnography of Guitar Hero and Rock Band argues that such games constitute musical performance and informal learning (Miller 2009), and Harmonix’s DropMix and Fuser made arrangement by stem selection a game mechanic with the engine handling key and tempo matching (Harmonix Music Systems 2020). One caution qualifies the picture: the first empirical studies of generative AI in composition, verified only through Crossref metadata and of mixed venue quality, include an experiment on AI ideation in composition tasks — not in a classroom setting — reporting reduced originality of output (Oros et al. 2026), very possibly the same study as the CMU poster discussed under E1 rather than a second result (Oros et al. 2025) — indicative rather than conclusive, but supporting the view that assistance at ideation is the risky configuration and assistance at realisation the safer one.

11.6 Implications for the studio

The build-versus-adopt line can be drawn sharply. The studio should adopt Verovio and MEI as its score surface and annotation store, MusicXML 4.0 as its interchange boundary, music21 as its analysis backend, symusic and MidiTok as its corpus and tokenisation layer, partitura for score–performance alignment, and spessasynth or FluidSynth for audition and headless rendering (Pugin et al. 2014; W3C Music Notation Community Group 2021; Cuthbert and Ariza 2010; Liao et al. 2024; Fradet et al. 2021; Cancino-Chacón et al. 2022; spessasus 2026). It should not build an engraver, a parser, a tokeniser or a synthesizer. It should build the three things the substrate lacks: a typed, time- and element-anchored annotation layer that survives round trips through MusicXML and MEI; a lossless bridge between an LLM-friendly textual representation — ABC being the practical candidate, with Humdrum’s spines as the model for carrying marks alongside notes — and engraving-grade notation; and the compile orchestration routing each annotation to the right specialised model (Walshaw 2011; Rosen 2026; Huron 1995). The integration target is MuseScore rather than a fork, with a CLAP or VST3 bridge and a desktop shell to escape the Web MIDI gap (Muse Group 2026; u-he et al. 2022; Wilson et al. 2026).

The data decision is a licensing decision and should be made once, at the start: training only on the permissive tier costs scale relative to Aria-MIDI’s hundred thousand hours but buys the right to release weights (Long et al. 2025; Gotham and Jonas 2022; Kong et al. 2022; H. Zhang et al. 2022; Bradshaw and Colton 2025; Malandro 2024). Per-file licence metadata carried through preprocessing lets any released artefact state its provenance, which C2 and C3 show is becoming a regulatory as well as an ethical requirement.

Three substrate gaps are research opportunities rather than engineering chores. No notation format has an adequate annotation model: MEI’s editorial layers come closest and Humdrum spines and RomanText cover parallel data and harmony, while MusicXML and MNX cover neither (Pugin et al. 2014; Gotham et al. 2019; Huron 1995; W3C Music Notation Community Group 2021). Score corpora are large but annotated corpora are small — When in Rome and the DCML sub-corpora are effectively the whole supply of expert harmonic annotation — which caps what an annotate step can learn and argues for treating human corrections as training data from day one (Gotham et al. 2019; Hentschel et al. 2021). And the education literature validates the compile metaphor while compiling loops and samples rather than notation; a notation-native compiler is the missing member of that family (Horn et al. 2022; Freeman et al. 2019; Aaron et al. 2016).

12. Context: products, law, ethics and economics (C)

The environment around a composer-centred studio changed more between 2024 and 2026 than the research did. This section covers the product landscape (C1), bifurcated into audio text-to-song platforms pulled into label-licensed walled gardens and symbolic assistants embedded in existing tools while the notation incumbents ship nothing generative; law and policy as of September 2026 (C2), where German courts have held memorisation in model weights to be an infringing reproduction while United States doctrine still requires human authorship; the provenance and disclosure infrastructure that emerged in response (C3); and the economics and creator attitudes that define the studio’s audience (C4).

12.1 C1 The product landscape 2024–2026

The audio family and its drift toward editing. Suno exemplifies the paradigm and the pressure toward editability within it: around the generator it built Suno Studio, announced 24 September 2025 as a browser “generative audio workstation” with a multitrack timeline, stem separation and per-section regeneration, with Studio 2.0 on 13 August 2026 adding MIDI import, recording and editing plus a chat-bar assistant — the MIDI living inside the workstation, not in the generator’s conditioning path (Suno Inc. 2026). Effective 3 September 2026 as part of the Warner settlement, downloads were capped at seven lifetime on the free tier, twenty per month on Pro and sixty on Premier, with prior models to be retired when licensed ones launch (Suno Inc. 2026; Music Business Worldwide 2025b). Udio traced the same arc earlier, then disabled downloads in October 2025 and announced a 2026 platform in which creations stay inside the walled garden, fingerprinted and filtered (Universal Music Group and Udio 2025; Music Business Worldwide 2025a). Google’s Lyria family offers the most complete audio editing loop among large-vendor products, with the Music AI Sandbox’s Create, Extend and Edit verbs, Lyria RealTime’s prompt-weighted streaming, SynthID watermarking, Lyria 3 in the Gemini app from February 2026 and the acquisition of Producer.ai — formerly Riffusion — announced on 24 February 2026 and rebranded by the trade press as Google Flow Music that April (Google DeepMind 2025b; Lyria Team et al. 2025; Forsgren and Martiros 2022). Eleven Music launched in August 2025 on Merlin and Kobalt licences with claimed commercial clearance (ElevenLabs 2025), Stable Audio’s Open model was trained only on Creative Commons audio (Evans et al. 2025), and Mureka reportedly plans structure before generating, on vendor benchmarks only (Kunlun Tech 2025).

DAW-native features and symbolic assistants. Logic Pro 11 (May 2024) shipped Session Players — Drummer joined by a Bass Player and a Keyboard Player with complexity and intensity controls — which follow the project’s Chord Track and, decisively, produce MIDI regions the user can edit (Apple Inc. 2024). This is the arranger-keyboard idea of F3 reborn inside a DAW; its parameters are a reasonable floor for the studio’s compile controls, its gaps the absence of notation, multimodal annotation and arrangement-level reasoning. Ableton Live 12 took a pointedly different position, using machine learning only for retrieval while its MIDI Generators and Transformations are rule-based and fully editable — a reminder, recorded as partial, that transparency is itself a feature many producers trust (Ableton AG 2024). Moises AI Studio generates context-aware stems fitted to the user’s audio while declining full songs or vocals (Moises Systems 2025), Splice Create assembles licensed human-made samples rather than generating anything (Splice 2024; Splice and LANDR 2025), and WavTool, whose GPT-4 “Conductor” edited projects from natural language, was acquired by Suno in June 2025 and became Suno Studio’s basis — the text-to-song leader buying editability (Suno, Inc. 2025). On the symbolic side Hookpad Aria is the closest shipping analogue of the studio’s loop, serving region-scoped chord, melody or joint infills from the Anticipatory Music Transformer fine-tuned on over 50,000 public TheoryTab analyses, as ordinary editable notation (Donahue et al. 2024; Thickstun et al. 2024); Composer’s Assistant 2 is the open equivalent in REAPER, infilling arbitrary track-measure selections with roughly ten controls (Malandro 2024); MIDI-GPT brings bar-level multi-track infilling with attribute control into Cubase and Ableton under a non-commercial licence (Pasquier et al. 2025); and Scaler 3 with the Orb and Captain Chords plugins shows the demand for lead-sheet-to-arrangement inside DAWs, on rule-based engines with per-block re-rolls and no notation, both partial (Scaler Music and Plugin Boutique 2025; Hexachords 2022). AI singers form a separate, symbolic-first category: Synthesizer V Studio 2, ACE Studio, Vocaloid 6 and Kits.ai take note-level input plus lyrics and produce sung vocals with per-note pitch and timbre editing, Kits.ai selling licensed revenue-shared artist voices, so a compiled score’s vocal line can be rendered without any text-to-song model (Dreamtonics 2025). Neutone Morpho runs neural audio models live as plugins with an SDK for training on one’s own recordings, making “insert example audio” realisable as a timbre annotation on consented material (Neutone Inc. 2024), and AbletonMCP heads a wave of LLM agents driving DAWs from natural language (Ahuja 2025).

The empty quadrant. Against all of this the notation incumbents ship nothing generative: Dorico, Sibelius and MuseScore Studio added proofreading, engraving, scripting and audio work through 2026 with no generative composition, and Finale is discontinued (Steinberg Media Technologies 2025; Avid Technology 2026; Muse Group 2025; MakeMusic, Inc. 2024). As of September 2026 a notation-native AI studio has no direct competitor; the nearest neighbours are a lead-sheet web app, two DAW plugins and one DAW feature set, none operating on engraved notation with multimodal annotations. That prompt-only control frustrates users is independently evidenced by the analysis of Suno and Udio prompts, metatags and lyrics from 2024, which documents the elaborate metatag strategies users invent to steer systems offering no structural handles (Casini et al. 2025).

12.2 C2 Law and policy as of September 2026

The label litigation and its settlements. On 24 June 2024 Universal, Sony and Warner, coordinated by the RIAA, sued Suno in the District of Massachusetts and Uncharted Labs, Udio’s operator, in the Southern District of New York for unlicensed copying of recordings for training, the complaints including side-by-side outputs replicating recordings (UMG Recordings, Inc. et al. 2024). Plaintiffs added DMCA §1201(a) stream-ripping claims in September 2025, and on 26 May 2026 Universal and Sony moved to assert 61,026 recordings after discovery indicated training on millions of theirs (UMG Recordings, Inc. et al. 2024). Universal settled with Udio on 29 October 2025, combining compensation with licences and a 2026 walled-garden platform with fingerprinting and artist opt-in (Music Business Worldwide 2025a); Warner settled with Udio and announced a Stability AI partnership on 19 November 2025, then settled with Suno on 25 November 2025, committing Suno to licensed models in 2026, deprecation of existing ones, artist opt-in and download caps (Music Business Worldwide 2025b). Sony and Universal versus Suno remains live and is the bellwether for fair use in music-AI training; the template is licensing in exchange for containment (Suno Inc. 2026).

The German rulings changed the analysis. On 11 November 2025 the Munich Regional Court I held in GEMA v. OpenAI, over nine German song lyrics, that memorisation of lyrics in model weights is a reproduction under §16 UrhG not covered by the text-and-data-mining exception in §44b UrhG and Article 4 of the DSM Directive, that outputting them infringes again, and that liability attaches to OpenAI rather than the prompting user (Landgericht München I 2025). On 31 July 2026 the same chamber decided GEMA v. Suno — the judgment is July 2026, the oral hearing originally set for 26 January 2026 was postponed over a recusal motion and heard on 9 March 2026, which is why January dates are sometimes reported for this case — over outputs resembling well-known catalogue songs, finding the works effectively stored in Suno’s models so that memorisation amounts to reproduction, that stream-ripping from YouTube circumvented technical protection measures, that §44b does not apply where works are retained in reproducible form, and that Suno rather than prompting users is liable for infringing outputs generated in Germany (Landgericht München I 2026). This first European judgment on the merits against a music generator extends the lyrics logic to music itself, with two consequences for any model distributed in the EU: memorisation audits become mandatory, for which MiRA is the open baseline, and clean training data becomes the only reliable defence (Batlle-Roca et al. 2024).

United States copyrightability points the other way, in the studio’s favour. The Copyright Office’s Copyright and Artificial Intelligence series — Part 1 on digital replicas (2024), Part 2 on copyrightability (29 January 2025) and Part 3 on training in pre-publication form (May 2025), the final Part 3 not confirmed published as of September 2026 and dates drawn from secondary sources — sets out the operative distinction. Part 2 holds that prompts alone confer no authorship, while human-authored expressive inputs, creative selection and arrangement, and modifications of AI output are protectable to the extent of the human contribution; Part 3 treats training as prima facie reproduction, unlikely to be fair use where outputs compete with the training works or the data was obtained unlawfully (U.S. Copyright Office 2025). Thaler v. Perlmutter fixed the floor: the D.C. Circuit affirmed in March 2025 that the Copyright Act requires a human author, and certiorari was denied on 2 March 2026, leaving undecided how much human contribution suffices (U.S. Court of Appeals for the D.C. Circuit 2025). Together these lines yield the conclusion that matters most for design: human-authored, human-edited notation is protectable, prompt-only output generally is not, and a provenance log may generate evidence of authorship as a by-product of ordinary operation (U.S. Copyright Office 2025; U.S. Court of Appeals for the D.C. Circuit 2025; Micchi et al. 2021).

Transparency and likeness regimes. Under Regulation (EU) 2024/1689 obligations for providers of general-purpose AI models have applied since 2 August 2025, with enforcement powers from August 2026: technical documentation, a copyright policy respecting machine-readable opt-outs under Article 4(3) of the DSM Directive, and a published “sufficiently detailed summary” of training content on the Commission’s July 2025 template, from neither of the latter two of which open-source models are exempt (European Parliament and Council 2024). For a studio trained only on public-domain, Creative Commons and consented data that summary is trivial to produce. Tennessee’s ELVIS Act, which took effect in 2024, was the first United States state law extending the right of publicity to voice including simulations, with liability both for unauthorised use and for distributing tools whose primary purpose is producing such replicas (Tennessee General Assembly 2024; Dreamtonics 2025). Concord Music Group v. Anthropic, filed October 2023 over lyrics and recorded as partial for 2026, tests output-side infringement for language models (Concord Music Group, Inc. et al. 2023), and Sturm and colleagues’ 2019 framing of the open questions — authorship, training-data legitimacy, the status of style — anticipated most of this (B. L. T. Sturm et al. 2019).

12.3 C3 Ethics, provenance and disclosure

Consent standards emerged from inside the industry. Ed Newton-Rex resigned as VP Audio at Stability AI in November 2023 over its fair-use position on training and launched the non-profit Fairly Trained in January 2024, certifying models trained only on licensed, public-domain or consented data, early certifications including Beatoven, Soundful, Kits.ai and LANDR (Newton-Rex 2024). The “Statement on AI training” of 22 October 2024 — one sentence declaring unlicensed use of creative works for training an unjust threat to creators’ livelihoods — launched with roughly 10,500 signatories and later exceeded 50,000; both entries are partial (Newton-Rex et al. 2024). Spawning built the opt-out infrastructure — Have I Been Trained? over LAION-5B, the Do Not Train registry and ai.txt — while its founders produced Holly+, a model of Holly Herndon’s own voice offered for others to use, as consent-based training in artistic practice (Herndon et al. 2022; Herndon and Dryhurst 2021).

Disclosure has become platform infrastructure. On 25 September 2025 Spotify announced an impersonation policy permitting removal of unauthorised AI voice clones, a spam filter against mass uploads and sub-30-second bait tracks — 75 million “spammy” tracks removed in the preceding year — and support for the DDEX AI-disclosure standard so distributors can credit AI’s role in vocals, instrumentation or production (Spotify 2025). Deezer’s telemetry charts the scale: fully-AI tracks rose from roughly 10 per cent of daily deliveries in January 2025 to over half at peak in June 2026, around 90,000 per day; they have been labelled to listeners since June 2026 and excluded from recommendation, and while they draw only 1 to 3 per cent of streams, up to 85 per cent of those streams were identified as fraudulent in 2025 and demonetised (Deezer 2026). Watermarking supplies the technical complement — Google applies SynthID to all Lyria outputs (Google DeepMind 2025b), and research on watermarking training data tests whether audio watermarks survive into a generator’s outputs (Epple et al. 2024) — while MiRA audits in the other direction, using audio similarity metrics to detect exact replication above roughly 10 per cent (Batlle-Roca et al. 2024). Why this matters commercially is illustrated by United States v. Michael Smith, who pleaded guilty on 19 March 2026 to wire-fraud conspiracy of $8.09 million after streaming hundreds of thousands of AI-generated tracks through roughly 1,040 bot accounts, detected by the Mechanical Licensing Collective rather than by the platforms (U.S. Attorney’s Office, Southern District of New York 2024).

What not to do, and the position that follows. The musescore.com scrape is the negative exemplar: an unofficial dump of scores, metadata and user data harvested through the site’s public API and distributed over IPFS so it could not be removed, unlicensed, largely user arrangements of copyrighted works and in breach of the platform’s terms — used, without disclosure, by several symbolic-model papers (Xmader 2021); PDMX demonstrates the clean alternative within the same source material (Long et al. 2025). The studio’s ethical position is therefore implementable rather than aspirational: outputs are symbolic and human-authored, so authorship rests with the composer and is protectable (U.S. Copyright Office 2025); computation runs locally where possible, so the composer’s material is not a training contribution by default; training data is restricted to public-domain, Creative Commons and consented sources with per-file licence metadata retained, making a Fairly Trained-style certification and an EU AI Act training summary achievable (Newton-Rex 2024; European Parliament and Council 2024; Long et al. 2025); vocal rendering, if offered, uses consented singing-synthesis voices (Dreamtonics 2025; Tennessee General Assembly 2024); and provenance is emitted by design, as DDEX AI credits and as a compile history recording which material was human-authored and which annotations produced which generated regions (Spotify 2025; Micchi et al. 2021). Residual risks should be stated rather than concealed: stylistic imitation of living composers remains possible, transcribed-from-audio corpora carry greyer provenance than PDMX, and any bundled audio renderer inherits its own training-data questions (Bradshaw and Colton 2025; Evans et al. 2025).

12.4 C4 Economics and creator attitudes

The economic studies commissioned by collecting societies frame the stakes in revenue terms. Goldmedia’s AI and Music study for GEMA and SACEM, published in January 2024 on a survey of roughly 15,000 members, projected the generative music market at about $3 billion by 2028 and put 27 per cent of creators’ revenues at risk by that date, with 71 per cent judging risks to outweigh opportunities and 95 per cent wanting disclosure of training data (Goldmedia GmbH 2024). CISAC’s study with PMP Strategy (December 2024) modelled generative output at roughly €64 billion by 2028 with creators risking a modelled 24 per cent of revenues through substitution and unlicensed training (CISAC and PMP Strategy 2024). Both are partially verified. The attitudinal picture is measured more precisely: APRA AMCOS’s report of 19 August 2024, surveying over 4,200 members, found 54 per cent agreeing that AI can assist the creative process against 82 per cent fearing for their livelihood, with 97 per cent wanting policy action and training-data disclosure, 95 per cent demanding permission before use, 23 per cent of revenue at risk by 2028 and 89 per cent of Aboriginal and Torres Strait Islander creators fearing cultural appropriation (APRA AMCOS and Goldmedia GmbH 2024). Listeners cannot police the boundary themselves: the Deezer and Ipsos survey of 9,000 adults across eight countries found 97 per cent unable to distinguish fully-AI tracks in blind listening, 80 per cent wanting labelling and 65 per cent opposing training on copyrighted material (Deezer and Ipsos 2025) — though detectability is not fixed, since in a blind crossover design over real Suno outputs listeners’ accuracy rose causally when pairs were made more similar (Figueiredo et al. 2025).

What professional composers ask for, when asked directly, reads as a specification of the studio. Newman, Morris and Lee’s ISMIR 2023 study of six professionals across classical, jazz, film, game, interactive-media and electroacoustic practice — 60- to 90-minute semi-structured interviews, two-coder analysis — produced four design implications: preserve creative control by confining AI to ideation rather than intentional decisions; provide interoperability through MIDI, WAV and MusicXML export or integration into software already in use; separate “influences” from “mechanisms”; and prefer open-source, explainable models over black boxes (Newman et al. 2023). The same preferences recur in the folk-rnn practitioner work and Sony CSL’s eighteen-month embedded study, which add that tool-switching kills adoption and that documented human selection and modification is what practitioners regard as constituting ownership (B. L. Sturm et al. 2019; Deruty et al. 2022; Micchi et al. 2021). The studio’s positioning is thus not a moral posture adopted at cost but the configuration these studies describe as acceptable.

12.5 Implications for the studio

The competitive conclusion is that the empty quadrant is real and defensible. Every 2026 product either generates opaque audio from a prompt and is now enclosed by licensing settlements, or generates editable symbolic material inside a DAW at clip or bar granularity, or engraves notation with no generative capability at all (Suno Inc. 2026; Universal Music Group and Udio 2025; Apple Inc. 2024; Malandro 2024; Steinberg Media Technologies 2025; Avid Technology 2026; Muse Group 2025); nothing compiles engraved notation from human-authored material plus multimodal annotations. Hookpad Aria validates the interaction primitive — select a region, ask, accept or edit — on a small, consented, functional-harmony dataset, showing it can be productised without a foundation model or a legally encumbered corpus (Donahue et al. 2024; Thickstun et al. 2024), and Suno Studio and WavTool supply the reverse confirmation: the leaders of the rejected paradigm spent two years acquiring editability, arriving at MIDI editing inside a workstation whose generator still cannot read it (Suno Inc. 2026; Suno, Inc. 2025).

The legal conclusion is that the studio’s architecture is also its legal strategy. Because outputs are notation the composer wrote, selected, arranged and edited, they fall on the protectable side of the Copyright Office’s Part 2 analysis and the Thaler rule, whereas prompt-only outputs generally do not (U.S. Copyright Office 2025; U.S. Court of Appeals for the D.C. Circuit 2025); and because training is confined to public-domain, Creative Commons and consented corpora, the memorisation-as-reproduction holding of the Munich rulings is not merely survivable but inapplicable, while the EU AI Act’s training-content summary becomes a differentiator rather than a burden (Landgericht München I 2025, 2026; European Parliament and Council 2024; Long et al. 2025). Three features follow and should be first-class: a provenance log recording authorship and edit history at region granularity, serving at once as authorship record, DDEX AI-credit source and debugging aid; a memorisation audit in the evaluation harness for any released model; and per-file licence metadata carried through the data pipeline (Micchi et al. 2021; Spotify 2025; Batlle-Roca et al. 2024).

Two gaps remain. No product in this landscape emits provenance a composer could use to demonstrate authorship, and no legal or platform regime specifies what such a record should contain, so a well-designed compile history is a contribution to practice as much as to software. And the economic literature measures what creators fear and want in aggregate while offering no longitudinal evidence on whether an assistive, symbolic, provenance-emitting tool changes professional output or income (APRA AMCOS and Goldmedia GmbH 2024; Goldmedia GmbH 2024; CISAC and PMP Strategy 2024) — evidence the studio is positioned to generate, which is the argument in E2 for building telemetry and longitudinal study design in from the first release (Newman et al. 2023; Deruty et al. 2022).

13. Gap analysis and a research agenda

The survey’s most useful output is not the inventory but the shape of what is missing. The gaps below were derived by intersecting two things: the density of the taxonomy’s nodes — where the corpus is thin — with the studio’s own design requirements, which are stated as positions on the ten coding dimensions. A node that is crowded but irrelevant (text-to-song, 23 primary entries) is not a gap; a node that is thin and load-bearing is. Each gap names the nearest prior art to build on, so that none of them starts from nothing.

13.1 Gaps that are candidate contributions

G1. Annotations as a compilable instruction set on notation. (Node S2.4: four primary entries. Dimensions D4, D6, D7.) This is the central gap and the one the studio is best positioned to close. The constituent parts are all present in the corpus and never assembled: typed, musical-time-anchored annotation records in MEI, Dezrann and RomanText (Pugin et al. 2014; Giraud et al. 2018; Gotham et al. 2019); free-form ink, images and audio placed on and between staves without an engraver enforcing syntax, as in EuterPen (Cavez et al. 2025); a reference grammar that decomposes a mixed natural-language-and-sketch request into temporal, spatial, operation and parameter components with per-modality resolvers and inspectable suggestions, as in ExpressEdit (Tilekbay et al. 2024); selection-then-instruction as an interaction, as in DirectGPT (Masson et al. 2024); a converged control vocabulary that supplies the annotation types (Rütte et al. 2023; Guo et al. 2022; Chen et al. 2024); and a demonstration that a music agent can act on musician-readable structural feedback in a text grammar, as in Libretto (Xu 2026). The research questions are which annotation types composers actually reach for, how ambiguous marks should be resolved and disambiguated, and how conflicts between annotations are reported rather than silently resolved.

G2. A lossless bridge between an LLM-friendly textual IR and engraving-grade notation. (Nodes S3.1, B1. Dimension D7.) No verified system in the corpus generates MusicXML or MEI natively. Generation lives in MIDI event tokens, ABC text or piano-roll tensors, and MetaScore converts scores out of MuseScore format into REMI+ rather than in (Xu et al. 2025). ABC is the pragmatic candidate because the strongest symbolic music LLMs already speak it, including MuPT’s synchronized multi-track SMT-ABC and NotaGen (Qu et al. 2024; Y. Wang et al. 2025), but ABC cannot express what an engraver needs. Verovio’s MEI-native pipeline is the natural target on the notation side (Pugin et al. 2014). Building and evaluating a round-trip that loses nothing a composer cares about is both a prerequisite for the studio and a publishable contribution.

G3. Notation-editor-native generative assistance. (Node S3.2 crossed with dimension D8.) The DAW side is well served — Composer’s Assistant in REAPER, MIDI-GPT in Cubase and Ableton, Magenta Studio, MMM4Live (Malandro 2024; Pasquier et al. 2025; Roberts et al. 2019; Metacreation Lab (Pasquier, Philippe and Ens, Jeff and Tchemeube, Renaud Bougueng) 2023). The notation side has DeepBach’s 2017 MuseScore plugin and Hookpad Aria’s lead-sheet copilot and essentially nothing else (Hadjeres et al. 2017; Donahue et al. 2024). Dorico, Sibelius and MuseScore Studio ship no generative features as of 2026 (Steinberg Media Technologies 2025; Avid Technology 2026; Muse Group 2025). Practitioners consistently say tool-switching kills adoption (Deruty et al. 2022; Newman et al. 2023), and MuseScore is GPL-3 with a DAW-converging roadmap. A notation-native studio has no direct competitor.

G4. Multimodal editing on a score. (Nodes S4.1, S1.3. Dimension D4.) Sketch-conditioned music generation exists in isolation — contour and density curves in Draw and Listen!, independent pitch and rhythm sketches in Music SketchNet, drawn curves with visual alignment explanations in Drawlody (Benetatos and Duan 2022; Chen et al. 2020; Liang and Wang 2024) — and natural-language plus sketch editing exists for video (Tilekbay et al. 2024). Nothing combines language, sketch, hum and attached example on notation. Drawlody’s usability scores against chord-grid and DAW baselines suggest the drawn contour is worth treating as a first-class annotation type.

G5. A return channel: the compiler’s build log. (Node S4.4: two primary entries. Dimension D6.) COFI’s coding of 92 co-creative systems found the AI-to-human direction almost entirely absent (Rezwana and Maher 2023). The exceptions are instructive and few: RefinPaint’s critic marks where a score should be modified before proposing how (Ramoneda et al. 2024), Cococo offered explanation and debugging affordances (Louie et al. 2020), ReaLJam renders committed chords opaque and tentative ones translucent so the human can see the agent’s plan before it commits (Scarlatos et al. 2025), and ExpressEdit’s examine panel shows how each reference was interpreted (Tilekbay et al. 2024). The compiler metaphor supplies the missing design directly: a build log that states which annotation produced which material, which constraints bound, which were relaxed and why, and where the model was least confident. Work on explaining latent spaces for the arts is the nearest theoretical anchor (Bryan-Kinns et al. 2021).

G6. Versioning with musical semantics. (Node S4.5: five primary entries.) Score diffing exists as tree edit distance rendered side by side in Verovio (Foscarin et al. 2019), Flat.io keeps per-edit history with contributor colouring (Tutteo Ltd (Flat.io) 2026), and mei-friend commits MEI to GitHub (Goebl and Weigl 2023). Branch and merge with musical semantics does not exist, and variation trees over compiled outputs — the pattern Luminate demonstrates for LLM design-space exploration (Suh et al. 2024), with antecedents in Side Views and Chronicle (Terry and Mynatt 2002; Grossman et al. 2010) — are unexplored for music. There is a second motive beyond convenience: a provenance log of human authorship and modification is exactly the record that the authorship analysis in §12.2 makes valuable, a point Micchi and colleagues raised from practice (Micchi et al. 2021).

G7. Symbolically-conditioned, composer-controllable audio rendering. (Node S3.8. Dimension D1.) Rendering a compiled score to audio under human control is thin and research-grade. JASCO accepts chords, melody salience and drums but ships non-commercial weights; Coco-Mulla, Music ControlNet and MuseControlLite accept chords, MIDI or time-varying curves; MIDI-DDSP renders full MIDI expressively but monophonically; Magenta RealTime 2 accepts live MIDI notes at roughly 200 ms control latency on a laptop (Tal et al. 2024; Lin et al. 2023; S.-L. Wu et al. 2024; Tsai et al. 2025; Wu et al. 2022; Lyria Team et al. 2025). No commercial full-song system accepts MIDI or chords as a generation condition. Inference-time optimization is the most promising general mechanism, since it turns any differentiable musical feature into a constraint on a frozen model without retraining (Novack et al. 2024).

G8. Interactive correction for loose input. (Node S1.2.) On a clean humming corpus with corrected onset labels, off-the-shelf transcribers reach 0.43–0.60 note-plus-onset F1 and a purpose-built CNN-plus-dynamic-programming model roughly 0.67 (S. Liu, Li, et al. 2024; Gupta et al. 2024). Humming will therefore always need disambiguation — key and scale locking, quantisation grids, smoothing, click-to-fix — rather than a claim of correctness (Doremir Music Research AB and Klangio GmbH 2014; Vochlea Music 2021). The transferable principle comes from an unexpected place: one targeted human annotation, clicking repeat signs, lifted audio-to-score alignment from 33% to 82% (Bukey et al. 2024). Cheap, well-placed human correction beats full automation, and designing where to spend the composer’s attention is a research question in its own right.

G9. Editable structural skeletons. (Node S3.4.) Dai and Dannenberg’s analyses established that deep generative outputs differ strikingly from human popular music in repetition and hierarchical structure (Dai, Yu, et al. 2022; Dai et al. 2024). The answers already point at the annotation layer: MusicFrameworks makes section and phrase structure, chords and a basic melody explicit user-editable inputs, and the whole-song cascaded diffusion model exposes four editable levels from form down to accompaniment (Dai et al. 2021; Z. Wang et al. 2024). A studio whose compile step reads a human-authored form annotation inherits both the structural fix and the control surface.

G10. Evaluating the loop rather than the artifact. (Nodes E1, E4.) Distributional audio metrics correlate weakly with human preference — Fréchet Audio Distance has been argued to be superseded, and benchmarks against 15,000 human pairwise preferences show weak metric-human agreement (Chung et al. 2025; Grötschla et al. 2025; Y. Huang et al. 2025). The instruments that matter for a co-creative tool exist — the Creativity Support Index, ownership and control items, paired listener comparisons, and Karimi and colleagues’ warning that co-creative systems over-measure user experience and under-measure the creativity of the collaboration (Cherry and Latulipe 2014; Karimi et al. 2018) — but longitudinal, in-practice studies remain rare (Krol et al. 2025; Deruty et al. 2022). Suggestion-level acceptance telemetry of the kind Hookpad Aria reports is the most under-used measure available (Donahue et al. 2024).

G11. Orchestration without over-trusting the orchestrator. (Node E6.) Specialised symbolic music LLMs outperform general models on composition tasks (Y. Wang et al. 2025; Qu et al. 2024), but every benchmark that probes musical reasoning reports weakness: ZIQI-Eval found all sixteen models tested poor, MuChoMusic showed audio-language models over-relying on text, and probing work questions whether generation models encode theory concepts at all (J. Li et al. 2024; Weck et al. 2024; Wei et al. 2024). The architecture this implies is an LLM as orchestrator around specialised symbolic models and deterministic analysers — with the analysers, not the language model, holding authority over theory.

G12. Licence-clean symbolic training data at scale. (Node B4. Dimension D10.) The clean sets are real but small relative to what the field trains on: PDMX at more than 250,000 public-domain MusicXML scores, OpenScore’s CC0 editions, GiantMIDI-Piano, ATEPP and the Bach Doodle corpus (Long et al. 2025; Gotham and Jonas 2022; Kong et al. 2022; H. Zhang et al. 2022; Huang, Hawthorne, et al. 2019). The large and convenient sets are not: Aria-MIDI’s 1.19 million files are CC-BY-NC-SA, Hooktheory’s data is non-commercial, and scraped MuseScore dumps are legally untenable (Bradshaw and Colton 2025; Xmader 2021). Composer’s Assistant’s decision to train only on permissively licensed MIDI shows the constraint is workable (Malandro 2024). Assembling and documenting a shippable symbolic training corpus, with an EU AI Act-style training-content summary, is unglamorous infrastructure work with outsized value to an open project.

13.2 A sequenced agenda

The gaps sort naturally into three horizons.

The near horizon is engineering that unblocks everything else and is publishable as systems work: the notation round-trip of G2, an annotation data model that reuses MEI or Dezrann’s record shape rather than inventing one (G1’s substrate), integration with an existing notation engine rather than a new renderer (G3), and the licence-clean corpus of G12. None of this requires new modelling. Existing infilling models can be wrapped immediately — the Anticipatory Music Transformer’s anticipation tokens map directly onto “everything the composer wrote is a hard constraint,” and it runs in a browser (Thickstun et al. 2024; Zhou et al. 2024).

The middle horizon is the studio’s actual research contribution: the annotation compiler of G1, evaluated against the instruments of G10, with the build log of G5 as its distinguishing feature. This is where a defensible claim lies, because it is simultaneously an interaction contribution, a systems contribution and an evaluation contribution, and because the corpus contains no competitor. The multimodal editing surface of G4 and the structural skeleton of G9 are natural components of the same system rather than separate projects.

The far horizon is rendering and performance: symbolically-conditioned audio under composer control (G7), and the real-time branch, where the RL-tuned accompaniment line from ReaLchords through ReaLJam to post-training fixes for reward hacking has produced open code and demonstrated interaction patterns worth building on (Yusong Wu et al. 2024; Scarlatos et al. 2025; Y. Wu, Brade, et al. 2026).

13.3 Concluding argument

Three claims stand up across the whole corpus, and together they make the case for the studio.

The first is that the composer-centered loop is not a contrarian preference but the direction every serious line of evidence points. Coconet described nonlinear scribbling and revision as the thing to model in 2017 (Huang et al. 2017). The controlled studies found that steering interfaces move composer control, ownership and self-efficacy even when the model is held fixed, and that interface and model gains are complementary (Louie et al. 2020, 2022). The field studies found practitioners decomposing songs into parts, generating into those parts and curating, and asking for systems that are decomposable, steerable and interpretable (Huang et al. 2020; Morris et al. 2024). The one deployment with public telemetry shows musicians accepting span-level suggestions inside a notation editor at scale (Donahue et al. 2024). And the commercial systems built on the opposite premise keep adding editing affordances after the fact (Suno Inc. 2026; Google DeepMind 2025b).

The second is that the technical substrate is unusually ready. Infilling under hard constraints is mature and, in several cases, permissively licensed and small enough to run locally (Thickstun et al. 2024; Pasquier et al. 2025; Malandro 2024). Notation rendering, symbolic parsing and analysis are solved well enough to adopt rather than rebuild (Pugin et al. 2014; Cuthbert and Ariza 2010; Liao et al. 2024; Fradet et al. 2021). Hierarchical pipelines with editable intermediate levels already exist as architectural templates (Z. Wang et al. 2024; Dai et al. 2021). Decompiling symbolic music into an editable program has been demonstrated with execution-based rewards, which makes “music as code” an empirical result rather than a metaphor (Kim, Gandhi, et al. 2026).

The third is that the gap between what exists and what a composer needs is concentrated almost entirely in the annotation and feedback layer — the two thinnest load-bearing nodes in the taxonomy, S2.4 and S4.4. That is a fortunate place for a gap to be. It is an interaction-design and systems problem more than a frontier-modelling problem, which means a small open-source project with a clear thesis can make real progress on it without competing for compute. It is also the layer that determines whether the human stays at the center, since annotations are how intent enters and the build log is how the machine accounts for what it did.

The studio’s premise, restated in the vocabulary this review has assembled: a score-driven, transformative system in the instrument paradigm (Rowe 1993), with alternating turn-taking (Kantosalo and Toivonen 2016), bidirectional communication (Rezwana and Maher 2023), multi-level editable intermediate representations, and the AI in the role of colleague at compile time and coach when it annotates back (Lubart 2005) — never author.
